Existence of vanishing physical viscosity solutions of characteristic initial-boundary value problems for systems of conservation laws
Fabio Ancona, Andrea Marson, Laura V. Spinolo

Abstract




Abstract.

We consider initial boundary-value problems for nonlinear systems of conservation laws in one space variable. It is known that in general different viscous mechanisms yield different solutions in the zero-viscosity limit. Here we focus on the most technically demanding case, known as boundary characteristic case, which occurs when one of the characteristic velocities of the system vanishes. We work in small total variation regimes and assume that every characteristic field is either genuinely nonlinear or linearly degenerate. We
establish existence of
admissible solutions satisfying a boundary condition consistent with the vanishing viscosity approximation
given by a large class of physical (that is, mixed hyperbolic-parabolic) systems. In particular, our results apply to the zero-viscosity limit of the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equations, written in both Eulerian and Lagrangian coordinates. Our analysis relies on a fine boundary layers analysis and is based on the introduction of a new wave front-tracking algorithm. From the technical viewpoint, the most innovative elements are i) a new class of interaction estimates for boundary layers and boundary characteristic wave fronts hitting the boundary, which yields the introduction of a new Glimm-type functional; ii) a detailed analysis of the behavior of the wave front-tracking algorithm close to the boundary, which in turn yields relevant information on the limit.

Keywords:
systems of conservation laws, initial-boundary value problems, hyperbolic systems, wave front-tracking, boundary characteristic case, mixed hyperbolic-parabolic systems, boundary layers

MSC (2010): 35L65, 35B30

1.
Introduction

We consider initial-boundary value problems for the nonlinear system of conservation laws

(1.1)

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{v})_{t}+\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v})_{x}=0,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},

where the unknown
\mathbf{v}
attains values in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
\mathbf{g},\mathbf{f}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
are smooth functions satisfying suitable assumptions that we discuss in the following. Note that (
1.1
) can be
formally
obtained as the
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
limit of the viscous approximation

(1.2)

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{v}^{\varepsilon})_{t}+\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}^{\varepsilon})_{x}=\varepsilon\Big(\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{v}^{\varepsilon})\mathbf{v}^{\varepsilon}_{x}\Big)_{x},\quad\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{N},

where the function
\mathbf{D}
attains values in the space of
N\times N
matrices and is positive semi-definite. We postpone the discussion on the precise assumptions satisfied by
\mathbf{D}
, here we just mention that we impose conditions, introduced in the fundamental paper by Kawashima and Shizuta
[
27
]
, that are satisfied by several relevant physical systems like the Navier-Stokes equations and the viscous magneto-hydrodynamics (MHD) equations. In particular, we tackle the case of a singular (that is, non-invertible) matrix
\mathbf{D}
, which is the most relevant from the physical viewpoint but involves severe technical challenges. In particular, owing to the singularity of
\mathbf{D}
, the initial-boundary value problem for (
1.2
) is in general overdetermined if one imposes a
full
boundary condition like
\mathbf{v}^{\varepsilon}(t,0)=\mathbf{v}_{b}(t)
. In the present work we regard the initial-boundary value problem for (
1.1
) as limit of (
1.2
) coupled with the initial and boundary conditions

(1.3)

\mathbf{v}^{\varepsilon}(0,\cdot)=\mathbf{v}_{0},\qquad\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\beta}}(\mathbf{v}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,0),\mathbf{v}_{b})=\mathbf{0}_{N},

where
\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}_{b}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
are functions with finite total variation and the function
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\beta}}
is defined in §
2.2
.
Very loosely speaking, the basic idea underpinning the construction of
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\beta}}
is that we impose a full boundary condition on the
parabolic
component of
\mathbf{v}^{\varepsilon}
, whereas on the
hyperbolic
component we impose boundary conditions along the characteristic fields entering the domain. We refer to §
2.2
and Remark
2.1
in §
2
for a more detailed discussion.

The analysis of the inviscid limit of (
1.2
) is particularly relevant for the analysis of initial-boundary value problems for (
1.1
) since it is known that (even in the most elementary linear case) the limit in general depends on
\mathbf{D}
, see
[
19
]
. This marks a fundamental difference with the case of the Cauchy problem, where in the conservative case the limit does not depend on the viscosity matrix, see
[
6
]
. Loosely speaking this difference is due to the fact that in the initial-boundary value problem the transient behavior in proximity of the domain boundary is described by the so-called
boundary layers
, which are steady solutions of (
1.2
) and as such strongly affected by
\mathbf{D}
. Remarkably, the fact
that solutions of (
1.1
) depend on the underlying viscous mechanism has also relevant consequences from the numerical viewpoint, see
[
29
]
. Note that establishing the convergence of (
1.2
) to (
1.1
) is presently an open problem, even in the case of the Cauchy problem. In the case of initial-boundary value problems, partial results under restrictive assumptions are established for instance in
[
3
,
13
,
19
,
22
,
25
,
30
,
35
]
. See also
[
32
,
33
]
for a general introduction to hyperbolic initial-boundary value problems.

To the best of our knowledge, the present paper provides the first global in time existence result for solutions of initial-boundary value problems for (
1.1
) satisfying a
boundary condition that is consistent with the viscous mechanism (
1.2
)
in the boundary characteristic case, see Remark
1.1
below. The boundary characteristic case occurs when one of the eigenvalues of the jacobian matrix of
\mathbf{f}
vanishes and it is known to be particularly challenging since the waves of the characteristic family can have a fairly complex behavior and can alternatively enter the domain, leave it, or even be tangential to the boundary.

We now provide the definition of the boundary condition we impose on (
1.1
) and point out that in the following we require that every eigenvector field of the jacobian matrix of the flux
\mathbf{f}
of (
1.1
) is either genuinely nonlinear or linearly degenerate.

Definition 1.1
.

Given system (
1.2
) and
\mathbf{\bar{v}}
,
\mathbf{v}_{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we say that “
\mathbf{\bar{v}}\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{v}_{b}
” if there is

\mathbf{\underline{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
such that the following conditions are both satisfied:

i)

\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{\bar{v}})=\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{\underline{v}})
and the
0
-speed discontinuity between
\mathbf{\bar{v}}
(on the right) and
\mathbf{\underline{v}}
(on the left)
satisfies the Lax admissibility condition;

ii)

there is a so-called “boundary layer”
\mathbf{w}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
such that

(1.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{w})-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{\underline{v}})\\
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\beta}}(\mathbf{w}(0),\mathbf{v}_{b})=\mathbf{0},\quad\lim_{y\to+\infty}\mathbf{w}(y)=\mathbf{\underline{v}}.\end{array}\right.

Some remarks are here in order. First, the heuristic meaning of the above definition is that
\mathbf{\bar{v}}\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{v}_{b}
if
\mathbf{v}_{b}
is connected to
\mathbf{\bar{v}}
by (either or both) a boundary layer and an admissible
0
-speed discontinuity. Second, we refer to
[
16
,
28
]
for the definition of Lax admissibility condition. Third, assume in condition i) that
\mathbf{\bar{v}}
is close to
\mathbf{\underline{v}}
and that the boundary is not characteristic, that is all the eigenvalues of the jacobian matrix of
\mathbf{f}
are bounded away from
0
; then owing to the Local Invertibility Theorem the condition
\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{\bar{v}})=\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{\underline{v}})
yields
\mathbf{\bar{v}}=\mathbf{\underline{v}}
and the Lax admissibility requirement is trivially satisfied. Fourth,
contact discontinuities are always Lax admissible and hence the Lax admissibility requirement in condition i) is redundant if the boundary is characteristic and the boundary characteristic field (that is, the field associate to the eigenvalue that vanishes) is linearly degenerate. Summing up, in the small total variation framework we consider in the following the Lax admissibility requirement in condition i) is only relevant in the boundary characteristic case when the boundary characteristic field is genuinely nonlinear. Finally, we remark in passing that there is a huge literature concerning the analysis of boundary layers for nonlinear systems of conservation laws. Here we only refer to the seminal papers
[
30
,
34
,
36
]
and to the references therein for a more extended discussion.

We can now pose the initial-boundary problem for (
1.1
) by assigning the initial and boundary conditions

(1.5)

\mathbf{v}(0,\cdot)=\mathbf{v}_{0},\qquad\mathbf{v}(\cdot,0)\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{v}_{b}

Note that another famous way of assigning the boundary condition on (
1.1
) is discussed in
[
18
]
and used in
[
1
]
to formulate the initial-boundary value problem and establish global-in-time existence results in the boundary characteristic case. Note however that the boundary condition given in (
1.5
) differs from the one in
[
18
]
, even in the simple case of a linear systems with an invertible viscosity matrix
\mathbf{D}
, provided
\mathbf{D}
does not coincide with the identity (see also
[
19
]
and Remark
1.1
below for a more detailed discussion about this point). This implies in particular that the in the boundary characteristic case the solution given by Theorem
1.1
below is in general different from the one in
[
1
]
. In all the cases where the convergence of (
1.1
) to (
1.2
) has been effectively established (see for instance
[
3
,
30
]
) the limit satisfies (
1.5
).

We can now state our main result. The precise formulation of the hypotheses requires some heavy notation and is therefore postponed to §
2
. We now provide instead an heuristic discussion: Hypothesis
1
states that there is a change of variables
\mathbf{v}\leftrightarrow\mathbf{u}
such that, up to the rescale
(t,x)\leftrightarrow(t/\varepsilon,x/\varepsilon)
, (
1.2
) can be rewritten as

(1.6)

\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{u}_{t}+\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{u}_{x}=\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{u}_{xx}+\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}_{x})\mathbf{u}_{x},

with (
1.6
)
in the normal form
in the Kawashima-Shizuta sense
[
27
]
. Hypothesis
2
is the so-called Kawashima-Shizuta condition, which very loosely speaking is a coupling condition between the hyperbolic and the parabolic component of (
1.6
). Hypothesis
3
is a standard strict hyperbolicity assumption, whereas Hypothesis
4
states that every eigenvector field of (
1.6
) is either genuinely nonlinear or linearly degenerate. Finally, Hypothesis
5
collects the conditions introduced in
[
8
,
9
]
to enusure that we can write the boundary layers equation for (
1.6
) in an explicit form (see the analysis in §
3
). In the statement of the following theorem,
BV
denotes the space of bounded total variation functions, see
[
2
]
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume Hypotheses
1,\cdots,5
in §
2
and fix
\mathbf{v}^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
; then there is a constant
\delta^{\ast}>0
only depending on the functions
\mathbf{g}
,
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{D}
in system (
1.2
), on the change of variables
\mathbf{v}\leftrightarrow\mathbf{u}
and on
\mathbf{v}^{\ast}
, such that the following holds. If
\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}_{b}\in BV(\mathbb{R}_{+})
satisfy

(1.7)

\mathrm{TotVar}\ \mathbf{v}_{0}+\mathrm{TotVar}\ \mathbf{v}_{b}+|\mathbf{v}_{0}(0^{+})-\mathbf{v}_{b}(0^{+})|\leq\delta^{\ast},\quad|\mathbf{v}_{0}(0^{+})-\mathbf{v}^{\ast}|\leq\delta^{\ast}

there is a global-in-time Lax admissible distributional solution
\mathbf{v}\in BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
of (
1.1
) satisfying the initial and boundary
1
1

1

Note that, since
v\in BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
, the trace
\mathbf{v}(\cdot,0)
is a well-defined, locally summable function, see
[
2
]
.
conditions (
1.5
). Also, if system (
1.1
) admits a convex entropy then the solution we construct is entropy admissible.

In (
1.7
) we denote by
\mathbf{v}_{0}(0^{+})
and
\mathbf{v}_{b}(0^{+})
the right limits at
x=0
and
t=0
of the functions
\mathbf{v}_{0}
and
\mathbf{v}_{b}
, respectively; these limits exist because the functions have bounded total variation. We now comment on Theorem
1.1
.

•

The small total variation assumption is fairly standard for the analysis of one-dimensional system of conservation laws, see
[
10
,
16
]
. Note that the counter-examples in
[
11
,
12
,
24
]
imply that this assumption is necessary to obtain global-in-time existence results that apply to general systems of conservation laws. The examples in
[
11
,
12
,
24
]
are technically speaking Cauchy problems, but can be easily adapted to the initial-boundary value problem owing to finite propagation speed.

•

Theorem
1.1
is an
existence
result. We are confident that one could establish stability and
uniqueness
results by combining the analysis in the present paper with the Standard Riemann Semigroup approach
á la
Bressan, see
[
10
]
. However, since the proof of Theorem
1.1
is already long and technical this is left for future work.

•

We refer to §
1.2
below for a detailed outline of the proof of Theorem
1.1
, here we point out that it relies on the introduction of a new wave front-tracking algorithm, which involves both wave fronts and boundary layers. As the limit of a wave front-tracking algorithm, the solution constructed in the proof of Theorem
1.1
enjoys several further regularity properties, see for instance
[
10
, Theorem 10.4]
. In particular, it satisfies the Lax admissibility condition along shock curves.

•

The main novelty of Theorem
1.1
is that we deal with the boundary characteristic case, that is we take into account the possibility that one eigenvalue of the matrix
\mathbf{E}^{-1}\mathbf{A}
in (
1.6
) attains the value
0
. In the non characteristic boundary case (i.e., when all the eigenvalues are bounded away from
0
), Goodman and Sablé-Tougeron
[
21
,
31
]
established global-in-time existence results by relying on a modified Glimm scheme
[
20
]
, whereas Amadori
[
1
]
used a wave front-tracking algorithm. The analysis in
[
1
]
covers the boundary characteristic case, but as mentioned before the boundary condition in
[
1
]
is different from (
1.5
), see Remark
1.1
below for a more detailed discussion. The fact that we use (
1.5
) accounts for severe technical challenges that we touch upon in Remark
1.1
. As mentioned before, the treatment of the boundary characteristic case is much more complicated than the non-characteristic, for reasons that we now try to explain. At the heuristic level, in the boundary characteristic case there is a deep interplay between the behavior of the solution
inside
the domain and the dynamics of boundary layers, which describe the transient behavior from (
1.2
) to (
1.1
) at the boundary. At a more technical level, one has to take into account the fact that boundary layers may have a component lying on a center manifold, which makes the analysis of their dynamics more delicate. To conclude, we point out that the analysis in
[
3
]
gives among other things a proof of Theorem
1.1
in the case where the matrix
\mathbf{D}
in (
1.2
) is the identity. Note however that in most of the physically relevant cases
\mathbf{D}
is not even invertible.

•

Fix
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
; by applying the change of variables
x\leftrightarrow x-\mu t
the analysis in the present paper immediately extends to the case where the boundary condition is posed at the boundary
x=\mu t
rather than at
x=0
. We are confident that our analysis could also be extended to the case of a Lipschitz continuous moving boundary
x=\mu(t)
, but to ease the exposition we do not deal with this case.

•

As it is fairly standard (see
[
10
,
16
,
32
,
33
]
) in the study of one-dimensional system of conservation laws, in the statement of Theorem
1.1
we assume that every vector field is either genuinely nonlinear or linearly degenerate. This assumption is satisfied in most of the physicallly relevant examples, see §
2.3
, and eases the analysis as it simplifies the structure of the solution of the Riemann problem and of the so-called boundary Riemann problem. In the case of the Cauchy problem, the analysis in
[
4
]
defines a wave front-tracking algorithm for general strictly hyperbolic system, with no genuine nonlinearity or linear degeneracy assumption. The extension of the analysis in the present work to the case of general systems is in principle possible, but it is likely to require heavy technicalities.

Remark 1.1
.

We now compare our analysis with the one in the paper
[
1
]
by Amadori: the main differences stem from the different way of assigning the boundary condition in the boundary characteristic case. We use Definition
1.1
and (
1.5
), whereas Amadori follows
[
18
]
and introduces condition
(C)
in
[
1
, p.2]
. To highlight the main differences between (
1.5
) and condition
(C)
let us focus on the simplest case and assume that
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{g}
and
\mathbf{D}
are such that one can assign a full boundary condition on (
1.2
) and (
1.4
), that is
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\beta}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}_{b})=\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}_{b}
. Condition

(C)
requires that for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
the trace
\mathbf{v}(t,0)
and the value
\mathbf{v}_{b}(t)
are connected by waves (shocks, rarefactions or contact discontinuities) with nonpositive speed. Instead, in (
1.5
) we require that
\mathbf{v}(t,0)
and
\mathbf{v}_{b}(t)
are connected by a
0
-speed shock or contact discontinuity and by a
boundary layer
described by system (
1.4
).
To see that these two conditions do not coincide, we consider the linear case where up to a rescaling of the independent variables (
1.2
) boils down to

\mathbf{v}_{t}+\mathbf{F}\mathbf{v}_{x}=\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}_{xx},\qquad\mathbf{F},\mathbf{D}\in\mathbb{M}^{N\times N}

and assume for simplicity that
\mathbf{D}
is invertible and that
\mathbf{F}
and
\mathbf{D}
are symmetric. Given
\mathbf{v}_{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, condition
(C)
dictates that
\mathbf{v}(t,0)-\mathbf{v}_{b}
lies in the eigenspace of
\mathbf{F}
associated to nonpositive eigenvalues, whereas condition (
1.5
) is satisfied if and only if
\mathbf{v}(t,0)-\mathbf{v}_{b}
lies in the eigenspace of
\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{F}
associated to nonpositive eigenvalues. Since in general

\mathbf{F}
and
\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{F}
have different eigenspaces, these two boundary conditions are different. Note in particular that condition (
1.5
)
depends
on the viscosity matrix
\mathbf{D}
, whereas condition
(C)
is unaffected by
\mathbf{D}
.

Wrapping up, the main difference between condition
(C)
and Definition
1.1
is that condition
(C)
does not provide any information of the underlying viscous mechanism, whereas Definition
1.1
takes into account the relevant information concerning the transient behavior from the viscous approximation to the hyperbolic limit.
This implies that, contrary to
[
1
]
, our analysis of the behavior of the approximate solutions near the domain boundary has to handle the interaction between an
hyperbolic
component given by rarefaction, shock and contact discontinuity
waves and a
parabolic
(or, more precisely, mixed hyperbolic-parabolic) component given by the boundary layers. In particular,
we use a different solution of the so-called boundary Riemann problem, see §
4
, which makes it much harder to obtain useful information (the so-called interaction estimates) on the behavior of the approximate solution near the domain boundary,
see for instance the fairly involved proof of Lemma
6.2
.

Remark 1.2
.

The non characteristic boundary case occurs when all the eigenvalues of the jacobian matrix
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{f}
are bounded away from
0
. In this case the analysis in
[
1
]
provides a proof of Theorem
1.1
; indeed, in the non characteristic case the boundary datum is assigned by the condition
(NC)
in
[
1
, p.2]
, which is consistent with (
1.5
), namely boils down to (
1.5
) through a suitable choice of the function
b
.

1.1.
Paper outline

For the reader’s convenience we provide a roadmap of the proof of Theorem
1.1
in §
1.2
. Also, in §
1.3
we collect the main notation used in the present paper. In §
2
we precisely state the hypotheses of Theorem
1.1
and check that they are satisfied by the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equations, written in both Eulerian and Lagrangian coordinates. In §
4
we recall the solution of the Riemann and of the so-called boundary Riemann problem, which constitute the building block for the definition of the wave front-tracking algorithm. In §
5
we define our wave front-tracking algorithm. Section
6
contains some of the most technically demanding and innovative results of the paper in the form of so-called interaction estimates. In §
7
we provide uniform bounds on the total variation of the wave front-tracking approximation through the introduction of suitable (and new) functionals. In §
8
we conclude the proof of the convergence of the wave front-tracking approximation. Finally, §
9
contain the other most innovative part of the present paper as in that section we show that any limit of our wave front-tracking approximation satisfies the boundary condition in (
1.5
). The proof is rather technical and involved, and unveils what we feel is interesting information on the limit solution at the domain boundary, see for instance Lemmas
9.5
,
9.6
,
9.7
and
9.9
.

1.2.
Proof roadmap

The proof of Theorem
1.1
relies on the introduction of a new wave front-tracking algorithm. Wave front-tracking algorithms stem from the celebrated Glimm
[
20
]
scheme and were introduced in the pivotal work by Dafermos
[
15
]
. We refer to
[
10
,
23
]
for an extended overview. In the present paper we build upon the algorithm discussed in
[
10
]
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, we recall
[
10
, Definition 7.1]
of
\varepsilon
-approximate solution of the Cauchy problem: heuristically speaking, it is a piecewise constant function with discontinuities occurring along finitely many straight lines in the
(t,x)
plane, which solves equation (
1.1
) and attains the initial datum up to an error “of size
\varepsilon
”. Also, the Lax admissibility condition is satisfied, again up to an error of size
\varepsilon
.

Definition 1.2
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, we say that
\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
is an
\varepsilon
-wave front tracking approximation of the initial-boundary value problem (
1.1
),(
1.5
) if it is an
\varepsilon
-approximate solution in the sense of
[
10
, Definition 7.1]
and furthermore

\|\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon}(0,\cdot)-\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\varepsilon,\quad\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon}(t,0)\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{\widetilde{v}}_{\varepsilon b}(t)\;\text{for a.e $t$ and for some $\mathbf{\widetilde{v}}_{\varepsilon b}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})$ s.t.}\;\|\mathbf{\widetilde{v}}_{\varepsilon b}-\mathbf{v}_{b}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\varepsilon.

Theorem
1.1
directly follows from Theorem
1.2
below.

Theorem 1.2
.

Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
for every
\varepsilon>0
there is an
\varepsilon
-wave front tracking approximation of the initial-boundary value problem (
1.1
), (
1.5
), which furthermore can be constructed in such a way that it satisfies the following properties. For every vanishing sequence
\{\varepsilon_{m}\}
there is a subsequence (which we do not relabel) such that the corresponding sequence
\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}
satisfies

(1.8)

\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon m}(t,\cdot)\to\mathbf{v}(t,\cdot)\quad\text{in $L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})$ as $m\to+\infty$ for every $t>0$}.

In the above formula
\mathbf{v}\in BV([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
for every
T>0
, is a Lax admissible distributional solution of (
1.1
) and satisfies the initial and boundary condition (
1.5
) in the sense of traces.

As pointed out before, as a limit of a wave front-tracking approximation, the solution
\mathbf{v}
actually enjoys further regularity properties, see the analysis in
[
10
, Ch.10]
. Note furthermore that we state that the convergence of the wave front-tracking approximation occurs up to subsequences because technically speaking we have not established uniqueness results for solutions of the initial-boundary value problem (
1.1
),(
1.5
). As mentioned above, it is highly likely that one could establish uniqueness of a suitable boundary Standard Riemann Semigroup and this would imply that the whole family
\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon}
converges as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. Finally, we recall Remark
1.2
and conclude that the analysis in
[
1
]
provides a proof of Theorem
1.2
in the non characteristic boundary case. Hence, in the following we focus on the characteristic boundary case, namely we assume that one eigenvalue of
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{f}
may attain the value
0
, see equation (
2.7
) for a more precise definition.

The building blocks of our wave front-tracking algorithm are the solution of the Riemann problem, which is a Cauchy problem where the initial datum has a single jump discontinuity at the origin, and of the so-called boundary Riemann problem, which is the initial-boundary value problem (
1.1
),(
1.5
) in the case where the data
\mathbf{v}_{0}
and
\mathbf{v}_{b}
are (in general, distinct) constant values in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. An admissible solution of the Riemann problem is constructed in the celebrated work by Lax
[
28
]
, whereas the solution of the boundary Riemann problem is discussed in
[
3
,
8
,
9
]
. Note that
[
8
,
9
]
deal with general hyperbolic systems, but the fact that we assume that the boundary characteristic field is either genuinely nonlinear or linearly degenerate leads to considerable simplifications that we use in our analysis. In §
4
we provide a quite detailed overview of
[
8
,
9
]
in the specific frameworks of genuinely nonlinear and linearly degenerate boundary characteristic field, and for the reader’s convenience we outline the main ideas and the basic steps in the construction in §
4.1
. Note that the starting point of the construction of the solution of the boundary Riemann problem is the boundary layer equation at the first line of (
1.4
), and indeed in §
3
we have to spend some work to write this equation in a sufficienly explicit form, a nontrivial task owing to the singularity of the matrix
\mathbf{D}
. Note furthermore that a key point for the construction of the solution of the boundary Riemann solver is the definition of the so-called
characteristic wave fan curve of admissible states

\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}
. Loosely speaking, in the case of a genuinely nonlinear boundary characteristic vector field, the curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
contains all the states that can be connected to the value
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
by rarefactions and Lax admissible shocks with nonnegative speed, and by boundary layers lying on a suitable center manifold. In the case of a linearly degenerate boundary characteristic vector field, the curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
contains all the states that can be connected to the value
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
by contact discontinuities with nonnegative speed, and by boundary layers lying on a suitable center manifold. We refer to Lemma
4.1
and §
4.4
for the detailed analysis.

After this preliminary analysis, in §
5
we define our wave front-tracking algorithm. The construction itself is fairly standard, but for the reader’s convenience we overview it here. As a first step, we approximate the initial and boundary data with piecewise constant functions with finitely many discontinuities. At each jump discontinuity of the initial datum we construct an approximate solution of the Riemann problem through an algorithm termed
accurate Riemann solver
, which yields a piecewise constant solution in the
(t,x)
plane with finitely many jump discontinuities along straight lines (the wave fronts after which the whole algorithm is named) emanating from the origin. At each jump discontinuity point of the boundary datum we instead use an algorithm that we term
accurate boundary Riemann solver.
We then juxtapose the solutions of the Riemann problems and consider the first
interaction time
, i.e. the first time at which either two discontinuity lines interact (that is, they intersect), or a discontinuity line reaches the boundary. In the first case, we solve the corresponding Riemann problem, in the second the corresponding boundary Riemann problem. We then extend the solution up to the next interaction time, solve the Riemann or boundary Riemann problem, and so on. To rule out the possibility that the total number of discontinuity lines blows up in finite time we introduce, as in
[
10
]
a technical correction and in some situations, defined in §
5.5
, we use so-called
simplified Riemann
and
boundary Riemann solvers
which introduce a minimal amount of new waves.

As a matter of fact, a key point in the proof of the convergence of a wave front-tracking algorithm is to establish the so-called
interaction estimates
, i.e. to obtain very precise information on the behavior of the approximate solution after an interaction time. In the case of our algorithm, this is done in §
6
. In particular, Lemmas
6.2
and
6.3
provide very refined estimates on the structure of the solution when a wave front of the boundary characteristic family hits the boundary. These estimates are in our opinion fairly innovative and of independent interest and, to the best of our knowledge, are the first boundary interaction estimates involving the positive part of the wave speed. Note furthermore that the proof of Lemma
6.2
, in turn, relies on estimate (
6.7
), which accurately controls the difference between Lax’s wave fan curve of admissible states and the characteristic wave fan curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}
. The proof of these results is based on a very careful analysis of the structure of the curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}
and uses the assumption that the characteristic vector field is either linearly degenerate or genuinely nonlinear.

In §
7
we show that the total variation of the
\varepsilon
-wave front-tracking approximation is uniformly bounded with respect to
\varepsilon
and time. As in
[
10
]
, the proof relies on the introduction of suitable Glimm-type functionals. The explicit form of our functional, given in §
7.1
, is new and in particular involves the waves speed besides their strength. In §
8
we establish the convergence result in (
1.8
), and show that the limit is a distributional solution of (
1.1
). The proof follows
the same main steps as in
[
10
]
, but the presence of the boundary and the consequent more complicated expression of our functionals accounts for much higher technicalities.

In §
9
we show that the limit function in (
1.8
) attains the boundary condition in (
1.5
). From the technical viewpoint, one of the main challenges in the analysis is the fact that, since the boundary is characteristic, in Definition
1.1
in general the boundary layer does not have as asymptotic state the trace
\mathbf{\bar{v}}
, but rather a possibly different state
\mathbf{\underline{v}}
which is connected to
\mathbf{\bar{v}}
through a
0
-speed Lax admissible shock (if the boundary characteristic field is genuinely nonlinear) or a
0
-speed contact discontinuity (if the boundary characteristic field is linearly degenerate). Let us now focus on the case of a genuinely nonlinear boundary characteristic field, which yields a richer and more interesting analysis. From the wave front-tracking approximation standpoint, the fact that
\mathbf{\bar{v}}\neq\mathbf{\underline{v}}
and hence that there is a non-trivial
0
-speed Lax admissible shock exactly located at the domain boundary may be due to three different mechanisms: (i) in the wave front-tracking approximation there is a
0
-speed Lax admissible shock located at the domain boundary, which persists in the limit; (ii) in the wave front-tracking approximation there is a non-trivial center component of the boundary layer, which in the limit gives origin to a
0
-speed Lax admissible shock located at the boundary; (iii) in the wave front-tracking approximation there are one or more shocks with vanishing speed that are located inside the domain but in the limit approach the domain boundary. Mechanisms (i) and (ii) are taken into account by Lemma
9.7
, whereas the analysis of mechanism (iii) is the most complicated from the technical viewpoint and relies on the definition of the set
\widetilde{E}
in (
9.2
). Up to negligible sets,
\widetilde{E}
is loosely speaking the set of points at which mechanism (iii) occurs and to show that the boundary condition (
1.5
) is satisfied on
\widetilde{E}
we rely on a series of technical results. In particular, Lemma
9.2
provides a uniform in
\varepsilon
bound on the total variation (with respect to time) of the flux function
\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon})
evaluated at the domain boundary. Note that this is a fairly non-trivial result as we do not expect that the traces
\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,0)
and
\mathbf{v}(\cdot,0)
have uniformly bounded total variation, see for instance the related counter-example
[
17
, §4.3]
. To show that the boundary condition (
1.5
) is satisfied on the complement
\mathbb{R}\setminus\widetilde{E}
we also rely on a considerably technical analysis; see in particular the proof of Lemma
9.5
, which requires a careful control of the behavior of the wave front-tracking approximation close to the domain boundary. To achieve this control we use among other things techniques introduces in
[
10
, Ch10]
to study the fine regularity properties of the limit of the wave front-tracking approximation.

Characteristic vs boundary characteristic vector field

We term

1.3.1.
Relation between the various constants

We now list the inequalities that in what follows we need to impose on the constants, and show that they are consistent.
We point out that as it will be clear in the following the constants
C_{i}
are given by the problem, whereas the constants
A
,
K_{i}
,
\delta
and
\delta^{\ast}
are to be chosen in the proof so that they satisfy suitable constraints. More precisely, we require that the above constants satisfy the following relations:

(1.9)

\max\{C_{7}[1+K_{1}\delta],C_{1}+K_{1}[C_{1}+C_{12}+C_{1}C_{12}]\delta\}+1\leq A

and

(1.10)

C_{2}+K_{1}[2C_{8}+C_{2}]\delta)+1\leq K_{2},\qquad C_{2}+K_{1}[2C_{8}+C_{2}]\delta+1\leq K_{1}.

and

(1.11)

C_{2}C_{7}+K_{1}[2C_{8}+C_{2}C_{7}]\delta)+1\leq K_{2},\qquad C_{2}C_{7}+K_{1}[2C_{8}+C_{2}C_{7}]\delta+1\leq K_{1}.

and

(1.12)

C_{6}+K_{1}\delta\big(2C_{6}+[1+C_{6}]C_{11}\big)+1\leq K_{3}

and

(1.13)

AC_{5}+1+K_{2}(\delta C_{4}C_{5}+2C_{3})\leq K_{1}(1-2A\delta C_{5}-A^{2}\delta^{2}C_{5}^{2})

and

(1.14)

C_{2}\delta\leq 1,\quad C_{8}\delta\leq\frac{1}{2},\quad C_{2}[1+C_{4}]\delta\leq 1

and

(1.15)

\max\{C_{14},1\}\delta^{\ast}\leq\delta

To this end we first choose
A
and
K_{2}
in such a way that

A\geq\max\{2C_{7},C_{1}+1\}+1

and
K_{2}\geq 2C_{7}C_{2}+1
. Next, we choose
K_{1}
and
K_{3}
such that

K_{1}\geq\max\{2[C_{2}C_{7}+1],2AC_{5}+2+2K_{2}(1+2C_{3})\},\quad K_{3}\geq C_{6}+2

Finally, we choose
\delta
sufficiently small to have

\begin{split}&K_{1}\delta\leq 1,\quad K_{1}[C_{1}+C_{12}+C_{1}C_{12}]\delta\leq 1,\quad C_{7}C_{2}\delta\leq\frac{1}{2},\quad\delta C_{4}C_{5}\leq 1,\\
&2A\delta C_{5}\leq\frac{1}{4},\quad A^{2}\delta^{2}C^{2}_{5}\leq\frac{1}{4},\quad K_{1}\delta\big(2C_{6}+[1+C_{6}]C_{11}\big)\delta\leq 1.\end{split}

and
\delta^{\ast}
sufficiently small to have (
1.15
).

2.
Hypotheses

In this section we state the hypotheses imposed on system (
1.6
) in the present work. In §
2.2
we define the function
\boldsymbol{\beta}
used to assign the boundary condition on the mixed hyperbolic-parabolic system (
1.6
). Finally, in §
2.3
we show that our assumptions are satisfied by the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equations, written in both Eulerian and Lagrangian coordinates.

2.1.
Statement of the hypotheses

To ease the exposition we state
global
assumptions, i.e. assumptions that hold for every
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. However, all our analysis is
local
, in the sense that the solution
\mathbf{u}
and the approximate solutions
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}
attain values in a small neighborhood of the value
\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{v}^{\ast})
, where
\mathbf{v}^{\ast}
is the same as in (
1.7
), and hence it suffices to assume that our assumptions hold in that neighborhood.

Hypothesis 1
.

System (
1.6
) is of the normal form, in the Kawashima Shizuta sense, see
[
27
]
. More precisely, the coefficients in (
1.6
) are smooth and satisfy the following assumptions:

i)

The matrix
\mathbf{B}
satisfies the block decomposition

(2.1)

\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{0}_{h\times h}&\mathbf{0}_{N-h}^{t}\\
\mathbf{0}_{N-h}&\mathbf{B}_{22}(\mathbf{u})\\
\end{array}\right)

for some symmetric and positive definite matrix
\mathbf{B}_{22}\in\mathbb{M}^{(N-h)\times(N-h)}
.

ii)

For every
\mathbf{u}
, the matrix
\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})
is symmetric.

iii)

For every
\mathbf{u}
, the matrix
\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{u})
is symmetric, positive definite and block diagonal, namely

(2.2)

\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{E}_{11}(\mathbf{u})&\mathbf{0}_{N-h}^{t}\\
\mathbf{0}_{N-h}&\mathbf{E}_{22}(\mathbf{u})\\
\end{array}\right),

where
\mathbf{E}_{11}\in\mathbb{M}^{h\times h}
and
\mathbf{E}_{22}\in\mathbb{M}^{(N-h)\times(N-h)}
.

iv)

The second order term can be written in the form
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}_{x})\mathbf{u}_{x}
, with

\mathbf{G}
satisfying

(2.3)

\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}_{x})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{0}_{h\times h}&\mathbf{0}_{N-h}^{t}\\
\mathbf{G}_{1}&\mathbf{G}_{2}\\
\end{array}\right),\qquad

for suitable functions
\mathbf{G}_{1}\in\mathbb{M}^{h\times(N-h)}
,
\mathbf{G}_{2}\in\mathbb{M}^{(N-h)\times(N-h)}
. The function
\mathbf{G}_{1}
depends on
\mathbf{u}
and on the last
N-h
components of
\mathbf{u}_{x}
and vanishes when all these components vanish. The function
\mathbf{G}_{2}
depends on
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{u}_{x}
and vanishes when
\mathbf{u}_{x}=\mathbf{0}_{N}
.

Note that the point iv) implies that

(2.4)

\mathbf{G}_{1}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{0}_{N-h})=\mathbf{0}_{h\times(N-h)},\quad\mathbf{G}_{2}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{0}_{N})=\mathbf{0}_{(N-h)\times(N-h)},\quad\text{for every $\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$}.

Hypothesis 2

(Kawashima-Shizuta condition)
.

For every
\mathbf{u}
the matrices
\mathbf{E}
,
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
satisfy

(2.5)

\Big\{\text{eigenvectors of}\;\mathbf{E}^{-1}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})\Big\}\cap\text{kernel}\;\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{u})=\emptyset.

Since the matrices
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{E}
are both symmetric by Hypothesis
1
, then owing to a classical result (see for instance
[
9
, Lemma10.1]
) the matrix
\mathbf{E}^{-1}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})

has
N
real eigenvalues, provided each eigenvalue is counted according to its multiplicity. We now introduce the standard hypothesis that the system is
strictly hyperbolic
.

Hypothesis 3
.

For every
\mathbf{u}
the matrix
\mathbf{E}^{-1}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})
has
N
distinct eigenvalues.

We term
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\dots,\lambda_{N}(\mathbf{u})
the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{E}^{-1}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})
, and
\mathbf{r}_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\dots,\mathbf{r}_{N}(\mathbf{u})
a set of corresponding eigenvectors. In the following, we mostly focus on the
boundary characteristic case
. More precisely, we assume that

(2.6)

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{u})<\dots<\lambda_{k-1}(\mathbf{u})\leq-c<0<c\leq\lambda_{k+1}(\mathbf{u})<\dots<\lambda_{N}(\mathbf{u}),\quad\text{for every $\mathbf{u}$}

for some
k=1,\dots,N
and
c>0
, and that eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u})
can attain the value
0
, i.e. there is
\mathbf{u}^{\ast}
such that

(2.7)

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})=0.

In the following we assume that
\mathbf{u}^{\ast}
is sufficiently close to our data, otherwise we can assume
\lambda_{k}\neq 0
because all our analysis is confined in a a small neighborhood of the data. Just to fix the ideas, we assume that
\mathbf{v}^{\ast}=\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})
satisfies (
1.7
).
Next, we recall that the
k
-th vector field is termed
genuinely nonlinear
if

(2.8)

\nabla\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{u})\geq d>0,\quad\text{for every $\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$}

and a suitable constant
d>0
. The
k
-th vector field is
linearly degenerate
if

(2.9)

\nabla\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{u})=0,\quad\text{for every $\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$}.

We now introduce the fairly standard assumption that every vector field is either linearly degenerate or genuinely nonlinear.

Hypothesis 4
.

For every
i=1,\dots,N
, the
i
-th vector field is either genuinely nonlinear or linearly degenerate.

To state our last assumption, we introduce the block decomposition of
\mathbf{A}
corresponding to the block decomposition (
2.1
), that is

(2.10)

\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{A}_{11}(\mathbf{u})&\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}(\mathbf{u})\\
\mathbf{A}_{21}(\mathbf{u})&\mathbf{A}_{22}(\mathbf{u}),\\
\end{array}\right)\qquad\mathbf{A}_{11}\in\mathbb{M}^{h\times h},\mathbf{A}_{22}\in\mathbb{M}^{(N-h)\times(N-h)},\mathbf{A}_{21}\in\mathbb{M}^{(N-h)\times h},

where
\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}
denotes the transpose of the matrix
\mathbf{A}_{21}
.

Hypothesis 5
.

The matrix
\mathbf{A}
satisfies one of the following conditions:

A)

we have

(2.11)

\mathrm{det}\ \mathbf{A}_{11}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0,\quad\text{for every $\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$};

B)

we have

(2.12)

\mathbf{A}_{11}(\mathbf{u})=\alpha(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{E}_{11}(\mathbf{u})

for
2
2

2

Note that condition (
2.12
) is only relevant if
h>1
. If
h=1
then
\mathbf{E}_{11}
is actually a scalar function which only attains strictly positive values since the matrix
\mathbf{E}
is positive definite owing to Hypothesis
1
, item (iii); hence, we can always set
\alpha(\mathbf{u}):=\mathbf{E}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{11}(\mathbf{u})
.
some
scalar
function
\alpha:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
. Also, if for some
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
we have
\alpha(\mathbf{u})=0
then

(2.13)

\nabla\alpha(\mathbf{u})=(\mathbf{0}_{h}^{t},\mathbf{s}^{t})

for some
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{N-h}
that is a linear combination of the columns of
\mathbf{A}_{21}(\mathbf{u})
.

C)

we have

(2.14)

\mathbf{A}_{11}(\mathbf{u})=\mathbf{0}_{h\times h},\quad\text{for every $\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$}.

Some remarks are here in order. First, conditions (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) are Hypotheses 4 and 5 in
[
9
, §2.1]
, respectively, and we refer to
[
9
, §2.1]
for some further comment. Second, as pointed out in §
2.3
the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equations (with both null and positive electrical resistivity) in Lagrangian coordinates satisfy (
2.14
), whereas the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equations (with both null and positive electrical resistivity) in Eulerian coordinates satisfy (
2.11
) if the fluid velocity is bounded away from
0
, and condition B) above otherwise. Third, Hypothesis
5
allows to write the boundary layers equation in a manageable form, see the analysis in §
3
. Finally, by combining the analysis in
[
8
,
9
]
we could handle more general assumptions than Hypothesis
5
, but to ease the exposition we decided to focus on conditions i), ii) and iii) above.

2.2.
The boundary condition for the mixed hyperbolic-parabolic system

We recall the construction of the function
\boldsymbol{\beta}
given in
[
8
,
9
]
by distinguishing the 3 cases considered in the statement of Hypothesis
5
. The function
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\beta}}
is then obtained from
\boldsymbol{\beta}
by passing to the
\mathbf{v}
coordinates.

Case A):
owing to item ii) in Hypothesis
1
, the matrix
\mathbf{A}_{11}
is symmetric and hence all its eigenvalues are real. We term
\ell\leq h
the number of negative eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}_{11}
and

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{11}:\mathbb{R}^{h}\to\mathbb{R}^{h}
the projection onto the subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{h}
generated by the eigenvectors associated to positive eigenvalues (remember that
0
cannot be an eigenvalue of
\mathbf{A}_{11}
owing to (
2.11
)). To define
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, we introduce the decomposition

(2.15)

\mathbf{u}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{u}_{1}\\
\mathbf{u}_{2}\\
\end{array}\right),\qquad\mathbf{u}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{h},\;\mathbf{u}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{N-h}

and set

(2.16)

\boldsymbol{\beta}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N},\qquad\boldsymbol{\beta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}_{b}):=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\boldsymbol{\pi}_{11}(\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{1b})\\
\mathbf{u}_{2}-\mathbf{u}_{2b}\\
\end{array}\right)

Case B):
the function
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is defined in
[
9
, §2.2]
, see in particular
[
9
, (2.17)]
.

Case C):
we recall the decomposition (
2.15
) and define
\boldsymbol{\beta}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
by setting

(2.17)

\boldsymbol{\beta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}_{b}):=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{0}_{h}\\
\mathbf{u}_{2}-\mathbf{u}_{2b}\end{array}\right)

Remark 2.1
.

A word of warning. We explicitely point out, that even if the function
\boldsymbol{\beta}
always attains values in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, its explicit form implies that by imposing say
\boldsymbol{\beta}(\mathbf{u}(t,0),\mathbf{u}_{b})=\mathbf{0}_{N}
we are actually imposing on
\mathbf{u}(t,0)
the following number of
conditions:
N-\ell
conditions in
case A
) and
N-h
conditions in
case C)
. In
case B)
we are imposing
N-\ell(\mathbf{u}_{b})
conditions, where
\ell(\mathbf{u}_{b})
denotes the number of non-positive eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}_{11}(\mathbf{u}_{b})
and depends on
\mathbf{u}_{b}
.

2.3.
Applications to the inviscid limit of the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equations in Eulerian and Lagrangian coordinates

In this paragraph we verify that Hypotheses
2
,
\dots
,
5
are satisfied by the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equation written in both Lagrangian and Eulerian coordinates. We recall that the Navier-Stokes equations describe the motion of a viscous and compressible fluid, whereas the viscous MHD equations in one space dimension describe the propagation of plane waves in an electrically charged viscous and compressible fluid.

2.3.1.
Navier-Stokes equation in Eulerian coordinates

We refer to
[
9
, §2.3]
and conclude that in this case
h=1
and

\mathbf{E}^{-1}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}u&\rho&0\\
R\theta/\rho&u&R\\
0&R\theta/e_{\theta}&u\\
\end{array}\right),\qquad\mathbf{u}=(\rho,u,\theta)

In the above expression,
\rho
,
u
and
\theta
denote the fluid density,
the fluid velocity, and
the absolute temperature, respectively. The internal energy
e
is a function of
\theta
and satisfies

e_{\theta}>0
. We are focusing on the case of a polytropic gas, with pressure law
p=R\theta\rho
.
The eigencouples of
\mathbf{E}^{-1}\mathbf{A}
are

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{u})=u-c,\;\mathbf{r}_{1}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\rho e_{\theta}\\
-e_{\theta}c\\
R\theta\\
\end{array}\right),\quad\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{u})=u,\;\mathbf{r}_{2}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-\rho\\
0\\
\theta\\
\end{array}\right),\quad\lambda_{3}(\mathbf{u})=u+c,\;\mathbf{r}_{3}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\rho e_{\theta}\\
e_{\theta}c\\
R\theta\\
\end{array}\right)

where
c=\sqrt{R\theta+R^{2}\theta/e_{\theta}}
denotes the sound speed. Since
e_{\theta}>0
,
c_{\theta}>0
, the first and third characteristic fields are genuinely nonlinear, the second is linearly degenerate. Summing up, when the fluid velocity is close to
c
the boundary is characteristic and
k=1
, when it is close to
-c
the boundary is again characteristic and
k=3
; in both cases, (
2.8
), (
2.11
) are both satisfied. When the fluid velocity vanishes the boundary is characteristic with linearly degenerate boundary characteristic field; also, the system satisfies conditions (
2.12
) and (
2.13
), see
[
9
, §2.3]
for the explicit computations.

2.3.2.
Navier-Stokes equations in Lagrangian coordinates

By using the analysis in
[
30
, §4.1]
we conclude that Hypotheses
1
,
2
and point C) in Hypothesis
5
are satisfied with
h=1
and furthermore

\mathbf{E}^{-1}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&-1&0\\
-R\theta/\tau^{2}&0&R/\tau\\
0&R\theta/\tau e_{\theta}&0\\
\end{array}\right),\qquad\mathbf{u}=(\tau,u,\theta),

where
\tau=\rho^{-1}
is the specific volume. The eigencouples of the above matrix are

\lambda_{1}=-\frac{c}{\tau},\;\mathbf{r}_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}e_{\theta}\\
ce_{\theta}/\tau\\
-R\theta/\tau\end{array}\right),\quad\lambda_{2}=0,\;\mathbf{r}_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-\tau/\theta\\
0\\
1\end{array}\right),\quad\lambda_{3}=\frac{c}{\tau},\;\mathbf{r}_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-e_{\theta}\\
ce_{\theta}/\tau\\
R\theta/\tau\end{array}\right).

The first and third characteristic fields are genuinely nonlinear, the second is linearly degenerate.

2.3.3.
Viscous MHD equation in Eulerian coordinates

We refer to
[
9
, §2.3]
and conclude that Hypotheses
1
,
2
are both satisfied. Note that
h=1
if the electrical resistivity is strictly positive, and
h=3
when the electrical resistivity is
0
. Also, we have

\mathbf{E}^{-1}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}u&\mathbf{0}_{2}^{t}&\rho&\mathbf{0}_{2}^{t}&0\\
\mathbf{0}_{2}&u\mathbf{I}_{2}&\mathbf{b}&-\beta\mathbf{I}_{2}&\mathbf{0}_{2}\\
R\theta/\rho&\mathbf{b}^{t}/\rho&u&\mathbf{0}_{2}^{t}&R\\
\mathbf{0}_{2}&-\beta\mathbf{I}_{2}&\mathbf{0}_{2}&u\mathbf{I}_{2}&\mathbf{0}_{2}\\
0&\mathbf{0}_{2}^{t}&R\theta/e_{\theta}&\mathbf{0}_{2}^{t}&u\\
\end{array}\right),\qquad\mathbf{u}=(\rho,\mathbf{b},u,\mathbf{w},\theta),

where
(\beta,\mathbf{b})
,
\beta>0
denotes the magnetic field and
(u,\mathbf{w})
is the fluid velocity.
By direct computations, one can verify that if
\mathbf{b}\neq\mathbf{0}_{2}
the matrix
\mathbf{E}^{-1}\mathbf{A}
satisfies Hypothesis
3
. For almost every given value
\mathbf{u}
, Hypothesis
4
is satisfied in a suitable neighborhood of
\mathbf{u}
.
Also, the fields corresponding to the eigenvectors
\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{u})=u-\beta\sqrt{\rho}
,
\lambda_{4}(\mathbf{u})=u
,
\lambda_{6}(\mathbf{u})=u+\beta\sqrt{\rho}
are linearly degenerate as the corresponding eigenvectors are

\mathbf{r}_{2}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0\\
-b_{2}\sqrt{\rho}\\
b_{1}\sqrt{\rho}\\
0\\
-b_{2}\\
b_{1}\\
0\end{array}\right),\quad\mathbf{r}_{4}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-\rho\\
0\\
0\\
0\\
0\\
0\\
\theta\\
\end{array}\right),\quad\mathbf{r}_{6}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0\\
-b_{2}\sqrt{\rho}\\
b_{1}\sqrt{\rho}\\
0\\
-b_{2}\\
b_{1}\\
0\end{array}\right).

If
u
vanishes then the fourth characteristic field is boundary characteristic, it satisfies (
2.9
) and by relying on the analysis in
[
9
, §2.4]
we conclude that the system satisfies conditions (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) in both cases of null and positive electrical resistivity. If the eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{u})
vanishes for some
i\neq 4
then by relying on
[
9
, §2.4]
one can show that (
2.11
) is satisfied in both cases of null and positive electrical resistivity.

2.3.4.
Viscous MHD equation in Lagrangian coordinates

By relying on
[
30
, §4.2]
we get that Hypotheses
1
and
2
are satisfied and moreover

\mathbf{E}^{-1}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}0&\mathbf{0}_{2}^{t}&-1&\mathbf{0}_{2}^{t}&0\\
\mathbf{0}_{2}&\mathbf{0}_{2\times 2}&\mathbf{b}/\tau&-\beta/\tau\mathbf{I}_{2}&\mathbf{0}_{2}\\
-R\theta/\tau^{2}&\mathbf{b}^{t}&0&\mathbf{0}_{2}^{t}&R/\tau\\
\mathbf{0}_{2}&-\beta\mathbf{I}_{2}&\mathbf{0}_{2}&\mathbf{0}_{2\times 2}&\mathbf{0}_{2}\\
0&\mathbf{0}_{2}^{t}&R\theta/\tau e_{\theta}&\mathbf{0}_{2}^{t}&0\\
\end{array}\right),\qquad\mathbf{u}=(\tau,\mathbf{b},u,\mathbf{w},\theta).

Again for almost every given value
\mathbf{u}
, Hypothesis
4
is satisfied in a suitable neighborhood of
\mathbf{u}
.
Note furthermore that the characteristic fields associated to the eigenvalues
\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{u})=-\beta/\sqrt{\tau}
,
\lambda_{4}(\mathbf{u})=0
and
\lambda_{6}(\mathbf{u})=\beta/\sqrt{\tau}
are linearly degenerate as the corresponding eigenvectors are

\mathbf{r}_{2}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0\\
-b_{2}/\sqrt{\tau}\\
b_{1}/\sqrt{\tau}\\
0\\
-b_{2}\\
b_{1}\\
0\end{array}\right)\quad\mathbf{r}_{4}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\tau\\
0\\
0\\
0\\
0\\
0\\
\theta\\
\end{array}\right),\quad\mathbf{r}_{6}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0\\
b_{2}/\sqrt{\tau}\\
-b_{1}/\sqrt{\tau}\\
0\\
-b_{2}\\
b_{1}\\
0\end{array}\right)

By relying on
[
30
, §4.2]
we also get (
2.14
) in both cases of null and positive electrical resistivity.

3.
Boundary layers analysis

Boundary layers are steady solutions of (
1.6
) and hence satisfy the equation

(3.1)

\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{u}^{\prime\prime}+\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\mathbf{u}^{\prime}.

Since the matrix
\mathbf{B}
is singular, see (
2.1
), some work is required to write the above equation in an explicit form, which is done in this section by following the analysis in
[
8
,
9
]
and separately considering cases A), B) and C) in Hypothesis
5
. We also provide the construction of the center manifold, which is relevant in our analysis because we deal with the boundary characteristic case and hence we have to take into account that in general the boundary layers have a component lying on the center manifold. The exposition is organized as follows: in §
3.1
we consider case A) in Hypothesis
5
, in §
3.2
case B) and in §
3.3
case C).

3.1.
Case A) of Hypthesis
5

3.1.1.
Boundary layers equation

We assume (
2.8
) and (
2.11
) and introduce the decomposition (
2.15
)
and by
using Hypothesis
1
we recast (
3.1
) as

(3.2)

\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}=\mathbf{a}(\boldsymbol{\omega})

provided
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{2N-h}

and
\mathbf{a}:\mathbb{R}^{2N-h}\to\mathbb{R}^{2N-h}
are defined by setting

(3.3)

\boldsymbol{\omega}\!:=\!\!\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{u}_{1}\\
\mathbf{u}_{2}\\
\mathbf{z}_{2}\\
\end{array}\right)\ \mathbf{a}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\!:=\!\!\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{2}\\
\mathbf{z}_{2}\\
\mathbf{B}^{-1}_{22}\displaystyle{\big[(\mathbf{G}_{1}-\mathbf{A}_{21})\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}+\mathbf{A}_{22}-\mathbf{G}_{2}\big]\mathbf{z}_{2}}\\
\end{array}\right)\ \mathbf{u}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{h},\mathbf{u}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{N-h},\mathbf{z}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{N-h}.

Since we need it in the following we introduce the projection

(3.4)

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}:\mathbb{R}^{2N-h}\to\mathbb{R}^{N},\quad\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{z}):=\mathbf{u}

We linearize system (
3.2
) at
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast}:=(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},\mathbf{0}_{N-h})
, where
\mathbf{u}^{\ast}
is the same as in (
2.7
) and
\mathbf{G}_{1}
and
\mathbf{G}_{2}
satisfy (
2.4
). To compute its center and stable space, we use the results that follow.

Lemma 3.1
.

If the vector
\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{u})\in\mathbb{R}^{N-h}
and the scalar
\mu(\mathbf{u})
satisfy

(3.5)

\big[-\mathbf{A}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}(\mathbf{u})+\mathbf{A}_{22}(\mathbf{u})-\mu(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{B}_{22}(\mathbf{u})\big]\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{u})=\mathbf{0}_{N-h}

then

(3.6)

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{j}(\mathbf{u}):=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{u})\\
\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{u})\\
\end{array}\right)

satisfies
[\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})-\mu(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{u})]\boldsymbol{\rho}(\mathbf{u})=\mathbf{0}_{N}
. Conversely, every vector belonging to the kernel of
[\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})-\mu\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{u})]
can be written in the form (
3.6
), with
\mathbf{p}
satisfying (
3.5
).

Next, we point out that the matrix
\mathbf{B}_{22}^{-1}[-\mathbf{A}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}+\mathbf{A}_{22}]\in\mathbb{M}^{(N-h)\times(N-h)}
is symmetric and use a particular case of
[
8
, Lemma 4.7]
. Note that owing to (
2.11
) the number
0
cannot be an eigenvalue of
\mathbf{A}_{11}
and that the following lemma implies in particular that
\ell\leq k-1
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\ell
denote the number of negative eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}_{11}
3
3

3

Owing to assumption (
2.11
),
\ell
does not depend on
\mathbf{u}
, then
the signature of the matrix
\mathbf{B}_{22}^{-1}[-\mathbf{A}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}+\mathbf{A}_{22}](\mathbf{u}^{\ast})
is as follows:
k-1-\ell
eigenvalues are strictly negative (each of them is counted according to its multiplicity),
0
is an eigenvalue with multiplicity
1
, and
N-h-k+\ell
eigenvalues are strictly positive.

3.1.2.
Center manifold construction

We apply the above lemma and conclude that the center space of
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{g}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast})
has dimension
N+1
and it is given by

(3.7)

M^{c}:=\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathrm{span}<\mathbf{p}_{k}>,

where
\mathbf{p}_{k}
is a nonzero vector in the kernel of
\mathbf{B}^{-1}_{22}\big[-\mathbf{A}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}+\mathbf{A}_{22}\big](\mathbf{u}^{\ast})
. We apply the Center Manifold Theorem and fix a center manifold
4
4

4

The center manifold is in general not unique, but we can arbitrarily fix one.

\mathcal{M}^{c}
. By arguing as in
[
7
, §4]
we establish the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

There is a map
\mathbf{p}_{c}
, defined in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)
, attaining values in
\mathbb{R}^{N-h}
and satisfying the following properties:

a)

\mathbf{p}_{c}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)
is a nontrivial vector in the kernel of
\big[-\mathbf{A}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}+\mathbf{A}_{22}\big](\mathbf{u}^{\ast})
.

b)

in a small enough neighborhood of
(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)
,

(3.8)

(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{z}_{2})\in\mathcal{M}^{c}\iff\mathbf{z}_{2}=z_{c}\mathbf{p}_{c}(\mathbf{u},z_{c})\quad\text{for some $z_{c}\in\mathbb{R}$}.

c)

We have

(3.9)

\mathbf{p}^{t}_{c}\mathbf{B}_{22}\mathbf{p}_{c}\equiv 1.

Also, by restricting system (
3.1
) on
\mathcal{M}^{c}
we obtain

(3.10)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\prime}=-z_{c}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}\mathbf{p}_{c}\\
\mathbf{u}_{2}^{\prime}=z_{c}\mathbf{p}_{c}\\
z_{c}^{\prime}=z_{c}\theta_{c}\\
\end{array}\right.

where the function
\theta_{c}
is defined in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)
, attains real values and satisfies

(3.11)

\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)=0.

Since we need it in the following, we introduce the notation

(3.12)

\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},z_{c})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}\mathbf{p}_{c}\\
\mathbf{p}_{c}\\
\end{array}\right)

and write down the explicit expression of
\theta_{c}
:

(3.13)

\begin{split}\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u},z_{c}):=\frac{1}{1+z_{c}\mathbf{p}_{c}^{t}\,\partial_{z_{c}}\mathbf{p}_{c}}\mathbf{p}_{c}^{t}\Big[&(\mathbf{G}_{1}-\mathbf{A}_{21})\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}+\mathbf{A}_{22}-\mathbf{G}_{2}-z_{c}\mathbf{D}_{\mathbf{u}_{1}}\mathbf{p}_{c}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}\mathbf{p}_{c}-z_{c}\mathbf{D}_{\mathbf{u}_{2}}\mathbf{p}_{c}\ \Big]\mathbf{p}_{c}\end{split}

Note that by combining property a) above with (
2.4
) we get (
3.11
) from (
3.13
).

The next lemma collects some properties
of
\theta_{c}
we use in the following.

Lemma 3.4
.

We have

(3.14)

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u})=\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)a(\mathbf{u})\quad\text{for some smooth function $a(\mathbf{u})\geq a_{0}>0$},

where
a_{0}>0
is a suitable constant. Also, if
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u})=0
then
\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)=\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{u})
, where
\mathbf{r}_{c}
and
\mathbf{r}_{k}
are as in (
3.12
) and (
3.6
), respectively.

Note that, if
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u})=0
then
\mu(\mathbf{u})=0
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
3.1
.

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

We plug the equality
\mathbf{z}_{2}=z_{c}\mathbf{p}_{c}
into (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), use the equality
z^{\prime}_{c}=\theta_{c}z_{c}
and get

\theta_{c}z_{c}\mathbf{p}_{c}+z_{c}\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{c}=\mathbf{B}^{-1}_{22}\big[(\mathbf{G}_{1}-\mathbf{A}_{21})\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}+\mathbf{A}_{22}-\mathbf{G}_{2}\big]z_{c}\mathbf{p}_{c}.

We divide both sides of the above equality by
z_{c}
, evaluate the result at
(\mathbf{u},0)
, recall (
2.3
) and point out that, owing to (
3.10
),
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{c}=\mathbf{0}_{N-h}
when
z_{c}=0
.
We arrive at
\theta_{c}\mathbf{B}_{22}\mathbf{p}_{c}=\big[-\mathbf{A}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}+\mathbf{A}_{22}\big]\mathbf{p}_{c}
, which in turn by applying Lemma
3.1
with
\mu=\theta_{c}
yields

(3.15)

[\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})-\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{u})]\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)=\mathbf{0}_{N},\;

provided
\mathbf{r}_{c}
is the same vector as in (
3.12
).
Owing to property a) in the statement of Lemma
3.3
and to Lemma
3.1
,
\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)
is an eigenvector of
\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})
associated to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})=0
. We then define the continuous function
\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{u})
in such a way that
\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{u})

is an eigenvector of
\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})
associated to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u})
, and
\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})=\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)
. Note that the corresponding
vector
\mathbf{p}_{k}(\mathbf{u})
given by Lemma
3.1
satisfies
\mathbf{p}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})=\mathbf{p}_{c}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0).

We now use (
3.15
), recall that all the matrices
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{E}
are symmetric and conclude that

(3.16)

\mathbf{r}^{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)=\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)\mathbf{r}^{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)=\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{r}^{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0).

Note that

\mathbf{r}^{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:B},\eqref{e:errei},\eqref{e:errec}}}{{=}}\mathbf{p}^{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})\mathbf{B}_{22}\mathbf{p}_{c}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{p}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})=\mathbf{p}^{t}_{c}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)}}{{=}}\mathbf{p}^{t}_{c}\mathbf{B}_{22}\mathbf{p}_{c}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:propc}}}{{=}}1

and that

\mathbf{r}^{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})=\mathbf{r}^{t}_{c}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)}}{{=}}\mathbf{r}^{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Hypothesis~\ref {h:normal}}}}{{>}}0.

This implies that both
\mathbf{r}^{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)
and
\mathbf{r}^{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)
are strictly positive if
\mathbf{u}
is sufficiently close to
\mathbf{u}^{\ast}
and hence that (
3.16
) implies (
3.14
), provided
a(\mathbf{u}):=\mathbf{r}^{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)/\mathbf{r}^{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)
. By combining (
3.14
) with (
3.15
) we conclude that we can choose
\mathbf{r}_{k}
in such a way that
\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)=\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{u})
when
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u})=0
.
∎

3.2.
Case B) of Hypothesis
5

We refer to
[
9
]
. In particular, the boundary layers equation is discussed in
[
9
, §3.1]
, whereas the construction of the center component of the boundary layers is given in
[
9
, §4]
.

3.3.
Case C) of Hypothesis
5

3.3.1.
Boundary layers equation

Lemma 3.5
.

Under (
2.14
) we have that for every
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
the rank of the matrix
\mathbf{A}_{21}(\mathbf{u})
is
h
.

Proof.

Assume by contradiction that there is
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{h}
such that
\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}_{N-h}
then

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{0}_{h\times h}&\mathbf{A}_{21}^{t}\\
\mathbf{A}_{21}&\mathbf{A}_{22}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{x}\\
\mathbf{0}_{N-h}\\
\end{array}\right)=\mathbf{0}_{N}

and this contradicts the Kawashima-Shizuta condition (
2.5
).
∎

Up to a permutation of columns, we can assume that

(3.17)

\mathbf{A}_{21}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}\\
\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}\\
\end{array}\right),\quad\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}\in\mathbb{M}^{h\times h},\;\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}\in\mathbb{M}^{(N-2h)\times h},\;\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}\in\mathbb{GL}(h),

that is the matrix
\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}(\mathbf{u})
is invertible for every
\mathbf{u}
. We now introduce the block decomposition of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
corresponding to the above decomposition of
\mathbf{A}_{21}
and we arrive at

\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbf{0}_{h\times h}&\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}&\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\\
\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}&\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{22}}&\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{32}}^{t}\\
\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}&\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{32}}&\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{33}}\\
\end{array}\right)\;\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{22}}\in\mathbb{M}^{h\times h},\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{32}}\in\mathbb{M}^{(N-2h)\times h},\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{33}}\in\mathbb{M}^{(N-2h)\times(N-2h)}

and

\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{u})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbf{0}_{h\times h}&\mathbf{0}_{h\times h}&\mathbf{0}_{h\times(N-2h)}\\
\mathbf{0}_{h\times h}&\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{22}}&\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}^{t}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(N-2h)\times h}&\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}&\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{33}}\\
\end{array}\right),\;\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{22}}\in\mathbb{M}^{h\times h},\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}\in\mathbb{M}^{(N-2h)\times h},\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{33}}\in\mathbb{M}^{(N-2h)\times(N-2h)}.

Next, we write

\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbf{z}_{1}\\
\mathbf{z}_{2}\\
\mathbf{z}_{3}\\
\end{array}\right),\;\mathbf{z}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{h},\;\mathbf{z}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{h},\mathbf{z}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{N-2h},\quad\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}_{x})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbf{0}_{h\times h}&\mathbf{0}_{h}&\mathbf{0}_{N-2h}^{t}\\
\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{21}}&\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{22}}&\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{23}}\\
\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{31}}&\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{32}}&\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{33}}\\
\end{array}\right)

and point out that (
3.1
) yields

(3.18)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{2}+\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{3}=\mathbf{0}_{h}\\
\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{22}}\mathbf{z}_{2}+\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{32}}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{3}=\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{22}}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{2}+\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}^{t}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{3}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{21}}\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{22}}\mathbf{z}_{2}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{23}}\mathbf{z}_{3}\\
\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{32}}\mathbf{z}_{2}+\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{33}}\mathbf{z}_{3}=\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{2}+\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{33}}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{3}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{31}}\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{32}}\mathbf{z}_{2}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{33}}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{3}.\end{array}\right.

We solve the first equation for
\mathbf{z}_{2}
, which yields

(3.19)

\mathbf{z}_{2}=-[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{3}\implies\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\prime}=-[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{3}+\mathbf{H}_{1}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{z}_{3})\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{H}_{3}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{z}_{3})\mathbf{z}_{3}

for suitable functions
\mathbf{H}_{1}\in\mathbb{M}^{h\times h}
,
\mathbf{H}_{3}\in\mathbb{M}^{h\times(N-2h)}
. The exact expression of
\mathbf{H}_{1}
and
\mathbf{H}_{3}
is not relevant here, what is important is that

(3.20)

\mathbf{H}_{1}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{0}_{N-2h})=\mathbf{0}_{h\times h},\;\mathbf{H}_{3}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{0}_{N-2h})=\mathbf{0}_{h\times(2N-h)}\;\text{for every $\mathbf{u}$}.

From the second equation in (
3.18
) we get

(3.21)

\begin{split}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}+\mathbf{H}_{1}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{21}}]\mathbf{z}_{1}=&\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{22}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{3}-\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{32}}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{3}-\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{22}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{3}\\
&+\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{22}}\mathbf{H}_{3}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{z}_{3})\mathbf{z}_{3}+\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}^{t}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{3}-\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{22}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{3}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{23}}\mathbf{z}_{3}\end{split}

We recall that by assumption
\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{21}}
and
\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{31}}
only depend on
\mathbf{u}
,
\mathbf{z}_{2}
and
\mathbf{z}_{3}
and hence, owing to (
3.19
), on
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{z}_{3}
, and vanish when
\mathbf{z}_{3}=\mathbf{0}_{N-2h}
. Owing to (
3.20
), this implies that in a neighborhood of
\mathbf{z}_{3}=\mathbf{0}_{N-2h}
the matrix

[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}+\mathbf{H}_{1}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{21}}]
is invertible as
\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}
and hence by using the above expression we can write
\mathbf{z}_{1}
as a function of
\mathbf{u}
,
\mathbf{z}_{3}
and
\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\prime}
. By plugging this function into the third line of (
3.18
) and using again (
3.19
) we get

\begin{split}\Big[\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{33}}&+\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}+\mathbf{H}_{1}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{21}}]^{-1}[\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{22}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}-\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}^{t}]-\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\Big]\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{3}\\
&=\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}+\mathbf{H}_{1}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{21}}]^{-1}\Big[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{22}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}-\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{32}}^{t}+\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{22}}\mathbf{H}_{3}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{z}_{3})-\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{22}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{23}}\Big]\mathbf{z}_{3}\\
&\quad-\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{32}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{3}+\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{33}}\mathbf{z}_{3}-\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}\mathbf{H}_{1}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{z}_{3})\mathbf{z}_{1}-\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}\mathbf{H}_{3}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{z}_{3})\mathbf{z}_{3}-\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{31}}\mathbf{z}_{1}\\
&\quad+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{32}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{3}+\mathbf{\widetilde{G}_{33}}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{3}\phantom{\Big]}\end{split}

We have the following lemma

Lemma 3.6
.

For every
\mathbf{u}
the matrix

\Big[\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{33}}+\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}]^{-1}[\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{22}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}-\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}^{t}]-\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\Big]

is invertible.

Proof.

The lemma is a particular case of
[
8
, Lemma 4.3]
, so we only provide a sketch of the proof.
Assume by contradiction there is
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{N-2r}
,
\mathbf{s}\neq\mathbf{0}_{N-2r}
such that

\Big[\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{33}}+\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}]^{-1}[\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{22}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}-\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}^{t}]-\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\Big]\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{0}_{N-2r}.

Set

\mathbf{q}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\mathbf{s}\\
\mathbf{s}\end{array}\right)\implies\mathbf{q}^{t}\mathbf{B}_{22}\mathbf{q}=\Big(-\mathbf{p}^{t}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}]^{-1};\mathbf{s}^{t}\Big)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{22}}&\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}^{t}\\
\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}&\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{33}}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\mathbf{s}\\
\mathbf{s}\end{array}\right)=0

and this contradicts the assumption that
\mathbf{B}_{22}
is positive definite.
∎

By using Lemma
3.6
and the fact that
\mathbf{H}_{1}
and
\mathbf{\widetilde{G}}_{21}
vanish when the derivative vanishes we can eventually write an explicit expression for
\mathbf{z}_{3}^{\prime}
in terms of
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{z}_{3}
. By arguing as in §
3.1
we can then rewrite (
3.1
) as a first order ODE for
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{z}_{3}
,

(3.22)

\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}=\mathbf{g}(\boldsymbol{\omega}),\qquad\boldsymbol{\omega}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{z}_{3}\\
\end{array}\right)

The exact expression of the right-hand side
\mathbf{g}
is not relevant here, what is important is that by linearizing at
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast}=(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},\mathbf{0}_{N-2h})
, where
\mathbf{u}^{\ast}
satisfies (
2.7
), we get the matrix

\mathbf{D}\mathbf{g}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{0}_{N\times N}&\mathbf{L}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(N-2h)\times N}&\mathbf{N}\\
\end{array}\right),

where

\begin{split}\mathbf{N}:=&\Big[\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{33}}+\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}]^{-1}[\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{22}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}-\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}^{t}]-\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{32}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\Big]^{-1}\\
&\times\Big[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}]^{-1}\big[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{22}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}-\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{32}}^{t}\big]-\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{32}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}+\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{33}}\Big].\end{split}

and

\mathbf{L}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{22}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}-\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{32}}^{t}\mathbf{z}_{3}-\mathbf{\widetilde{B}_{22}}[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\mathbf{N}\\
-[\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{21}}^{t}]^{-1}\mathbf{\widetilde{A}_{31}}^{t}\\
\mathbf{I}_{(N-2h)\times(N-2h)}\\
\end{array}\right).

Next, we point out that the matrix
\mathbf{N}
is symmetric, and that by direct computations one can check that
\mathbf{N}\boldsymbol{\xi}=\mu\boldsymbol{\xi}
if and only if
(\mathbf{A}-\mu\mathbf{B})\mathbf{L}\boldsymbol{\xi}=\mathbf{0}_{N}
. Next, we recall (
2.7
) and (
2.14
) and apply
[
9
, Lemma 10.3]
(see also
[
8
, Lemma 4.7]
) to conclude that the matrix
\mathbf{N}
has
k-1-h
eigenvalues with strictly negative real part (each of them counted according to its multiplicity) and the eigenvalue
0
has multiplicity
1
.
This implies that the stable manifold of system (
3.22
) linearized at the equilibrium point
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast}
has dimension
k-1-h
.

4.
Solutions of the Riemann and boundary Riemann problem

In this section we go over the construction of the solutions of the so-called Riemann and boundary Riemann problems, which is pivotal to the construction of our wave front-tracking algorithm. The analysis relies on
[
8
,
9
,
28
]
. Note however that the assumptions that the
k
-th (boundary characteristic) field is either linearly degenerate or genuinely nonlinear provides considerable simplifications with respect to the original analysis in
[
8
,
9
]
, and hence for the reader’s convenience we provide the complete construction of the solution of the boundary Riemann problem in these cases. Since the discussion in the present section is fairly technical, but also very important for the subsequent analysis, in §
4.1
for the reader’s convenience we provide an informal overview of the main ideas. In the next paragraphs we then provide the complete construction with the technical details.

4.1.
An informal roadmap to the construction of the solution of the boundary Riemann problem

We first recall that the Riemann problem is a Cauchy problem posed by coupling (
1.1
) with the initial datum

(4.1)

\mathbf{v}(0,x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{v}^{-}&x<0\\
\mathbf{v}^{+}&x>0\\
\end{array}\right.,\quad\mathbf{v}^{-},\mathbf{v}^{+}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}.

In his pioneering work
[
28
]
, Lax constructed an admissible solution of (
1.1
),(
4.1
) under the main assumptions that
|\mathbf{v}^{+}-\mathbf{v}^{-}|
is sufficiently small and that every vector field is either genuinely nonlinear or linearly degenerate. As a matter of fact, Lax’s analysis implies that to solve the Riemann problem (
1.1
),(
4.1
) one has to determine
(s_{1},\dots,s_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
in such a way that

(4.2)

\mathbf{u}^{-}=\mathbf{t}_{1}(\cdot,s_{1})\circ\mathbf{t}_{2}(\cdot,s_{2})\circ\dots\circ\mathbf{t}_{N}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s_{N}),

where
\mathbf{u}^{\pm}=\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{v}^{\pm})
and we are using the
\mathbf{u}
variables since it will be more convenient for what follows. Also, in (
4.2
)
\mathbf{t}_{i}
denotes the so-called
wave fan curves of admissible states
5
5

5

Note that for technical reasons we introduce a slight difference with respect to the original paper
[
28
]
. In particular,
\mathbf{t}_{i}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}},\cdot)
is the wave fan curve of admissible
left
states, whereas in the original work
[
28
]
Lax constructed the wave fan curve of admissible
right
states.
, whose construction we review in §
4.2
.

Let us now move towards the so-called
boundary Riemann problem
: to pose it, we consider the underlying viscous approximation (
1.2
) and correspondigly assign the boundary condition according to Definition
1.1
. The boundary Riemann problem is the initial-boundary value problem posed by coupling (
1.1
) with the data

(4.3)

\mathbf{v}(0,\cdot)=\mathbf{v}^{+},\qquad\mathbf{v}(\cdot,0)\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{v}_{b},

for given data
\mathbf{v}^{+}
,
\mathbf{v}_{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. The very basic idea in
[
8
,
9
]
is to look for an identity like (
4.2
); to proceed step by step we first consider the simplest possible case and assume that the matrix
\mathbf{B}
in (
1.6
) is positive definite (and henceforth invertible) and that the boundary is not characteristic, which means that all eigenvalues of the matrix
\mathbf{E}^{-1}\mathbf{A}
in (
1.6
) are bounded away from
0
.
To fix the notation, we term
n
the number of strictly negative eigenvalues, and hence
N-n
is the number of strictly positive eigenvalues. Since the matrix
\mathbf{B}
is positive definite, we can impose a full boundary condition on the solution of (
1.6
). This is consistent with §
2.2
and Remark
2.1
since in this case
h=\ell=0
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}_{b})=\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}_{b}
. Wrapping up, the boundary condition in (
4.3
