) boils down to the requirement that there is a
boundary layer

\mathbf{w}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
such that (recall (
3.1
))

(4.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}+\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\\
\mathbf{w}(0)=\mathbf{u}_{b},\quad\lim_{y\to+\infty}\mathbf{w}(y)=\mathbf{\bar{u}},\end{array}\right.

where
\mathbf{u}_{b}=\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{v}_{b})
and the trace
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{v}(t,0))
does not depend on time since the solution we construct is self-similar. To study (
4.4
), we employ the standard Stable Manifold Theorem, which dictates that (
4.4
) has a solution if and only if
\mathbf{u}_{b}
lies on the so-called
stable manifold
computed at the point
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
. A by-now classical lemma (see for instance
[
5
]
or also
[
9
, Lemma 10.2]
) states that the dimension of the stable manifold is
n
. In the following, we term
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},\cdot)
a map parameterizing the stable manifold. To solve the boundary Riemann problem we then replace (
4.2
) with the identity

(4.5)

\mathbf{u}_{b}=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\cdot,\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})\circ\mathbf{t}_{n+1}(\cdot,s_{n+1})\dots\circ\mathbf{t}_{N}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s_{N}),

and by relying again on
[
9
, Lemma 10.2]
we conclude that this uniquely determines the values
(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n},s_{n+1},\dots,s_{N})
. Once these values are known, we set
\mathbf{\bar{u}}:=\mathbf{t}_{n+1}(\cdot,s_{n+1})\dots\circ\mathbf{t}_{N}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s_{N})
and point out that, by construction,
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
can be connected to
\mathbf{u}^{+}
by rarefaction waves, shocks or contact discontinuities with
positive
speed, and to
\mathbf{u_{b}}
by a boundary layer satisfying (
4.4
).

We now move forward and consider the case where the matrix
\mathbf{B}
is still invertible, but the boundary is characteristic, namely one eigenvalue of
\mathbf{E}^{-1}\mathbf{A}
can attain the value
0
. As in (
2.7
), we assume that the vanishing eigenvalue is the
k
-th. In this case, the basic idea of the construction is still that the trace
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
must be connected to
\mathbf{u}_{b}
by a boundary layer, but the analysis of (
4.4
) is much more delicate. Loosely speaking, this is first of all due to the fact that by linearizing at the point
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
one obtains a non-trivial center space. In other words, in general there are solutions of the equation at the first line of (
4.4
) that lie on the stable manifold and hence converge exponentially fast, but there might also be converging solutions lying on a center manifold, and hence having a slower decay at infinity. Also, there might be shock or rarefaction waves (if the
k
-th vector field is genuinely nonlinear) or contact discontinuities (if the
k
-th vector field is linearly degenerate) of the
k
-th family with non-negative speed, which should be taken into account. To handle these challenges, we proceed as follows.

First, we construct the so-called
characteristic wave fan curve of admissible states

\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}
. In §
4.3
we assume that the
k
-th vector field is genuinely nonlinear: in this case, the curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
describes the states that can be connected to
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
by a shock or a rarefaction with
non-negative
speed close to
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
, and by boundary layers (that is, solutions of (
3.1
)) lying on the
6
6

6

The center manifold obtained by linearizing a given system at a given point is in general not unique, but me can arbitrarily fix one
center manifold. In §
4.4
we assume that the
k
-th vector field is linearly degenerate: the curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
describes the states that can be connected to
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
by a contact discontinuity with
non-negative
speed equal to
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
, or by boundary layers lying on the center manifold.

In §
4.5
we then complete the analysis by considering
all
boundary layers, not only those lying on the center manifold. From the technical standpoint, the main tool we use is the Slaving Manifold Theorem, a classical result in dynamical systems theory, see
[
26
]
for an extended discussion and
[
9
, §11]
for a very brief exposition oriented towards the applications we need in the following. We now provide a very handwaving exposition of the main ideas underpinning the construction of the slaving manifold, and we refer to
[
9
, §11]
for the precise statements. Fix an autonomous systems of ODEs
7
7

7

Note that we are using a different notation compared to
[
9
, §11]
and here we denote by
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\mathbf{a}
what in
[
9
]
is termed
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{g}
, respectively.
like (
3.2
)
an equilibrium point
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast}
,
\mathbf{a}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast})=\mathbf{0}
, and an orbit
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}
of (
3.2
) lying on a center manifold and confined in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast}
. Very loosely speaking, the Slaving Manifold Theorem characterizes the orbits of (
3.2
) that converge exponentially fast to
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}
at
+\infty
. Also, it provides a description of these orbits: each of them is given by
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}
, plus an orbit lying on the so-called uniformly stable manifold (whose dimension is the same as the number of eiegenvalues of
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{a}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast})
with strictly negative real part) and a “perturbation term”. The “perturbation term” (which could also be called “interaction term”) is due to the nonlinearity of
\mathbf{a}
and it is small, in a suitable quantified sense, with respect to the previous two, see
[
9
, eq. (11.6)]
.
Note that in the applications to boundary layers discussed in the following the orbit
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}
is obtained from the curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}
, and can boil down to a single equilibrium point in some cases
8
8

8

We refer to the comments after (
4.44
) below for some further details about the instances where the orbit
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}
boils down to an equilibrium point.
. At the end of the day, by applying the Slaving Manifold Lemma we arrive at the formula

(4.6)

{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\cdot,\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k})\circ\mathbf{t}_{k+1}(\cdot,s_{k+1}),\dots,t_{N}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s_{N})=\mathbf{u}_{b},

which is the analogous of (
4.2
) and (
4.5
). In the above expression,

{\boldsymbol{\phi}}
is a suitable function whose precise expression is provided in §
4.5
, see in particular formula (
4.49
). One can then show that the identity (
4.6
) uniquely determines the values of
(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k},\dots,s_{N})
and from them one reconstructs the solution of the boundary Riemann problem.

To complete our overview, we are left to add one last item to the picture, namely the fact that the matrix
\mathbf{B}
in (
1.6
) is in general not invertible. As pointed out in §
2.2
, this first of all requires a more complicated formulation of the boundary condition, which is expressed by the function
\boldsymbol{\beta}
introduced in there. Note that this also applies to the boundary layers, so the system we have to consider is now

(4.7)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}+\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{\beta}(\mathbf{w}(0),\mathbf{u}_{b})=\mathbf{0}_{N},\quad\lim_{y\to+\infty}\mathbf{w}(y)=\mathbf{\underline{u}},\end{array}\right.

rather than (
4.4
). In the above system, due to the fact that we are considering the boundary characteristic case the asymptotic state
\mathbf{\underline{u}}
is in general different from the trace
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{u}(t,0)
. Note that Definition
1.1
dictates the relation between the asymptotic state

\mathbf{\underline{u}}
and
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
: either they coincide or there is a
0
-speed Lax admissible shock (if the
k
-th vector field is genuinely nonlinear) or
0
-speed contact discontinuity (if the
k
-th vector field is linearly degenerate) connecting
\mathbf{\underline{u}}
(on the left) and
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
(on the right).

To write down the analogous of (
4.2
),(
4.5
) and (
4.6
) it is convenient to split between
cases A)
and
C)
in Hypothesis
5
on one side and
case B)
on the other side.
In cases A) and C) we impose

(4.8)

\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\cdot,\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k})\circ\mathbf{t}_{k+1}(\cdot,s_{k+1}),\dots,t_{N}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s_{N}),\mathbf{u}_{b})=\mathbf{0}_{N}.

In the above expression,
\ell
denotes the number of negative eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}_{11}
in
case A)
, and
\ell=h
(the dimension of the kernel of
\mathbf{B}
) in
case C)
. Note that this yields that the dimension of the vector
(\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k},\dots,s_{N})
is exactly the number of boundary conditions imposed through the function
\boldsymbol{\beta}
given by (
2.16
) and (
2.17
), respectively, see Remark
2.1
. The analysis in
[
9
]
dicates that (
4.8
) uniquely determines the values of
(\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k},\dots,s_{N})
and henceforth the solution of the boundary Riemann problem. We now consider
case B)
in Hypothesis
5
: in this case the number of non-positive eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}_{11}

depends
on
\mathbf{u}_{b}
, and is denoted by
\ell(\mathbf{u}_{b})
. For instance, in the case of the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equations written in Eulerian coordinates this number depends on the sign of the fluid velocity, which is the second component of
\mathbf{u}_{b}
. In place of (
4.8
) we have then

(4.9)

\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\cdot,\xi_{\ell(\mathbf{u}_{b})+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k})\circ\mathbf{t}_{k+1}(\cdot,s_{k+1}),\dots,t_{N}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s_{N}),\mathbf{u}_{b})=\mathbf{0}_{N},

and as a consequence of the analysis in
[
9
]
we have that the above identity
uniquely determines the values of
(\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k},\dots,s_{N})
and henceforth the solution of the boundary Riemann problem.

4.2.
The Lax wave fan curves of admissible states

The key point in Lax’s pioneering paper
[
28
]
on the solution of the Riemann problem is the construction of the so-called
i
-th wave fan curve of admissible states
, which we denote by
\mathbf{t}_{i}
. We now briefly recall its definition and fix some notation. We first consider the genuinely nonlinear case, next the linearly degenerate case.

4.2.1.
Genuinely nonlinear vector field

Fix a state
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
and
i=1,\dots,N
and assume that the
i
-th vector field is genuinely nonlinear (
2.8
). We term
\mathbf{i}_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
the rarefaction wave curve, which is the integral curve passing through
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
of the vector field
\mathbf{r}_{i}
, namely the solution of the Cauchy problem

(4.10)

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial\mathbf{i}_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)}{\partial s}=\mathbf{r}_{i}(\mathbf{i}_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))}\qquad\mathbf{i}_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},0)=\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}.

We also term
\mathbf{h}_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
the curve of the states that can be connected to
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
by a (non necessarily Lax admissible) shock with speed close to
\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
, that is the set of points such that the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions

(4.11)

\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{h}_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))-\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})=\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)[\mathbf{g}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{h}_{i})-\mathbf{g}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})]

hold for some
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
close to
\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
. In the previous expression,
\mathbf{v}
denotes the change of variables which allows to pass from (
1.6
) to (
1.2
). The analysis in
[
28
]
dictates that the
i
-th
wave fan curve of admissible states
is defined by setting
9
9

9

We recall footnote
5
on page
5
and explicitely remark that, since we are defining the wave fan curve of admissible
left
states, the
negative
values of
s
correspond to rarefactions, and the
positive
to shocks.

(4.12)

\mathbf{t}_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{i}_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)&s<0\\
\mathbf{h}_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)&s>0\\
\end{array}\right.

and comprises all the states
\mathbf{u}^{-}
(on the left) that can be connected to
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
on the right by either a Lax admissible shock (if
s>0
) or by a rarefaction (if
s<0
).

Since we will need it in the following, we introduce some further notation: we recall that
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
denotes the speed of the shock connecting
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
with
\mathbf{h}_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
, that is it satisfies (
4.11
).
If the
k
-th characteristic field is genuinely nonlinear (
2.8
) the map
s\mapsto\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
is strictly monotone and henceforth invertible.
We term
\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
the unique value satisfying

(4.13)

\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}))=0.

Note that, by the Implicit Function Theorem we have that the map
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}\mapsto\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
is Lipschitz continuous, namely

(4.14)

|\underline{s}(\mathbf{u}_{1})-\underline{s}(\mathbf{u}_{2})|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{2}|.

Owing again to genuine nonlinearity, the map
s\mapsto\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{i}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))
is also strictly monotone, we term
\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
the unique value satisfying

(4.15)

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{i}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}))=0.

4.2.2.
Linearly degenerate vector field

If the
i
-th vector field is linearly degenerate we set

(4.16)

\mathbf{t}_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)=:\mathbf{i}_{i}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s),

where
\mathbf{i}
is the same as in (
4.10
).

4.3.
The characteristic wave fan curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}
in the genuinely nonlinear case

To ease the exposition we assume (
2.11
), that is case A) in Hypothesis
5
(the analysis in the other cases is similar). Note this covers the case of the inviscid limit of the Navier-Stokes equations written in Eulerian coordinates when the fluid velocity is close to
\pm c
, where
c
denotes the sound speed, see §
2.3.1
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Assume (
2.8
) and let
\mathbf{u}^{\ast}
be the same as in (
2.7
). There is a sufficiently small constant
\nu>0
10
10

10

The constant
\nu
only depends on the functions
\mathbf{E}
,
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{B}
,
\mathbf{G}
, and on the value
\mathbf{u}^{\ast}
such that if
|\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}-\mathbf{u}^{\ast}|\leq\nu
, then there is curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot):]-\nu,\nu[\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
satisfying the following properties:

1.

\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},0)=\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
.

2.

For every
s\in]-\nu,\nu[
one (and only one) of the following conditions holds true:

i)

\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
(on the right) and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
(on the left) are connected by a Lax admissible shock with nonnegative speed

ii)

there is a value
\bar{\mathbf{u}}
(possibly coinciding with either
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
or
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
) such that

ii
1
)

\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
(on the right) and

\bar{\mathbf{u}}
(on the left) are connected by a rarefaction wave with nonnegative speed

ii
2
)

there is a
boundary layer

\mathbf{w}:[0,+\infty[\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
such that

(4.17)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}+\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\\
\boldsymbol{\beta}(\mathbf{w}(0),\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))=\mathbf{0},\quad\lim_{x\to+\infty}\mathbf{w}(x)=\bar{\mathbf{u}}.\end{array}\right.

3.

The function
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to both variables. It is also differentiable with respect to
s
at
s=0
and satisfies

(4.18)

\left.\frac{d\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)}{ds}\right|_{s=0}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})&\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0\\
\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},0)&\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0.\\
\end{array}\right.

Note that in point ii) if
\bar{\mathbf{u}}
coincides with
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
then condition ii
{}_{1})
becomes trivial, if
\bar{\mathbf{u}}
coincides with
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
then condition ii
{}_{2})
becomes trivial. A more technical observation is provided in Remark
4.1
.

Remark 4.1
.

One might wonder why in item ii)
1
in the statement of Lemma
4.1
one does not take into account the possibility that
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
(on the right) and
\bar{\mathbf{u}}
(on the left) are connected by a Lax admissible shock with nonnegative speed: this is due to the genuine nonlinearity assumption. Indeed, assume by contradiction that
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
(on the right) and
\bar{\mathbf{u}}
(on the left) are connected by a Lax admissible shock with nonnegative speed; then
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})>0
and hence there is no nontrivial orbit satisfying (
4.17
) and lying on a center manifold.

We now provide the proof of Lemma
4.1
by constructing
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}
.
The exposition is organized as follows: in §
4.3.1
we define the function
\mathbf{b}_{k}
describing the point connected to
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
by a boundary layer, and describe some of its properties. In §
4.3.2
we define the function
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}
and discuss the heuristic ideas underpinning the definition. In §
4.3.3
we show that
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}
satisfies the properties in the statement of Lemma
4.1
.

4.3.1.
The boundary layer function
\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)

We consider the Cauchy problem obtained by coupling

(4.19)

\displaystyle{\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{d\tau}=\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},z_{c})}\qquad\displaystyle{\frac{dz_{c}}{d\tau}=\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u},z_{c})}

with the initial datum

(4.20)

\mathbf{u}(\tau=0)=\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\qquad\qquad z_{c}(\tau=0)=0

where
\mathbf{r}_{c}
is the same vector as in (
3.12
). Note that (
4.19
) is formally obtained from (
3.10
) by dividing by
z_{c}
.
We define
\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
by setting

(4.21)

\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s):=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{u}\\
z_{c}\mathbf{p}_{c}(\mathbf{u},z_{c})\\
\end{array}\right)(\tau=s),

where
\mathbf{p}_{c}
is the same vector as in Lemma
3.3
and
(\mathbf{u},z_{c})
is the solution of (
4.19
),(
4.20
) at
\tau=s
. Note that the function
\mathbf{b}_{k}
attains values in
\mathbb{R}^{2N-h}
and lies on the same center manifold

\mathcal{M}^{c}
as in Lemma
3.3
.

Lemma 4.2
.

The function
\mathbf{b}_{k}
is of class
C^{2}
with respect to both
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
and
s
. Also,

(4.22)

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})=0\implies\left.\frac{\partial\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)}{\partial s}\right|_{s=0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\\
\mathbf{0}_{N-h}\\
\end{array}\right),

where
\mathbf{r}_{k}
is an eigenvector of
\mathbf{E}^{-1}\mathbf{A}
associated to
\lambda_{k}
.

Proof.

The regularity of
\mathbf{b}_{k}
follows from the classical theory of ODEs. The implication (
4.22
) follows from Lemma
3.4
and the equalities

\partial z_{c}(s=0)/\partial s=\theta_{c}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},0)
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Assume that
\mathbf{u}
and
z_{c}
solve the Cauchy problem (
4.19
),(
4.20
). There is a sufficiently small constant
\nu>0
such that if
|\mathbf{u}(s)-\mathbf{u}^{\ast}|<\nu
and
|z_{c}(s)|<\nu
then

(4.23)

\left.\frac{d\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}(\tau))}{d\tau}\right|_{\tau=s}\geq\frac{d}{2}>0,

and

(4.24)

\left.\frac{d\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u}(\tau),z_{c}(\tau))}{d\tau}\right|_{\tau=s}\geq\frac{d}{2a_{1}}>0,

provided
d
is the same constant as in (
2.8
) and
a_{1}
is an upper bound for the function
a(\mathbf{u})
in the statement of Lemma
3.4
.

Proof.

To establish (
4.23
) we point out that

\begin{split}\left.\frac{d\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}(\tau))}{d\tau}\right|_{\tau=s}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:rescala}}}{{=}}\nabla\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}(s))\cdot\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u}(s),z_{c}(s))=\nabla\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},0)+\mathcal{O}(1)|\mathbf{u}(s)-\mathbf{u}^{\ast}|+\mathcal{O}(1)|z_{c}(s)|\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:uast},\,\text{Lemma~\ref {l:lambdac}}}}{{=}}\nabla\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})+\mathcal{O}(1)|\mathbf{u}(s)-\mathbf{u}^{\ast}|+\mathcal{O}(1)|z_{c}(s)|\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gnl}}}{{\geq}}d+\mathcal{O}(1)|\mathbf{u}(s)-\mathbf{u}^{\ast}|+\mathcal{O}(1)|z_{c}(s)|\geq\frac{d}{2},\end{split}

provided the constant
\nu
is sufficiently small. We now establish (
4.24
):

\begin{split}&\left.\frac{d\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u}(\tau),z_{c}(\tau))}{d\tau}\right|_{\tau=s}=\nabla_{\mathbf{u}}\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u}(\tau),z_{c}(\tau))\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{d\tau}+\frac{\partial\theta_{c}}{\partial z_{c}}\frac{dz_{c}}{d\tau}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:rescala}}}{{=}}\nabla_{\mathbf{u}}\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u}(\tau),0)\cdot\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)+\mathcal{O}(1)|z_{c}(s)|+\mathcal{O}(1)|\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u}(\tau),z_{c}(\tau))|\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:thetaczero}}}{{=}}a(\mathbf{u})^{-1}\nabla\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)+\mathcal{O}(1)|\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u},0)|+\mathcal{O}(1)|z_{c}(s)|+\mathcal{O}(1)|\mathbf{u}(s)-\mathbf{u}^{\ast}|\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{Lemma}~\ref {l:lambdac}}}{{\geq}}\frac{1}{a_{1}}\nabla\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{\ast})+a_{1}|z_{c}(s)|+\mathcal{O}(1)|\mathbf{u}(s)-\mathbf{u}^{\ast}|\geq\frac{d}{2a_{1}}.\qed\end{split}

Lemma 4.4
.

Assume
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0
and that
z_{c}
is the same as in (
4.19
); then there there is
\underline{s}>0
, depending on
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
, such that

(4.25)

z_{c}(\tau=\underline{s})=0.

Also, there is a
0
-speed traveling wave
\mathbf{w}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
satisfying

(4.26)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}+\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\\
\lim_{x\to-\infty}\mathbf{w}(x)=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\underline{s}),\quad\lim_{x\to+\infty}\mathbf{w}(x)=\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\end{array}\right.

where
\mathbf{b}_{k}
is as in (
4.21
).
If
s<\underline{s}
then there is a boundary layer
\mathbf{w}:[0,+\infty[\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
satisfying (
4.17
) with
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}},s)
, where the projection
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}
is the same as in (
3.4
), and
\bar{\mathbf{u}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}.

Proof.

We proceed according to the following steps.

Step 1:
we show the existence of
\underline{s}>0
satisfying (
4.25
).
First, we recall that
z_{c}(\tau=0)=0
and point out that

(4.27)

\left.\frac{dz_{c}(\tau)}{d\tau}\right|_{\tau=0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:rescala},\eqref{e:id}}}{{=}}\theta_{c}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:thetaczero}}}{{=}}a^{-1}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0.

and hence
z_{c}(\tau)<0
for
\tau>0
sufficiently small.
On the other hand,

(4.28)

z_{c}(\tau)=\int_{0}^{\tau}\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u}(\xi),z_{c}(\xi))d\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:giallo}}}{{\geq}}\underbrace{\theta_{c}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},0)}_{=a(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0}\tau+\frac{d}{4a_{1}}\tau^{2},

where as before
a_{1}
is an upper bound for the function
a(\mathbf{u})
in the statement of Lemma
3.4
. We can then conclude that
z_{c}
attains the value
0
for some
\underline{s}>0
.

Step 2:
we fix
s<\underline{s}
and exhibit a boundary layer
\mathbf{w}:[0,+\infty[\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
satisfying (
4.17
) with
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}},s)
, and
\bar{\mathbf{u}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}.
Just to fix the ideas we assume
s>0
(the proof in the case
s<0
is analogous). Owing to (
4.27
) and by definition (
4.25
) of
\underline{s}
we have
z_{c}(\tau)<0
for every
\tau\in]0,s]
.
We consider the Cauchy problem

(4.29)

\displaystyle{\frac{dx_{c}}{d\tau}=\frac{1}{z_{c}(\tau)}}\qquad x_{c}(s)=0.

Owing to (
4.28
),

(4.30)

\lim_{\tau\to 0^{+}}x_{c}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:caux}}}{{=}}\lim_{\tau\to 0^{+}}\left(-\int_{\tau}^{s}\frac{1}{z_{c}(\xi)}d\xi\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:esplode}}}{{\geq}}\lim_{\tau\to 0^{+}}\left(-\int_{\tau}^{s}\frac{1}{\theta_{c}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},0)\tau}d\xi\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta_{c}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},0)<0}}{{=}}+\infty

and hence
x_{c}
is an invertible map from
]0,s]
onto
[0,+\infty[
. We term
x_{c}^{-1}
its inverse and we consider the maps
\mathbf{u}(x):=\mathbf{u}(x_{c}^{-1}(x))
,

z_{c}(x):=z_{c}(x_{c}^{-1}(x))
. By combining (
4.19
) and (
4.29
) we conclude that
(\mathbf{u},z_{c})
is a solution of (
3.10
) and hence
by taking
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{u}
we obtain a solution of the equation at the first line of (
4.17
). By recalling (
4.30
) and (
4.20
)
we get
\mathbf{w}(0)=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}},s)
and
\lim_{x\to+\infty}\mathbf{w}(y)=\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}
.

Step 3:
we conclude the proof of the lemma by considering the case
s=\underline{s}
.
We consider the Cauchy problem obtained by coupling the same ODE as in (
4.29
) with the condition
x_{c}(\underline{s}/2)=0
. By arguing as in (
4.30
) we get that
\lim_{\tau\to 0^{+}}x_{c}(\tau)=+\infty.
Owing to (
4.24
) the function
\tau\mapsto\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u}(\tau),z_{c}(\tau))
is monotone increasing and, since
z_{c}(\underline{s})=0
, then

z_{c}(\tau)\geq\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u}(\underline{s}),z_{c}(\underline{s}))(\tau-\underline{s})\quad\text{for every $\tau\in[0,\underline{s}]$.}

Note furthermore that
\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u}(\underline{s}),z_{c}(\underline{s}))>0
. We conclude that

\lim_{\tau\to\underline{s}^{-}}x_{c}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:caux}}}{{=}}\lim_{\tau\to\underline{s}^{-}}\int_{\underline{s}/2}^{\tau}\frac{1}{z_{c}(\xi)}d\xi\leq\lim_{\tau\to\underline{s}^{-}}\int_{\underline{s}/2}^{\tau}\frac{1}{\theta_{c}(\mathbf{u}(\underline{s}),z_{c}(\underline{s}))(\tau-\underline{s})}d\xi=-\infty.

By arguing as before we establish (
4.26
).
∎

We recall that the value
\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
is defined by (
4.13
).
Note that, owing to (
4.26
), there is a Lax admissible 0-speed shock connecting

\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
(on the right) with
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\underline{s})
(on the left). This implies that
we can choose the parametrization of the curve
\mathbf{t}_{k}
in such a way that
\underline{s}=\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
and that

(4.31)

(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})),\mathbf{0}_{N-h})=\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}))\quad\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\leq 0$}.

We also have the following result.

Lemma 4.5
.

Assume
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})=0
, then for every
s<0
there is a boundary layer
\mathbf{w}:[0,+\infty[\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
satisfying (
4.17
) with
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}},s)
and
\bar{\mathbf{u}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}.

Proof.

Owing to (
3.14
) we have
\theta_{c}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},0)=0
. We now consider the Cauchy problem (
4.19
),(
4.20
) and by arguing as in (
4.28
) we conclude that

z_{c}(s)>0
for
s<0
. We then consider the Cauchy problem (
4.29
) and by arguing as in
Step 2
in the proof of Lemma
4.4
we get the desired result.
∎

4.3.2.
Definition of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)

We now provide the definition of the curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
by distinguishing the cases
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0
and

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0
.

Case
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0
We recall that
\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
and
\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
are defined by (
4.13
) and (
4.15
), respectively. If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0
then owing to genuine nonlinearity (
2.8
) we have
\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\leq 0
. We then set

(4.32)

\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)&s\geq\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\\
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})),s-\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}))&s\leq\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\\
\end{array}\right.\quad\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0$}.

Note that

(4.33)

\left.\frac{\partial\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))}{\partial s}\right|_{s=\bar{s}(\widetilde{u})}=\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:baresse},\eqref{e:siattacca}}}{{=}}\left.\frac{\partial[\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})),s-\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}))]}{\partial s}\right|_{s=\bar{s}(\widetilde{u})}

and hence the curve is of class
C^{1,1}
in this case.
Very loosely speaking, the basic idea underpinning definition (
4.32
) is the following: if
s>0
,
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
(on the right) and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
(on the left) are connected by a Lax admissible shock that has positive speed since
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0
. For
s<0
,
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
“follows”
\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
as long as the latter describes a rarefaction with positive speed. When the speed would became negative,
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
“switches” to the boundary layers curve.

Case
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0
.
If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0
then owing again to genuine nonlinearity (
2.8
) we have
\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})>0
. We recall (
4.31
)
and set

(4.34)

\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)&s\leq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\\
\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)&s\geq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\\
\end{array}\right.\quad\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0$}.

Note that in this case the curve is of class
C^{1,1}
away from the point
s=\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
, where the first derivative may be discontinuous.
In a nutshell, the very basic idea behind definition (
4.34
) is that the values
s<\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
correspond to boundary layers, whereas the values
s>\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
correspond to Lax admissible shocks with non-negative speed.

4.3.3.
Proof of Lemma
4.1

We now show that the curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
defined at the previous paragraph satisfies the properties in the statement of Lemma
4.1
.

We first consider the case
\lambda_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})\geq 0
, which yields (
4.32
) and
\bar{s}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})<0
, and this implies that item i) in the statement of Lemma
4.1
is satisfied. If
\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\leq s<0
, then we set
\mathbf{\bar{u}}:=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
and item ii) in the statement of Lemma
4.1
is satisfied with item ii
{}_{2})
trivially satisfied by the constant function
\mathbf{w}\equiv\mathbf{\bar{u}}
. The most interesting behavior occurs when
s<\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
. In this case we set

\mathbf{\bar{u}}:=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}))
, and in this way item ii
{}_{1})
is satisfied. To establish ii
{}_{2})
one can then rely on the change of variables (
4.29
),(
4.30
).

We now assume
\lambda_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})<0
. We recall that
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
is defined by (
4.34
) and that in this case
\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})>0
. If
s\geq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})>0
then item ii) is satisfied provided
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
and
\mathbf{w}\equiv\mathbf{\bar{u}}
in item ii)
2
. If
s<\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
, then
we set
\mathbf{\bar{u}}:=\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
. In this way item ii
{}_{1})
is trivially satisfied, whereas item ii
{}_{2})
is satisfied by Lemma
4.4
.

4.4.
Boundary layers analysis in the linearly degenerate case

We assume (
2.9
) and to ease the exposition in the following we directly focus on the most interesting case, that is B) in Hypothesis (
5
).
This is the case of the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equations written in Eulerian coordinates when the fluid velocity vanishes. Note that another interesting case involving a linearly degenerate field is the case of the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equations written in Lagrangian coordinates. However, in that case
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u})\equiv 0
and the analysis trivializes: there are no boundary layers lying on the center manifold or
k
-th waves entering the domain and we set

\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s):=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s).

Finally, we mention the case of the viscous MHD equations when
u=\pm\beta\sqrt{\rho}
: in this case the boundary characteristic field is also linearly degenerate and we have (
2.11
). This case can be handled by combining the analysis at the previous paragraph with the proof of
[
9
, Lemma 4.6]
.

The detailed construction of the curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}
in case B) of Hypothesis
5
is provided in
[
9
]
, see in particular
[
9
, Lemma 4.6]
for the construction of the characteristic wave fan curve. Since we need it in what follows, we now briefly overview
[
9
, §4.2]
and in particular Lemma 4.6. Note that

(4.35)

\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)&\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0\\
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)&\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0,\\
\end{array}\right.

where
\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
is the same as in (
4.16
) and
\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
is defined in
[
9
, (4.4)]
and is constructed from the solution, evaluated at
\tau=s
of the Cauchy problem

(4.36)

\frac{d\mathbf{u}}{d\tau}=\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},z_{00},0),\quad\frac{dz_{00}}{d\tau}=\theta_{00}(\mathbf{u},z_{00},0),\quad\mathbf{u}(0)=\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\quad z_{00}(0)=0,

namely
\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)=(\mathbf{u}(s),z_{00}(s)
.
In the above expression, we have set

\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},z_{00},\sigma):=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-e_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{d}^{t}\mathbf{r}_{00}\\
\mathbf{R}_{0}\mathbf{r}_{00}\\
\end{array}\right)(\mathbf{u},z_{00},\sigma).

and the functions
e_{11}
,
\mathbf{d}
,
\mathbf{r}_{00}
,
\theta_{00}
are as in
[
9
, Lemma 4.2]
. Note that, owing to
[
9
, Lemmas 4.4, 4.5]
we have

(4.37)

\theta_{00}(\mathbf{u},0,\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}))=0,\qquad\mathbf{r}_{c}(\mathbf{u},0,\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}))=\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{u}).

Very loosely speaking, the rationale underpinning definition (
4.35
) is the following. If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0
then there is a contact discontinuity with nonnegative speed joining
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
. If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0

then by
[
9
, Lemma 4.6 Aii)]
there is a boundary layer connecting
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)
, i.e. there is a solution of (
3.1
) such that

(4.38)

\mathbf{u}(0)=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s),\qquad\lim_{y\to+\infty}\mathbf{u}(y)=\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}.

4.5.
Complete boundary layers analysis

The curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}
we discuss at the previous paragraphs describes, among other things, the boundary layers (that is, solutions of (
3.1
) with finite limit at
+\infty
) lying on the center manifold.
We now describe
all
the boundary layers, not necessarily lying on the center manifold, and we refer to §
4.1
for the main ideas involved on the construction, which relies on the Slaving Manifold Lemma. Here instead we provide the technical details of the construction and to ease the exposition, we assume (
2.11
). Case B) in Hypothesis
5
is discussed in
[
9
]
, whereas the case of
2.14
is discussed in
[
8
]
and is analogous to what follows.

To complete the boundary layer analysis we recall the analysis in
[
9
, §11]
and apply it to the case where the function
\mathbf{a}
in the ODE (
3.2
) is
11
11

11

Concerning the notation we recall the footnote
7
at page
7
is defined by (
3.3
), and hence (
3.2
) is equivalent to the boundary layers equation (
3.1
). We then proceed as follows.

•

We apply
[
9
, Lemma 11.1]
to (
3.2
) in the case where the function
\mathbf{a}
is given by (
3.3
), and in this case one can show (see
[
8
, Lemma 4.7]
) that
n_{-}=k-1-\ell
. We recall that
n_{-}
denotes the number of eigenvalues of
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{a}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast})
with strictly negative real part, and
\ell
is the number of negative eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}_{11}
. We also define the equilibrium points
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast}
and
\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}}
by setting

(4.39)

\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast}:=(\mathbf{u}^{\ast},\mathbf{0}_{N-h}),\qquad\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}}:=(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s_{k}),\mathbf{0}_{N-h}),

where
\mathbf{u}^{\ast}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}
are the same as in the statement of Lemma
4.1
. Lemma 11 in
[
9
]
yields the existence of an
(k-1-\ell)
-dimensional invariant manifold, the so-called uniformly stable manifold, which is parametrized by the function
\mathbf{m}_{-}(\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}},\cdot)
. The solutions of (
3.2
) lying on the uniformly stable manifold converge exponentially fast to
\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}}=(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s_{k}),\mathbf{0}_{N-h})
.
The Lipschitz continuous function
\mathbf{m}_{-}
attains values in
\mathbb{R}^{2N-h}
: we set

(4.40)

\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s_{k}),\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1}):=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{m}_{-}(\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1})

where
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}
denotes the projection onto the first
N
components.

•

We now have to introduce some notation and we do so by first considering the genuinely nonlinear (
2.8
) case. We recall the definition of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
, namely (
4.32
) and (
4.34
), and conclude that if either
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0
and
s\geq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
or
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0
and
s\geq\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
then there is no boundary layer lying on the center manifold and therefore the function
\mathbf{m}_{-}
describes all the boundary layers.
If either
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0
and
s<\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
or
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0
and
s<\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
then
there are boundary layers lying on the center manifold. To complete our construction we first
introduce the function
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
by setting

(4.41)

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s_{k}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s_{k})&\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0$ and $s_{k}<\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})$ }\\
(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s_{k}),\mathbf{0}_{N-h})&\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0$ and $s_{k}\geq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})$ }\\
\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})),s_{k}-\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}))&\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0$ and $s_{k}<\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})$}\\
(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s_{k}),\mathbf{0}_{N-h})&\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0$ and $s_{k}\geq\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})$}.\\
\end{array}\right.\quad

Note that, owing to (
4.22
), if
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0
then the curve
\gamma_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
is of class
C^{1,1}
.
Note furthermore that
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},0)=(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\mathbf{0}_{N-h})
for every
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
, and

(4.42)

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s_{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:uguale1},\eqref{e:regcwfc}}}{{=}}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s_{k}).

•

We now consider the linearly degenerate (
2.9
) case and we recall the definition of the curve
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},)
, namely (
4.35
). If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0
, then there is no boundary layer lying on the center manifold, otherwise there is. In this case we set

(4.43)

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s_{k}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s_{k})&\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0$}\\
(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\xi_{k}),\mathbf{0}_{N-h})&\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0$}.\\
\end{array}\right.

•

We are now in a position to complete our analysis and provide a description of
all
boundary layers, not necessarily lying on either the center manifold or the uniformly stable manifold.
We rely on a Slaving Manifold Theorem. More precisely, in both the linearly degenerate and in the genuinely nonlinear case we apply
[
9
, Lemma 11.2]
with
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\ast}
and
\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}}
defined as in (
4.39
), and

(4.44)

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0):=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s_{k}).

Note that owing to (
4.43
)
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0)
is the initial datum for an orbit
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}
of (
3.2
) lying on the center manifold. Note furthermore that by comparing (
4.41
) and (
4.43
) we get the equality
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0))=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}})
. In all cases where we have the stronger equality
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0)=\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}}
(which holds for instance if
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0
and
s_{k}\geq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})
, see again (
4.43
)) the orbit
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}
of (
3.2
) with initial datum
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0)
boils down to a single equilibrium point.
Lemma 11.2 in
[
9
]
yields the existence of a Lipschitz continuous map
\mathbf{m}_{p}(\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0),\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{k-\ell-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{2N-h}
, which has the following property: let
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}
be the solutions of the Cauchy problem obtained by coupling (
3.2
) with the initial datum

(4.45)

\boldsymbol{\omega}(0):=\mathbf{m}_{-}(\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1})-\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}}+\mathbf{m}_{p}(\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0),\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1})+\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0),

and
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0)
, respectively; then

(4.46)

\lim_{y\to+\infty}|\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(y)-\boldsymbol{\omega}(y)|=0.

We postpone to §
4.6.1
and §
4.6.2
below an extended discussion on how this asymptotic behavior translates in the specific framework of the boundary layers system (
3.1
). We set

(4.47)

\boldsymbol{\psi}_{p}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k}):=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{m}_{p}(\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0),\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1}),

where as usual
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}
denotes the projection onto the first
N
coordinates. We also set

(4.48)

{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k}):=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\omega}(0)),

where
\boldsymbol{\omega}(0)
is the same as in (
4.45
). By combining (
4.48
) with (
4.45
), (
4.40
),(
4.47
) and the equality
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0))=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}(\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}})
we arrive at

(4.49)

{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k}):={\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{s}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}},s_{k}),\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1})+\boldsymbol{\psi}_{p}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k}).

Note that, if the
k
-th vector field is genuinely nonlinear, the function
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
describes the states that can be connected to
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
by either Lax admissible shocks or rarefaction waves with non-negative speed, and by boundary layers. If the
k
-th vector field is linearly degenerate, the function
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\cdot)
describes the states that can be connected to
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
by contact discontinuities with non-negative speed, and by boundary layers. Note furthermore that, owing to
[
9
, formula (11.6)]
we have

(4.50)

|\boldsymbol{\psi}_{p}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k})|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}|s_{k}|\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}|\xi_{i}|&\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0$ and $s_{k}<\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})$ }\\
0&\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0$ and $s_{k}\geq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})$ }\\
\left|s_{k}-\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\right|\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}|\xi_{i}|&\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0$ and $s_{k}<\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})$ }\\
0&\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0$ and $s_{k}\geq\bar{s}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})$ }&\end{array}\right.

in the genuinely nonlinear case, and

(4.51)

|\boldsymbol{\psi}_{p}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k})|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}|s_{k}|\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}|\xi_{i}|&\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})<0$ }\\
0&\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})\geq 0$ }\\
\end{array}\right.

in the linearly degenerate case.

4.6.
The solution of the boundary Riemann problem

We now discuss the solution of the Riemann problem posed by coupling (
1.1
) with (
4.3
). As pointed out in §
4.1
, in either
Case A)
or
Case C)
in Hypothesis
5
we have to solve (
4.8
) for
(\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k},\dots,s_{N})
. In
Case B)
in Hypothesis
5
we have to use (
4.9
).

For the reader’s convenience we now go over the main properties of the solution of the boundary Riemann problem that we need in the following and we separately consider the case where the
k
-th vector field is genuinely nonlinear and the case where it is linearly degenerate.

4.6.1.
Genuinely nonlinear
k
-th vector field

To simplify the notation, we assume (
2.11
), that is
case A)
in Hypothesis
5
. As mentioned before, this applies to the inviscid limit of the Navier-Stokes equations written in Eulerian coordinates when the fluid velocity is close to
\pm c
, where
c
denotes the sound speed, see §
2.3.1
.

To discuss the main properties of the solution of (
1.1
), (
4.3
) consider
(\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k},\dots,s_{N})
satisfying (
4.8
) and set

(4.52)

\mathbf{\hat{u}}:=\mathbf{t}_{k+1}(\cdot,s_{k+1})\circ\dots\mathbf{t}_{N}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s_{N}),

see Figure
1
for a representation.

Figure 1.
The solution of the boundary Riemann problem
t
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
\mathbf{u}^{+}

Note that
\mathbf{u}^{+}
and
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
are connected by waves (shocks, rarefactions, contact discontinuities) of the families
k+1,\dots,N
with strictly positive and bounded away from
0
speed.
To complete the description of the solution of the boundary Riemann solver we now consider the characteristic wave fan curve of admissible states
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\cdot)
, whose construction is given in §
4.3
. Note that here compared with the notation in §
4.3
we have
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
and
s_{k}
in place of
\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}
and
s
, respectively. We distinguish between the following cases.

i)

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\geq 0
and
s_{k}>0
then owing to (
4.32
) we have
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
and the solution of the boundary Riemann problem contains a Lax
admissible shock between
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
(on the right) and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
(on the left).
In this case the trace
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{u}(t,0)
is
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
and satisfies
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})>0
. Note that we have

(4.53)

\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k}),\mathbf{u}_{b})=\mathbf{0}_{N}

provided
\xi_{k}=0
. To establish the above identity, we recall that
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
and that
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})>0
,
s_{k}>0\geq\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
, which owing to (
4.50
) yields

(4.54)

\boldsymbol{\psi}_{p}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k})=\mathbf{0}_{N}

and by (
4.49
) this yields

(4.55)

\begin{split}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}&(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:bl10},\eqref{e:psip}}}{{=}}{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k}),\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1})\\
&={\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:bl10},\eqref{e:psip}}}{{=}}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},0).\end{split}

By combining (
4.8
),(
4.52
) and (
4.55
) we arrive at (
4.53
) with
\xi_{k}=0
.
Note furthermore that (
4.55
) combined with the definition (
4.40
) of
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}
yields the existence of a solution of (
3.1
) with initial datum

{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1})
and converging exponentially fast to
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
. This yields the existence
of a boundary layer
\mathbf{w}
satisfying (
4.7
) with asymptotic state
\mathbf{\underline{u}}=\mathbf{\bar{u}}
.

ii)

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\geq 0
and
\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\leq s_{k}\leq 0
, then again owing to (
4.32
) we have
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
. The solution of the boundary Riemann problem contains a rarefaction wave with rightmost state
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
and leftmost state
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
and the trace
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{u}(t,0)
is
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
and satisfies
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})\geq 0
. By arguing as in case i) we get (
4.53
) with
\xi_{k}=0
and the existence of a boundary layer
\mathbf{w}
satisfying (
4.7
) with asymptotic state
\mathbf{\underline{u}}=\mathbf{\bar{u}}
.

iii)

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\geq 0
and
s_{k}<\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
we recall (
4.32
) and conclude that the solution of the boundary Riemann problem contains a rarefaction wave with rightmost state
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
and leftmost state
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))
. We set
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))
and point out that
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{u}(t,0)
, that is
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
is the trace of the solution of the boundary Riemann problem and satisfies
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})=0
and henceforth
\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})=0=\bar{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})
, by the very definition of
\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
and
\underline{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
, see (
4.15
) and (
4.13
), respectively.
We now recall (
4.49
) and point out that, under the assumptions of case iii),

\begin{split}{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}}&(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k}),\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:uguale1}}}{{=}}{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},s_{k}-\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})),\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:uguale1}}}{{=}}{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},s_{k}-\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})),\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1})\end{split}

Concerning
{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{p}}
, we recall (
4.54
) and that
\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0)
are given by

\boldsymbol{\check{\omega}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:chicosa}}}{{=}}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:uguale1}}}{{=}}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},s_{k}-\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\;\text{and}\;\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:festanatale2}}}{{=}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:chicosa2}}}{{=}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},s_{k}-\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))

respectively. By combining the above equalities with (
4.49
) we arrive at

(4.56)

{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k})={\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k}-\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})).

and by combining (
4.8
) with (
4.52
)this yields (
4.53
) with
\xi_{k}=s_{k}-\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\leq 0=\bar{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})
. We now recall (
4.48
) and that
\boldsymbol{\omega}(0)
in there is the initial point of an orbit of (
3.2
) satisfying (
4.46
),
where
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}
is an orbit of (
3.2
) with initial datum given by (
4.44
). In our case, owing to (
4.41
) the initial datum

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}(0)
is
\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},s_{k}-\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))
and owing to Lemma
4.5
we conclude that
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}
converges to
(\mathbf{\bar{u}},\mathbf{0}_{N-h})
as
y\to+\infty
. Wrapping up, (
4.46
) yields the
existence of a boundary layer
\mathbf{w}
satisfying (
4.7
) with asymptotic state
\mathbf{\underline{u}}=\mathbf{\bar{u}}
.

iv)

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})<0
and
s_{k}>\underline{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
then owing to (
4.34
) we have
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
and the solution of the boundary Riemann problem contains a Lax
admissible shock between
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
(on the right) and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
(on the left).
The trace is
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
, satisfies
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})>0
and by arguing as in case i) we get (
4.53
) with
\xi_{k}=0
and the existence of a boundary layer
\mathbf{w}
satisfying (
4.7
) with asymptotic state
\mathbf{\underline{u}}=\mathbf{\bar{u}}
.

v)

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})<0
and
s_{k}=\underline{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
then owing to (
4.34
) we have
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
. We set
\mathbf{\underline{u}}:=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
and point out that
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
(on the right) and

\mathbf{\underline{u}}
(on the left) are connected by a
0
-speed Lax admissible shock
12
12

12

We explicitely point out that this shock does
not
appear in the solution since it loosely sits at the domain boundary
. The solution of the boundary Riemann problem contains no wave of the
k
-th family and the trace of the solution of the boundary Riemann problem is
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{\hat{u}}
, which satisfies
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})<0
. Since
\mathbf{\hat{u}}=\mathbf{\bar{u}}
by combining (
4.8
) with (
4.52
) we arrive at (
4.53
) with
\xi_{k}=s_{k}=\underline{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
. Note furthermore that owing to (
4.50
) we have (
4.54
), which yields

{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:bl10},\eqref{e:psip}}}{{=}}{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k}),\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1})

and hence, by arguing as in case i) we get the existence of a boundary layer
\mathbf{w}
satisfying (
4.7
) with asymptotic state
\mathbf{\underline{u}}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\underline{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))
.

vi)

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})<0
and
s_{k}<\underline{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
then owing to (
4.34
) the solution of the boundary Riemann problem contains no wave of the
k
-th family. The trace of the solution is
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{\hat{u}}
, satisfies
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})<0
and by recalling (
4.8
) and (
4.52
) we arrive at (
4.53
) with
\xi_{k}=s_{k}
. We also have

\begin{split}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}&(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{\hat{u}}=\mathbf{\bar{u}}}}{{=}}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:bl10}}}{{=}}{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}}(\underbrace{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},s_{k})}_{=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},s_{k})\ \text{by~\eqref{e:regcwfc}}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1})+{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{p}}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1},s_{k}).\end{split}

By arguing as just after formula (
4.56
) and using Lemma
4.4
rather than Lemma
4.5
we conclude that there is a boundary layer
\mathbf{w}
solving (
4.7
) with asymptotic state
\mathbf{\underline{u}}=\mathbf{\hat{u}}=\mathbf{\bar{u}}
.

We now highlight a property of the trace
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{u}(t,0)
of the solution of the boundary Riemann problem that we will use a lot in the following: if the
k
-th vector field is genuinely nonlinear and
\xi_{k}
is the same as in (
4.53
) by direct check on cases i),
\dots
,vi) above one can verify that

(4.57)

\xi_{k}\leq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})\;\text{if}\;\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})\leq 0,\qquad\xi_{k}=0\;\text{if}\;\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}))>0,

whence

(4.58)

\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},\xi_{k})\leq 0\quad\text{if $\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})\leq 0$}.

4.6.2.
Linearly degenerate
k
-th vector field

To ease the exposition, we focus on the most interesting case and consider
case B)
in Hypothesis
5
, which is the case of the inviscid limit of the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equations written in
Eulerian
coordinates when the fluid velocity vanishes, see §
2.3.1
and §
2.3.3
. Note that (
2.14
) occurs for instance in the case of the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equations written in
Lagrangian
coordinates, which is much simpler since the
k
-th vector fields vanishes
identically
.

To discuss the structure of the solution of the boundary Riemann problem we recall (
4.9
) and (
4.52
) and we separately consider the following cases.

•

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})>0
then owing to (
4.35
) we have
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
and the solution of the boundary Riemann problem contains a contact discontinuity with right state
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
and left state
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
. The trace of the solution of the boundary Riemann problem is
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
, which satisfies

(4.59)

\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\bar{u}},\xi_{\ell(\mathbf{u}_{b})+1},\dots,\xi_{k}),\mathbf{u}_{b})=\mathbf{0}_{N},

provided
\xi_{k}=0
. By arguing as in case i) in §
4.6.1
and using (
4.51
) we get the existence of a boundary layer
\mathbf{w}
solving (
4.7
) with asymptotic state
\mathbf{\underline{u}}=\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
.

•

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})=0
then owing to (
4.35
) we have
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
and
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
are connected by a
0
-speed contact discontinuity
13
13

13

As in case v) in §
4.6.1
we explicitely point out that this contact discontinuity does
not
appear in the solution since loosely speaking it sits at the domain boundary
. The solution of the boundary Riemann problem does not contain
any wave of the
k
-th family and its trace is
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{\hat{u}}
. By arguing as in case v) in §
4.6.1
we get (
4.59
) with
\xi_{k}=s_{k}
and the existence of a boundary layer solving (
4.7
) with asymptotic state
\mathbf{\underline{u}}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
.

•

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})<0
then the solution of the boundary Riemann problem does not contain
any wave of the
k
-th family, its trace is
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{\hat{u}}
and this yields (
4.59
) with
\xi_{k}=s_{k}
. By arguing as in case vi) in §
4.6.1
we also get the existence of a boundary layer solving (
4.7
) with asymptotic state
\mathbf{\underline{u}}=\mathbf{\hat{u}}=\mathbf{\bar{u}}
.

Note that by the above analysis, the trace
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{u}(t,0)
of the solution of the boundary Riemann problem satisfies the following property: if
\xi_{k}
is the same as in (
4.59
) then

(4.60)

\xi_{k}=0\quad\text{if}\;\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})>0.

5.
Construction of the wave front-tracking approximation

In this section we describe our wave front-tracking algorithm. In the following sections we will show that this algorithm yields for every
\varepsilon>0
an
\varepsilon
-approximate solution of the initial-boundary value problem (
1.1
), (
1.5
) in the sense of Definition
1.2
, and that the other properties stated in Theorem
1.2
are satisfied. We basically follow the same construction as in
[
10
, §7.2]
and in particular we introduce both
accurate
and
simplified
solvers. The only differences with the analysis in
[
10
, §7.2]
are:

(i)

owing to the specific expression of our interaction functionals in §
7
we have to put particular care in choosing the speed of the wave fronts of the
k
-th family;

(ii)

we have to construct the solution of the boundary Riemann problems that occur at the origin, at discontinuity points of
\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\varepsilon}
and when a wave front hits the boundary.

The exposition is organized as follows: in §
5.1
we briefly discuss the construction of the approximate initial and boundary datum, in §
5.2
we define the accurate and simplified approximate solutions of the Riemann problem, in §
5.3
the accurate approximate solution of the boundary Riemann problem, in §
5.4
the simplified solution of the boundary Riemann problem and finally in §
5.5
we specify when we use the accurate solvers and when we use the simplified solvers. We do not outline the rest of the construction of the wave front-tracking approximation as one can exactly proceed as in
[
10
, §7.2]
.

5.1.
Approximation of the initial datum and of the boundary datum

We fix
\varepsilon>0
, recall (
1.7
) and set
\mathbf{u}_{0}:=\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{v}_{0})
,
\mathbf{u}_{b}:=\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{v}_{b})
. We conclude that there are two functions
\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon},\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
that are piecewise constant with a finite number of jump discontinuities and satisfy

(5.1)

\|\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{u}_{0}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\varepsilon,\;\mathrm{TotVar}\ \mathbf{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon}\leq\mathrm{TotVar}\ \mathbf{u}_{0},\quad\|\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{u}_{b}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\varepsilon,\;\mathrm{TotVar}\ \mathbf{u}_{b}^{\varepsilon}\leq\mathrm{TotVar}\ \mathbf{u}_{b},

and

(5.2)

|\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}_{0}(0)-\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}_{b}(0)|\leq|\mathbf{u}_{0}(0)-\mathbf{u}_{b}(0)|.

5.2.
Riemann solvers

We consider a Riemann problem for (
1.1
) and we construct two approximate solutions by using either the
accurate
or the
simplified
solver discussed below. In §
5.5
we specify when we use the accurate and when the simplified solver.

5.2.1.
Accurate Riemann solver

As mentioned above, the only point where we cannot simply follow the construction in
[
10
, §7.2]
is that we have to put extra-care in choosing the speed of the wave fronts of the
k
-th family. To this end, we recall
[
10
, Remark 7.1 p.132]
and
[
10
, Remark 7.2 p.142]
. We consider a wave-front
\alpha
and we term
\mathbf{u}_{\alpha}
its right state, by
s_{\alpha}
its size, by
\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\alpha},s_{\alpha})
its left state and by
\mathrm{sp}_{\alpha}
its speed. We want to choose
\mathrm{sp}_{\alpha}
in such a way that

(5.3)

\mathrm{sp}_{\alpha}<0\implies\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\alpha},s_{\alpha})<0\;\text{if~\eqref{e:gnl}},\qquad\quad\mathrm{sp}_{\alpha}<0\implies\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\alpha})<0\;\text{if~\eqref{e:lindeg}},

where

(5.4)

\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u},s_{k})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u},s_{k})&s_{k}\geq 0\\
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u},s_{k}))&s_{k}<0,\\
\end{array}\right.

and
\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u},s_{k})
is the same as in (
4.11
).
To this end we set the following rules:

•

if the
k
-th characteristic field is genuinely nonlinear (
2.8
) and
s_{\alpha}>0
(i.e. if
s_{\alpha}
is a shock front) we can choose
\mathrm{sp}_{\alpha}
in such a way that it satisfies both the Lax condition

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\alpha})<\mathrm{sp}_{\alpha}<\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\alpha},s_{\alpha}))

and the implication (
5.3
)
14
14

14

The reason why we cannot simply set
\mathrm{sp}_{\alpha}:=\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\alpha},s_{\alpha})
is because, as in
[
10
]
, we want to avoid interactions of more than two wave-fronts, and this implies that we may have to very slightly modify the speed of some wave-fronts
.

•

if either the
k
-th characteristic field is linearly degenerate (
2.9
) or it is genuinely nonlinear and
s_{\alpha}<0
(i.e.,
\alpha
is a rarefaction front) we recall
[
10
, (FT4) p.143]
and set

(5.5)

\mathrm{sp}_{\alpha}=\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\alpha}).

We also recall that following the construction in
[
10
, p.129]
, we fix
r_{\varepsilon}>0
15
15

15

In
[
10
, p.129]
this threshold is actually termed
\delta
and we make sure that each rarefaction front has strength bounded by
r_{\varepsilon}
by splitting, if needed, big rarefaction fronts into smaller ones. As in
[
10
]
, we make an exception to this rule when a rarefaction front of the
i
-th family interacts with another wave and generates a rarefaction front of the
i
-th family: in this case the front exiting the interaction is not partitioned, even if its strength is greater than
r_{\varepsilon}
.

5.2.2.
Simplified Riemann solver

We follow the same construction as in
[
10
]
[p.131] and we fix once and for all the speed of the non-physical wave fronts as
\hat{\lambda}>0
satisfying

(5.6)

\hat{\lambda}\geq\sup|\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{u})|\quad\text{for every $i=1,\dots,N$.}

Remark 5.1
.

A word of warning concerning the use of the simplified Riemann solver. We recall footnote
5
at page
5
and the construction in
[
10
, pp.131-132]
and realize that when using the simplified Riemann solver we have to use (rather than the admissible wave-fan curves of
left
states
\mathbf{t}_{i}
) the admissible wave-fan curves of

right
states, the one introduced in the original paper by Lax
[
28
]
, which in the following we term
\boldsymbol{m}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,N
. In this way, physical fronts can travel with very large and
positive
speed. Note however that from the point of view of the strength of the wave fronts, the two constructions are completely equivalent since (up to a suitable choice of the parametrization) we have the relation

(5.7)

\mathbf{t}_{i}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)=\mathbf{u}^{-}\iff\boldsymbol{m}_{i}(\mathbf{u}^{-},-s)=\mathbf{u}^{+},\;\text{for every $s$, $\mathbf{u}^{+}$ and $i=1,\dots,N$}.

5.3.
Accurate boundary Riemann solver

In this paragraph we consider the boundary Riemann problem obtained by coupling (
1.1
) with the boundary and initial conditions (
4.3
). We pass to the
\mathbf{u}
coordinates and set
\mathbf{u}^{+}=\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{v}^{+})
,
\mathbf{u}_{b}=\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{v}_{b})
. We separately consider the cases of a genuinely nonlinear and a linearly degenerate
k
-th family.

5.3.1.
Accurate boundary Riemann solver (genuinely nonlinear case)

We assume (
2.8
) and to fix the notation we assume (
2.11
). Cases B) and C) in Hypothesis
5
can be handled analogously. We consider
(\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi^{\prime}_{k-1},s^{\prime}_{k},s^{\prime}_{k+1},\dots,s^{\prime}_{N})
satisfying

(5.8)

\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\cdot,\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi^{\prime}_{k-1},s^{\prime}_{k})\circ\mathbf{t}_{k+1}(\cdot,s^{\prime}_{k+1})\circ\dots\circ\mathbf{t}_{N}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s^{\prime}_{N}),\mathbf{u}_{b})=\mathbf{0}_{N-\ell},

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}
are the same as in (
2.16
) and (
4.49
), respectively. We recall cases i),
\dots
, vi) in §
4.6.1
and define the accurate boundary Riemann solver as follows.

Step 1:
we define
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
by setting

(5.9)

\mathbf{\hat{u}}:=\mathbf{t}_{k+1}(\cdot,s^{\prime}_{k+1})\circ\dots\mathbf{t}_{N}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s^{\prime}_{N}),

see Figure
1
, and we use the accurate Riemann solver to solve the Riemann problem between
\mathbf{u}^{+}
(on the right) and
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
.

Step 2
we separately consider the following cases

i)

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\geq 0
and
s^{\prime}_{k}>0
we use the accurate Riemann solver to solve the Lax admissible shock between
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
(on the right) and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})
(on the left).

ii)

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\geq 0
and
\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\leq s^{\prime}_{k}\leq 0
we use the accurate Riemann solver to solve the rarefaction between
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
(on the right) and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})
(on the left). If the boundary Riemann problem (
1.1
), (
4.3
) is originated at the interaction between a wave front and the boundary and the hitting wave front is a rarefaction of the
k
-th family then the outgoing rarefaction front between
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s_{k})
is not partitioned, even if its strength is greater than the threshold
r_{\varepsilon}
.

iii)

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\geq 0
and
s^{\prime}_{k}<\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
we set
\mathbf{\bar{u}}:=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
and we use the accurate Riemann solver described at the previous point to solve the rarefaction between
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
(on the right) and
\bar{\mathbf{u}}
(on the left). If the boundary Riemann problem (
1.1
), (
4.3
) is originated at the interaction between a wave front and the boundary and the hitting wave front is of the
k
-th family, then the rarefaction front between
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
and
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
is not partitioned, even if its strength is greater than the threshold
r_{\varepsilon}
.

iv)

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})<0
and
s^{\prime}_{k}>\underline{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
we use the accurate Riemann solver to solve the Lax admissible shock between
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
(on the right) and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})
(on the left).

v)

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})<0
and
s^{\prime}_{k}=\underline{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
there is no wave of the
k
-th family entering the domain after the interaction.

vi)

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})<0
and
s^{\prime}_{k}<\underline{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
there is no wave of the
k
-th family entering the domain after the interaction.

5.3.2.
Accurate boundary Riemann solver (linearly degenerate case)

We assume (
2.9
) and to fix the notation we focus on case B) in Hypothesis
5
. Cases A) and C) in Hypothesis
5
can be handled analogously. We recall the analysis in §
4.6.2
, solve

(5.10)

\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\cdot,\xi^{\prime}_{\ell(\mathbf{u}_{b})+1},\dots,\xi^{\prime}_{k-1},s^{\prime}_{k})\circ\mathbf{t}_{k+1}(\cdot,s^{\prime}_{k+1})\circ\dots\circ\mathbf{t}_{N}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s^{\prime}_{N}),\mathbf{u}_{b})=\mathbf{0}_{N},

and consider the same value
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
as in (
5.9
). We use the accurate Riemann solver to solve the Riemann problem between
\mathbf{u}^{+}
(on the right) and
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
(on the left) and

•

if
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})>0
we accurately solve the Riemann problem with a contact discontinuity between
\mathbf{u}^{+}
(on the right) and
\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})
(on the left);

•

if
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\leq 0
there is no wave of the
k
-th family entering the domain after the interaction.

Remark 5.2
.

Let
\bar{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}
denote the trace of the wave front-tracking approximation after the interaction. Owing to the analysis in §
4.6.1
and §
4.6.2
we have (
4.57
) and (
4.58
) (in the genuinely nonlinear case) and (
4.60
) (in the linearly degenerate case) with

\xi^{\prime}_{k}
and
\bar{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}
in place of
\xi_{k}
and
\bar{\mathbf{u}}
, respectively. Here
\xi^{\prime}_{k}
satisfies
\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi^{\prime}_{k}),\mathbf{u}_{b})=\mathbf{0}_{N}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}_{\ell(\mathbf{u}_{b})+1},\dots,\xi^{\prime}_{k}),\mathbf{u}_{b})=\mathbf{0}_{N}

in case A) and B) in Hypothesis
5
, respectively. The explicit expression of
\xi^{\prime}_{k}
in terms of
s^{\prime}_{k}
can be obtained by arguing as in §
4.6.1
and §
4.6.2
.

5.4.
Simplified boundary Riemann solver

To define the simplified boundary Riemann solver we consider a wave-front hitting the boundary and we term
\mathbf{u}^{+}
and
\mathbf{u}^{-}
the states at the right and left-hand side of the wave, respectively. We term
j
the family of the hitting wave front and we separately consider the following cases.

Case
j<k
:
the simplified boundary Riemann solver is a non-physical wave front traveling with speed
\hat{\lambda}
and with left and right states
\mathbf{u}^{-}
and
\mathbf{u}^{+}
, respectively.

Case
j=k
:
we consider the solution of (
1.1
), (
4.3
) and
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
defined by (
5.9
). To define the simplified boundary Riemann solver

(1)

we accurately solve the boundary Riemann problem (
1.1
),(
4.3
) with
\mathbf{v}^{+}
replaced by
\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
. Note that by the definition of
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
the solution only involves boundary layers and a wave front of the
k
-th family (if any);

(2)

we introduce a non-physical front between
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
(on the left) and
\mathbf{u}^{+}
(on the right);

(3)

we juxtapose the two.

Note that, if the
k
-th vector field is genuinely nonlinear, then the trace
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
of the wave front-tracking approximation after the interaction (solved by using the simplified boundary Riemann solver) satisfies again (
4.57
).

5.5.
Discrimination between accurate and simplified Riemann and boundary Riemann solvers

We fix a parameter
\omega_{\varepsilon}
, to be determined in the following, and set the following rules:

•

Riemann problems involving the initial datum and boundary Riemann problems involving discontinuities in the boundary datum are always solved accurately;

•

at interactions between two wave fronts with strengths
|s_{\alpha}|
and
|s_{\beta}|
inside the domain we use the accurate Riemann solver if
|s_{\alpha}s_{\beta}|\geq\omega_{\varepsilon}
, the simplified Riemann solver otherwise;

•

when a wave front of strength
|s|
and family
j<k
hits the boundary, we use the accurate boundary Riemann solver if
|s|\geq\omega_{\varepsilon}
, the simplified boundary Riemann solver otherwise;

•

assume that a
k
-th wave front of strength
|s|
hits the boundary, and the
k
-th characteristic field is genuinely nonlinear
2.8
. In this case we use the accurate boundary Riemann solver if

|s|\big([\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)]^{-}+|\xi_{k}|\big)\geq\omega_{\varepsilon}
, the simplified boundary Riemann solver otherwise. In the previous expression,
\varsigma_{k}
is the same as in (
5.4
),
\mathbf{u}^{+}
denotes the right state of the hitting wave front and
|\xi_{k}|
the strength of the center component of the boundary layer. That is, if
\mathbf{u}_{b}
denotes the boundary datum and if we have (
2.11
) then

\boldsymbol{\beta}({\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\cdot,\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k})\circ\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s),\mathbf{u}_{b})=\mathbf{0}_{N-\ell}

for some
\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k-1}
.

•

when a
k
-th wave front hits the boundary, and the
k
-th characteristic field is linearly degenerate
2.9
we use the accurate boundary Riemann solver if

|s|\big([\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+})+|\xi_{k}|\big)\geq\omega_{\varepsilon}
, the simplified boundary Riemann solver otherwise.
Here
\xi_{k}
is the same as at the previous item.

The motivation for the above choices comes from Lemma
6.1
,
6.2
and
6.3
below, from the interaction estimates
[
10
, (7.31),(7.32)]
, and from the consequent expression of the functionals defined in §
7
.

6.
Main interaction estimates

In this section we establish new interaction estimates: more precisely, we accurately track the strength of the new wave fronts that are generated when a wave front interacts with the boundary. From the technical viewpoint, the main estimate (
6.5
) is one of the most innovative results of the paper. The exposition is organized as follows: in §
6.1
we focus on the interaction of a wave front of the family
j
,
j<k
, with the boundary. In §
6.2
we state our main results concerning the interaction between a wave front of the
k
-th family with the boundary, and in §
6.3
we give the proof in the case of a genuinely nonlinear
k
-th characteristic field, and in §
6.5
we deal with the linearly degenerate case. In §
6.4
we establish some further interaction estimates we need in the following sections.

6.1.
Interaction between the boundary and a wave front of the
j
-th family,
j<k

We fix
j<k
and consider a wave front of the
j
-th family hitting the boundary
x=0
, see Figure
2
(left).

Figure 2.
A wave front of the
j
-th family,
j<k
, (left) and of the
k
-th family (right) hitting the boundary and the wave fronts that are generated at the interaction.
t
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
\mathbf{u}^{+}
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
t
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
\mathbf{u}^{+}
\mathbf{\hat{u}}

To ease the exposition, we assume (
2.11
), but the analysis in cases B) and C) in Hypothesis
5
is entirely analogous.
We introduce the following notation: we term
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
and
\mathbf{u}^{+}
the left and the right state, respectively, of the hitting wave front of the
j
-th family, which yields

(6.1)

\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{t}_{j}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s_{j}),\quad j<k,

for some suitable
s_{j}
. In the previous expression,
\mathbf{t}_{j}
denotes the wave fan curve of admissible states, see
[
28
]
and §
4.2
. We also term
\mathbf{u}_{b}
the “boundary datum”,
namely we assume
\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}_{b})
in the sense of Definition
1.1
. Note that owing to the analysis in §
4.6.1
and §
4.6.2
this implies
(
4.53
) for some
(\xi_{\ell+1},\dots,\xi_{k})
with
\xi_{k}
satisfying (
4.57
) (if the
k
-th field in genuinely nonlinear) or (
4.60
) (if the
k
-th vector field is linearly degenerate). To solve the boundary Riemann problem, we have to determine
