\mathbf{v}\in BV(]0,T[\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
for every
T>0
. What we actually show is that, for every vanishing
sequence
\{\varepsilon_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
there is a subsequence (which to ease the notation we do not relabel) such that

(8.1)

\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,\cdot)\to\mathbf{u}(t,\cdot)\quad\text{in $L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})$ as $m\to+\infty$ for every $t>0$},

where
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}
is the wave front tracking approximation constructed as in §
5
and
\mathbf{u}
is a suitable limit function. To obtain (
1.8
) we then simply apply the change of variables
\mathbf{u}\leftrightarrow\mathbf{v}
. The main steps in the proof of (
8.1
) are the same as in the proof of
[
10
, Theorem 7.1]
, so we do not repeat every detail, but only focus on the parts where the proof is different.
More precisely, we establish Lemmas
8.1
,
8.2
,
8.3
, which heuristically speaking state that the total number of wave fronts is finite, that the strength of each rarefaction front is small and that the total strength of non-physical wave fronts is small, respectively.

Note that the analysis in
[
10
, Chapters 7,10]
yields that
\mathbf{v}
being the limit of the wave front-tracking approximation enjoys several further properties: in particular it has the regularity properties dictated by
[
10
, Theorem 10.4]
and if the system (
1.1
) has a convex entropy then
\mathbf{v}
is entropy admissible. Conversely, showing that
\mathbf{v}
satisfies the boundary condition
\mathbf{v}_{b}
in the sense of Definition
1.1
requires quite some new analysis, which is provided in section §
9
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon}
be the wave front-tracking approximation constructed in §
5
, then the total number of wave fronts and interactions is finite and bounded by a constant that only depends on
N
, on the number of discontinuities in the initial and boundary conditions
\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}_{0}
and
\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}_{b}
, and on the threshold parameter
\omega_{\varepsilon}
in §
5.5
.

Proof.

We recall the rules stated in §
5.5
and we proceed according to the following steps.

Step 1:
we show that the total number of physical fronts is finite. The number of physical fronts created at
t=0
is finite because so is the number of discontinuities of the approximate initial datum. By also recalling the analysis in §
5.2.1
, §
5.3
, §
5.2.2
, §
5.4
we conclude that the number of physical fronts can only increase

•

at times where the approximate boundary datum has a discontinuity. These times are finitely many by construction of the approximate boundary datum;

•

at times where the accurate Riemann solver is used to solve interactions inside the domain. By using the analysis in §
7.5

and in particular (
7.49
) we conclude that any time this happens, we have
\Delta\Upsilon\leq-\omega_{\varepsilon}
. Since
0\leq\Upsilon(t)\leq\Upsilon(0)
, this can happen at most finitely many times;

•

at times where the accurate boundary Riemann solver is used to solve boundary Riemann problems at the domain boundary. Owing to (
7.17
) and (
7.26
), any times this happens we have
\Delta\Upsilon\leq-\omega_{\varepsilon}
and hence this can happen at most finitely many times;

•

the number of physical fronts does not increase at times where the simplified Riemann or boundary Riemann solver is used.

Step 2:
we show that the total number of non-physical fronts is finite, which is the most delicate point.

Figure 5.
The argument in
Step 2
of the proof of Lemma
8.1
t
\tau_{1}
\alpha
\tau^{\ast}
\tau_{2}

Non-physical fronts are not generated at time
t=0
. By the analysis in §
5.2.2
, §
5.4
we conclude that the number of non-physical fronts can only increase

•

at times where an interaction between two physical fronts occurs inside the domain. Since two given physical fronts can interact only once, then by the analysis at
Step 1
this can happen at most finitely many times.

•

at times where a physical front hits the boundary. Wave fronts of the family
j<k
can only hit the boundary once and hence this kind of interactions can occur at most finitely many times. However, it may in principle happen that a single wave front of the
k
-th family is bounced back and forth between the boundary and other waves inside the domain infinitely many times. To rule out this possibility, fix a given wave front
\alpha
of the
k
-th family which leaves the boundary (with positive speed) at
t=\tau_{1}
. This wave front is marked in red in Figure
5
. Assume that
\alpha
does not cross any physical front on the time interval
]\tau_{1},\tau_{2}[
; we now show that it cannot cross non-physical fronts either. Indeed, assume by contradiction that is does. Since the speed of a non-physical front (marked in blue in Figure
5
) is larger than the speed of
\alpha
, the non-physical front must have been created at a time
\tau_{\ast}>\tau_{1}
when a physical wave front (marked in maroon in Figure
5
) had hit the boundary, and this physical front must have crossed
\alpha
on the interval
]\tau_{1},\tau_{\ast}[\subseteq]\tau_{1},\tau_{2}[
, which yields a contradiction. Summing up, if at time
\tau_{2}>\tau_{1}
the wave front
\alpha
crosses another wave front and as a result the speed of
\alpha
becomes negative, then
\alpha
must have crossed a physical front in the interval
]\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]
. Since two given physical fronts can interact only once, then by the analysis at
Step 1
this can happen at most finitely many times and hence a given wave front can be bounced back and forth between the boundary and other waves at most finitely many times.

This implies that the total number of non-physical fronts is also finite.

Step 3:
the above analysis also implies that the total number of interactions is finite.
∎

We recall form §
5.2.1
that
r_{\varepsilon}
represents the threshold for the strength of rarefaction waves

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
\alpha
be a rarefaction wave front in the
\varepsilon
-wave front-tracking approximation
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon}
; then the strength of
\alpha
satisfies

(8.2)

|s_{\alpha}|\leq C_{9}r_{\varepsilon}

for some suitable constant
C_{9}>0
only depending on the functions
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{E}
,
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{G}
and on the value
\mathbf{u}^{\ast}
.

Proof.

We fix a rarefaction wave front
\alpha
, which is created at time
\tau_{\alpha}
and exists till time
T_{\alpha}
(with
T_{\alpha}
possibly equal to
+\infty
). We term
s_{\alpha}(t)
its size at time
t
and
i_{\alpha}
the family it belongs to.
Note that
s_{\alpha}(\cdot)
is a piecewise constant function defined for
t\geq\tau_{\alpha}
and that
|s_{\alpha}(\tau_{\alpha})|\leq r_{\varepsilon}
by construction. To control the growth of
s_{\alpha}
we adapt an argument given in
[
10
, p.139]
and we set

(8.3)

Y_{\alpha}(t):=\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{A}(\alpha)}|\vartheta_{\beta}|+\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{I}}|\vartheta_{\beta}|+|\xi_{k}|,

where
\mathcal{A}(\alpha)
is the set of waves approaching
\alpha
at the time
t
and
\mathcal{I}
is the set of waves of the families
1,\dots,k-1
. The reason why we need to include the term with
\mathcal{I}
is to handle
Case 3
below when the hitting wave is not characteristic.
Finally, we set

(8.4)

I(t):=K_{1}Q(t)+K_{1}R(t)+K_{2}S(t)+K_{3}Z(t),\qquad L_{\alpha}(t):=|s_{\alpha}(t)|\exp\big(K_{5}[Y_{\alpha}(t)+I(t)]\big),

where
K_{5}
is a suitable constant satisfying

(8.5)

K_{5}\geq\max\{1,4C_{5}\},\qquad K_{5}\delta\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Assume for a moment that we have shown that the function
L
is monotone non-increasing, then

\begin{split}|s_{\alpha}(t)|&\leq|s_{\alpha}(t)|\exp\big(K_{5}[Y_{\alpha}(t)+I(t)]\big)\leq|s_{\alpha}(\tau_{\alpha})|\exp\big(K_{5}[Y_{\alpha}(\tau_{\alpha})+I(\tau_{\alpha})]\big)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:assumption}}}{{\leq}}r_{\varepsilon}\underbrace{\exp\big(K_{5}\delta\big)}_{:=C_{9}}\quad\text{for every $t\in]\tau_{\alpha},T_{\alpha}[$}\end{split}

that is (
8.2
). To show that the function
L
is monotone non-increasing we assume that a given time
\tau
an interaction or a discontinuity in the boundary datum occurs and by using an elementary computation based on the convexity of the exponential function we get

(8.6)

\begin{split}\Delta L(\tau)&\leq\exp\big(K_{5}[Y_{\alpha}(\tau^{+})+I(\tau^{+})]\big)\Big[\Delta|s_{\alpha}|(\tau)+K_{5}|s_{\alpha}(\tau^{-})|[\Delta Y_{\alpha}(\tau)+\Delta I(\tau)]\Big]\end{split}

We now prove
\Delta L(\tau)\leq 0
by separately considering the following cases.

Case 1:
the interaction occurs inside the domain and does not involve
\alpha
, but rather two wave fronts with sizes
s_{\beta}
and
s_{\gamma}
. We have

\Delta|s_{\alpha}|(\tau)=0.

Since
\xi_{k}(\tau^{+})=\xi_{k}(\tau^{-})
then by (
7.38
) and (
7.39
) we get

\Delta Y_{\alpha}(\tau)\leq AC_{5}|s_{\beta}||s_{\gamma}|
and by following the analysis in §
7.5
and in particular (
7.49
) we conclude

\Delta Y_{\alpha}(\tau)+\Delta I(t)\leq 0

and by combining the above inequalities with (
8.6
) we conclude that
\Delta L(\tau)\leq 0
.

Case 2:
the interaction occurs inside the domain and involves
\alpha
, which interacts with a physical front
\beta
with strength
s_{\beta}
.
If
\alpha
and
\beta
belong to the same family,
i_{\alpha}=i_{\beta}
, then
\beta
must be a shock front (because rarefaction wave fronts of the same family do not cross each other) and then
\Delta|s_{\alpha}|(\tau)\leq 0
owing to cancellation. If
i_{\beta}\neq i_{\alpha}
then owing to (
7.38
) we have
\Delta|s_{\alpha}|(\tau)\leq C_{5}|s_{\alpha}s_{\beta}|
.
Also,

\Delta Y_{\alpha}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:iebressan}}}{{\leq}}-|s_{\beta}|+AC_{5}|s_{\alpha}||s_{\beta}|+\stackrel{{\scriptstyle AC_{5}\delta\leq 8^{-1}}}{{\leq}}-\frac{1}{4}|s_{\beta}|

By following again the analysis in §
7.5.2
and in particular (
7.49
) we get
\Delta I(\tau)\leq 0
.
Note that the same estimates hold if the front that interacts with
\alpha
is a non-physical one (in this case we actually have the stronger estimate
\Delta s_{\alpha}(\tau)=0
). Either case, by combining the above inequalities with (
8.5
) and (
8.6
) we conclude that
\Delta L(\tau)\leq 0.

Case 3:
the interaction occurs at the domain boundary and does not involve
\alpha
; in this case we have
\Delta|s_{\alpha}|(\tau)=0
and, owing to the analysis in §
7.2
,§
7.3
and in particular to (
7.17
),(
7.26
),(
7.19
), we have
\Delta Y_{\alpha}(\tau)+\Delta I(\tau)\leq 0
and owing to (
8.6
) this yields
\Delta L(\tau)\leq 0
.

Case 4:
the interaction occurs at the domain boundary and involves
\alpha
; we separately consider the following cases.

Case 4A:

\alpha
belongs to the family
j_{\alpha}=k
. If there is no wave front of the
k
-th family entering the domain after the interaction, or if the wave front of the
k
-th family is a a shock, then the rarefaction wave front ceases to exist at time
\tau
and there is nothing to prove. If there is a rarefaction wave front entering the domain after the interaction then we are in case ii) or iii) in §
5.3
. Also, we can apply Lemma
6.8
and arrive at

(8.7)

\Delta|s_{\alpha}|(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:rarepiccola}}}{{\leq}}|s_{\alpha}|(\tau^{-})\big[-1+C_{8}([\varsigma_{k}]^{-}+|\xi_{k}|)\big]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:sole}}}{{\leq}}-\frac{1}{2}|s_{\alpha}|(\tau^{-}),\quad\quad\Delta I(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:c:dupsilon}}}{{\leq}}0.

Also,
\Delta Y_{\alpha}(t)\leq|\xi^{\prime}_{k}|-|\xi_{k}|\leq|\xi^{\prime}_{k}|\leq\delta
and by using (
8.5
) and (
8.6
) this yields
\Delta L(\tau)\leq 0.
Note that the previous considerations apply in both the case of the accurate Riemann solver and the simplified Riemann solver.

Case 4B:

\alpha
belongs to the family
i_{\alpha}<k
. In this case the wave front
\alpha
ceases to exist at time
\tau
and there is nothing to prove.

Case 5:
the boundary datum
\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\varepsilon}
is discontinuous at
\tau
. We have
\Delta|s_{\alpha}|(\tau)=0
and by recalling the analysis in §
7.4
and in particular (
7.37
) we infer
\Delta Y_{\alpha}(\tau)+\Delta I(\tau)\leq 0
and owing to (
8.6
) this yields
\Delta L(\tau)\leq 0
.∎

We recall that
\omega_{\varepsilon}
represents the threshold to discriminate between the accurate and the simplified Riemann and boundary Riemann problem, see
5.5
.

Lemma 8.3
.

For every
\nu>0
, there is
\chi_{\nu}>0
such that if
\omega_{\varepsilon}\leq\chi_{\nu}
then

(8.8)

\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{NP}}|s_{\alpha}|\leq\nu,

where
\mathcal{NP}
denotes the set of non-physical fronts.

Proof.

The proof is based on the same argument as in
[
10
, pp.139-142]
, so instead of providing all the details we only focus on the points where the argument is different. We proceed according to the following steps.

Step 1:
we show that

(8.9)

|s_{\alpha}|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\omega_{\varepsilon},\quad\text{for every $\alpha\in\mathcal{NP}$}.

We recall that, by construction, when a non-physical front is created its strength is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(1)\omega_{\varepsilon}
. To conclude with (
8.9
) we can then use exactly the same argument as in
[
10
, p.140]
.

Step 2:
we assign to each wave-front
\alpha
a
generation order

n_{\alpha}
. To this end, we proceed as follows

•

all the waves generated at
t=0
and at discontinuity times of
\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}_{b}
have generation order
1
;

•

when two waves with generation orders
n_{\alpha}
and
n_{\beta}
interact inside the domain, the generation order of the outgoing waves is assigned as
in
[
10
, pp.140]
;

•

if a wave-front
\alpha
with generation order
n_{\alpha}
hits the boundary and
i_{\alpha}=k
, then wave front of the
k
-th family entering the domain after the interaction (if any) has generation order
n_{\alpha}
, the other wave fronts entering the domain have generation order
n_{\alpha}+1
;

•

if a wave-front
\alpha
with generation order
n_{\alpha}
hits the boundary and
i_{\alpha}<k
, then all wave fronts entering the domain after the interaction have generation order
n_{\alpha}+1
.

Note that we are heuristically speaking regarding the boundary as a wave front with generation order
1
.

Step 3:
we fix
n\geq 2
and we set

\begin{split}&V^{+}_{n}(t):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{\alpha}\geq n\\
i_{\alpha}\geq k\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|,\quad V^{-}_{n}(t):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{\alpha}\geq n\\
i_{\alpha}<k\end{subarray}}|\vartheta_{\alpha}|,\quad Q_{n}(t):=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\max\{n_{\alpha},n_{\beta}\}\geq n}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!w_{\alpha\beta}|\vartheta_{\alpha}\vartheta_{\beta}|,\quad R_{n}(t):=|\xi_{k}|\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{\alpha}\leq k,\\
n_{\alpha}\geq n\end{subarray}}|\vartheta_{\alpha}|,\\
&W_{n}(t):=V^{+}_{n+1}(t)+V^{-}_{n}(t),\end{split}

where we formally count non-physical fronts as belonging to the family
N+1
. We also introduce the notation

S_{n}(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{\alpha}=k,\\
n_{\alpha}\geq n\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|[\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\alpha},s_{\alpha})]^{-}}&\text{if~\eqref{e:gnl}}\\
\\
\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{\alpha}=k,\\
n_{\alpha}\geq n\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|\big[\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\alpha})\big]^{-}}&\text{if~\eqref{e:lindeg}}\\
\end{array}\right.

and

I_{n}(t):=K_{1}Q_{n}(t)+K_{1}R_{n}(t)+K_{2}S_{n}(t),\qquad\text{for $n\geq 2$}

and we set

V_{1}^{+}(t):=|\xi_{k}|+\sum_{i_{\alpha}\geq k}|s_{\alpha}|,\qquad V^{-}_{1}(t):=\sum_{i_{\alpha}<k}|\vartheta_{\alpha}|,\qquad W_{1}(t):=V_{2}^{+}(t)+V_{1}^{-}(t),\qquad I_{1}(t):=I(t),

where
I
is as in (
8.4
).
We also term

•

J^{0}_{n}
the set of times at which two wave fronts
\alpha
and
\beta
with
\max\{n_{\alpha},n_{\beta}\}=n
interact;

•

J_{n}^{k}
the set of times at which a wave-front
\alpha
with
i_{\alpha}=k
and
n_{\alpha}=n
hits the boundary;

•

J^{-}_{n}
the set of times where a wave front
\alpha
with
i_{\alpha}<k
and
n_{\alpha}=n
hits the boundary.

In the remaining part of the proof we will always assume
n\geq 3
.
We have

(8.10)

\begin{array}[]{rl}\Delta W_{n}(t)=0&t\in J^{0}_{1}\cup\dots\cup J^{0}_{n-2}\cup J^{k}_{1}\cup\dots\cup J^{k}_{n-1}\cup J^{-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup J^{-}_{n-1};\\
\Delta W_{n}(t)+\Delta I_{n-1}(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@sectionsign\ref {ss:inside}}}{{\leq}}0&t\in\displaystyle{\bigcup_{m\geq n-1}J^{0}_{m}};\\
\Delta W_{n}(t)+\Delta I_{n-1}(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@sectionsign\ref {ss:accuratek}}}{{\leq}}0&t\in\displaystyle{\bigcup_{m\geq n}J^{k}_{m}};\\
\Delta W_{n}(t)+\Delta I_{n-1}(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@sectionsign\ref {ss:accuratem}}}{{\leq}}0&t\in\displaystyle{\bigcup_{m\geq n}J^{-}_{m}.}\end{array}

If
t\in J^{0}_{1}\cup\dots\cup J^{0}_{n-2}
then
\Delta R_{n}(t)=\Delta S_{n}(t)=0
, so
\Delta I_{n}(t)=K_{1}\Delta Q_{n}(t)
, which owing to (
7.43
), (
7.49
) and to the inequality
W_{n}\leq W_{n-1}
yields

\Delta I_{n}(t)+K_{1}C_{5}A\Delta\Upsilon W_{n-1}(t^{-})\leq\Delta I_{n}(t)+K_{1}C_{5}A\Delta\Upsilon W_{n}(t^{-})\leq 0.

If
t\in J^{k}_{1}\cup\dots\cup J^{k}_{n-1}
we have
\Delta S_{n}(t)=0
, whereas
R_{n}
can change because the strength
\xi_{k}
can change at
t
. This yields

\Delta I_{n}(t)=K_{1}\Delta Q_{n}(t)+K_{1}\Delta R_{n}(t)
, which owing to (
7.20
) and (
7.26
) yields

\Delta I_{n}(t)+K_{1}C_{2}A\Delta\Upsilon W_{n-1}(t^{-})\leq 0.

If
t\in J^{-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup J^{-}_{n-1}
we again have
\Delta I_{n}(t)=K_{1}\Delta Q_{n}(t)+K_{1}\Delta R_{n}(t)
, which owing to (
7.11
) and (
7.17
) yields

\Delta I_{n}(t)+K_{1}C_{1}A\Delta\Upsilon W_{n-1}(t^{-})\leq 0.

If
t\in J^{0}_{n-1}
then owing to (
7.38
), (
7.39
) and (
7.49
) we have

\displaystyle\Delta Q_{n}(t)+AC_{5}V(t^{-})\Delta I_{n-1}(t)\leq 0,\qquad\Delta R_{n}(t)+AC_{5}|\xi_{k}(t^{-})|\Delta I_{n-1}(t)\leq 0

We now point out that there are two possible contributions to
\Delta S_{n}(t)
: i) new
k
-family waves may be created at the interaction; ii)
k
-family waves with generation order greater or equal than
n
change their strength and speed. Owing to (
7.38
),(
7.39
),(
7.40
), (
7.42
) and (
7.49
) yields

\Delta S_{n}(t)+C_{4}C_{5}\delta\Delta I_{n-1}(t)+C_{3}C_{5}\delta\Delta I_{n-1}(t)\leq 0.

If
t\in\displaystyle{\bigcup_{m\geq n}J^{0}_{m}}
then owing to (
7.49
) we have
\Delta I_{n}\leq 0
owing to (
7.26
).
If
t\in\displaystyle{\bigcup_{m\geq n}J^{k}_{m}}
then
\Delta I_{n}\leq 0
. If
t\in\displaystyle{\bigcup_{m\geq n}J^{-}_{m}}
then
\Delta S_{n}=0
(because the new
k
-family waves created at the interaction, if any, have positive speed)
and, owing to (
7.11
) and (
7.16
)

\Delta Q_{n}(t)+\Delta R_{n}(t)+\frac{C_{1}+C_{12}+C_{12}C_{1}}{A-C_{1}}\Delta W_{n-1}(t)\delta\leq 0.

Summing up, we have

(8.11)

\begin{array}[]{rl}\Delta I_{n}(t)+\mathcal{O}(1)\Delta\Upsilon(t)W_{n-1}(t^{-})\leq 0&t\in J^{0}_{1}\cup\dots\cup J^{0}_{n-2}\cup J^{k}_{1}\cup\dots\cup J^{k}_{n-1}\cup J^{-}_{1}\cup\dots\cup J^{-}_{n-1}\\
\Delta I_{n}(t)+\mathcal{O}(1)\delta\Delta I_{n-1}(t)\leq 0&t\in J^{0}_{n-1}\\
\Delta I_{n}(t)\leq 0&t\in\displaystyle{\bigcup_{m\geq n}J^{k}_{m}}\\
\Delta I_{n}(t)+\mathcal{O}(1)\Delta W_{n-1}(t)V(t^{-})\leq 0&t\in\displaystyle{\bigcup_{m\geq n}J^{-}_{m}}\\
\end{array}

Note that (
8.10
) yields

(8.12)

W_{n}(t^{+})\leq\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta I_{n-1}(\tau)]_{-},

where in the previous expression the sum is over the times
\tau
of interaction, and that owing to (
8.11
) we also have

(8.13)

\begin{split}I_{n}(t)&\leq\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta I_{n}(\tau)]_{+}\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\sup_{\tau}W_{n-1}(\tau)\underbrace{\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta\Upsilon(\tau)]_{-}}_{\leq\Upsilon(0)\leq\delta}+\mathcal{O}(1)\delta\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta I_{n-1}(\tau)]_{-}\\
&+\mathcal{O}(1)\delta\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta W_{n-1}(\tau)]_{-}\end{split}

Note that
W_{n-1}(0)=0
since
n\geq 3
and this implies

\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta W_{n-1}(\tau)]_{-}=\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta W_{n-1}(\tau)]_{+}-W_{n-1}(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{stimew}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta I_{n-2}(\tau)]_{-}-W_{n-1}(t)\leq\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta I_{n-2}(\tau)]_{-}

and by plugging the above inequality into (
8.13
) and recalling (
8.12
) we arrive at

\begin{split}\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta I_{n}(\tau)]_{+}\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\delta\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta I_{n-1}(\tau)]_{-}+\mathcal{O}(1)\delta\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta I_{n-2}(\tau)]_{-}\end{split}

and since

\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta I_{n-1}(\tau)]_{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle I_{n-1}(0)=0}}{{=}}\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta I_{n-1}(\tau)]_{+}-I_{n-1}(t)\leq\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta I_{n-1}(\tau)]_{+}

by setting

\tilde{I}_{n}:=\sum_{0\leq\tau\leq t}[\Delta I_{n}(\tau)]_{+}

we arrive at

(8.14)

\tilde{I}_{n}\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\delta\tilde{I}_{n-1}+\mathcal{O}(1)\delta\tilde{I}_{n-2}

From (
8.14
) a fairly standard induction argument implies that, if
\delta
is sufficiently small then
\tilde{I}_{n}\leq(2\mathcal{O}(1)\delta)^{e_{n}}
, with
e_{n}=[(n-3)/2]+1
and
[\cdot]
denoting the integer part. From this we can then argue as in
[
10
, p. 141-142]
, control
I_{n}
and
W_{n}
and whence (by arguing as in
[
10
, (7.77)]
) the total strength of non-physical waves. This concludes the proof of Lemma
8.3
.
∎

9.
Boundary condition

The goal of this section is to conclude the proof of Theorem
1.2
(and hence of Theorem
1.1
) by establishing the following result.

Theorem 9.1
.

Under the same assumption as in the statement of Theorem
1.1
, let
\mathbf{v}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
be the same as in (
1.8
). Then for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
the trace
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t):=\lim_{x\to+0^{+}}\mathbf{v}(t,x)
satisfies
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{v}_{b}(t)
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

The proof of Theorem
9.1
is rather technical and involved, and represents one of the most innovative parts of the present paper.
Before giving some technical details, a word of warning: in the previous sections, we have always worked with the variable
\mathbf{u}
only because in the boundary layers analysis we have used the fact that the viscous system (
1.6
) is in the
normal
form, in the Kawashima Shizuta sense
[
27
]
. In this section, instead, we investigate the boundary condition and hence we have to use the variable
\mathbf{v}
as well, because Definition
1.1
involves the flux function
\mathbf{f}
. For the reader’s convenience we now provide a sketchy overview of the proof of Theorem
9.1
.

9.1.
Roadmap of the proof of Theorem
9.1

For the most part, in what follows we assume that the
k
-th characteristic field is genuinely nonlinear, namely we assume (
2.8
), and only in §
9.6
we discuss how to adapt the proof to the case of a linearly degenerate (
2.9
)
k
-th characteristic field.

In §
9.2
we show that

(9.1)

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbf{f}\left(\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x)\right)=\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}(t,x),\qquad\text{for a.e. $t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}$,}

where
\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}=\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}})
and
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}
is the same as in (
8.1
)
19
19

19

Note that in (
8.1
)
\varepsilon_{m}
is a specific subsequence and, in principle, the limit
\mathbf{u}
depends on the particular choice of the subsequence (as we mentioned in the introduction, we are confident that one could establish uniqueness results, but this goes beyond the scopes of the present paper). However, in the remaining of this section we will always work with the same sequence
\varepsilon_{m}
and this uniquely identifies the limit
\mathbf{u}
.
.
To this end, the main issue is establishing Lemma
9.2
, which provides a uniform in
\varepsilon
total variation bound on
\mathbf{f}\left(\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(\cdot,x)\right)
. In the proof of Lemma
9.2
we introduce the functional
\Lambda
, defined as in (
9.5
), and prove that it is monotone non-increasing by carefully tracking how it varies any time either a wave front hits the boundary, or the approximate boundary datum has a jump discontinuity.

In §
9.3
we construct the set
\widetilde{E}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
, which plays a main role in our analysis and is defined by setting

(9.2)

\widetilde{E}:=F\cup G\cup H\cup D.

In the previous expression, the sets
F
and
H
are rigorously defined in (
9.24
) and (
9.26
) below, but, very loosely speaking, a time
\tau
belongs to
F
if there are a lot of wave fronts of the wave front-tracking approximation
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}
hitting the domain at times that accumulate around
\tau
as
m\to+\infty
. Conversely, an instant
\tau
belongs to
H
if there are a lot of waves of the wave front-tracking approximation
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}
that interact in proximity of the domain boundary at times that accumulate around
\tau
as
m\to+\infty
. By relying on the rigorous definitions (
9.24
) and (
9.26
) it is fairly easy to show that
F
and
H
are both
\mathcal{L}^{1}
-negligible. The set
D
is the set where the boundary datum
\mathbf{v}_{b}
is discontinuous, and it is also
\mathcal{L}^{1}
-negligible owing to the assumption
\mathrm{TotVar}\,\mathbf{v}_{b}<+\infty
. Finally, the (not necessarily negligible) set
G
in formula (
9.2
) is rigorously defined by (
9.25
) and heuristically speaking represents the set of times where there is a sequence of Lax admissible shocks for
\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}
with vanishing speed that as
m\to+\infty
accumulate at the domain boundary. This heuristic interpretation is consistent with Lemma
9.5
, which dictates that if
t\notin\widetilde{E}
, the limit of the traces of
\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}
coincides with the trace of the limit
\mathbf{v}
.

In §
9.4
we show that, for a.e.
t\notin\widetilde{E}
, the trace
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)=\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}\mathbf{v}(t,x)
satisfies
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{v}_{b}(t)
in the sense of Definition
1.1
, whereas in §
9.5
by using (
9.1
) we establish the same property for a.e.
t\in\widetilde{E}
. Finally, in §
9.6
we establish the proof of Theorem
9.1
in the case where the
k
-th vector field is linearly degenerate.

9.2.
Proof of (
9.1
)

The proof of (
9.1
) relies on the following total variation bound.

Lemma 9.2
.

Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
we have

(9.3)

\mathrm{TotVar}\,\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(\cdot,0)\leq C_{13}\delta,\quad\text{for every $m\in\mathbb{N}$}

and for a suitable constant
C_{13}>0
only depending on
\mathbf{g}
,
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{D}
and on the change of variables
\mathbf{u}
, and on the value
\mathbf{u}^{\ast}
. In the above expression, the constant
\delta
is the same as in (
7.1
).

The proof of the above theorem uses the following result.

Lemma 9.3
.

Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
, if the
k
-the vector field is genuinely nonlinear (
2.8
) we have

(9.4)

|\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})-\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))|\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{O}(1)|s||\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)|&s>0\\
\mathcal{O}(1)|s|\max\{|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))|,|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})|\}&s<0.\\
\end{array}\right.

Proof of Lemma
9.3
.

We separately consider the following cases.

Case 1:

s\geq 0
. We have

|\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})-\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:rh}}}{{=}}|\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)||\mathbf{g}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})-\mathbf{g}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s||\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s)|

and this establishes (
9.4
).

Case 2:

s<0
. We have

\begin{split}|\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})&-\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:laxc}}}{{=}}|\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})-\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{i}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:raref}}}{{=}}\left|\int_{s}^{0}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{f}\,\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\ \mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{i}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},z))dz\right|\\
&=\left|\int_{s}^{0}\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{i}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},z))\mathbf{D}\mathbf{g}\,\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\ \mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{i}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},z))dz\right|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\int_{s}^{0}|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{i}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},z))|dz\\
&\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s|\max\{|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{i}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))|,|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})|\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:laxc}}}{{=}}\mathcal{O}(1)|s|\max\{|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},s))|,|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}})|\},\end{split}

i.e. (
9.4
). To establish the last inequality at the second line of the above expression we have used the fact that, owing to (
2.8
), the function
z\mapsto\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{i}_{k}(\mathbf{\widetilde{u}},z))
is monotone non-decreasing and hence the maximum of its modulus on an interval is attained at one the two extrema.
∎

We are now ready to give the

Proof of Lemma
9.2
(genuinely nonlinear case).

We assume that the
k
-th vector field is genuinely nonlinear (
2.8
) and set

(9.5)

\Lambda(t):=F(t)+K_{4}\Upsilon(t)

where

(9.6)

F(t)=\mathrm{TotVar}_{[0,t[}\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(\cdot,0),

\Upsilon
is the same as in (
7.4
) and
K_{4}
is a suitable constant, to be determined in the following.
Assume for a moment that we have shown that the functional
\Lambda
is monotone non-increasing; then

\mathrm{TotVar}\;\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(\cdot,0)\leq\lim_{t\to+\infty}\Lambda(t)\leq\Lambda(0)\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\delta

and this establishes (
9.3
). To show that
\Lambda
is monotone non-increasing, we first of all recall that owing to the analysis in §
7
the term
\Upsilon
is monotone non-increasing. We now separately analyze the three instances where the term
F
can vary.

Case I:
at time
\tau
a wave front
\alpha
of the family
j_{\alpha}<k
with size
s_{j}
hits the boundary.
Assume for a moment that we have established the inequality

(9.7)

\Delta F(\tau)\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s_{j}|.

Then owing to (
7.17
) we can choose the constant
K_{4}
in (
9.5
) in such a way that
\Delta\Lambda(\tau)\leq 0
. To establish (
9.7
) we first have to introduce some notation. We term
\mathbf{u}^{-}
and

\mathbf{u}^{+}
the left and the right state of
\alpha
, and denote by
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
the same state as in (
5.9
). We recall the construction in §
5.2.2
and conclude that if we solve the boundary Riemann problem (
1.1
),(
4.3
) by using the simplified boundary Riemann problem then
\Delta F(\tau)=0
and (
9.7
) is trivially satisfied. We are left to consider the case where the boundary Riemann problem (
1.1
),(
4.3
) is solved by using the accurate boundary Riemann solver: we recall the construction in §
4.6.1
and §
5.3.1
and we separately consider cases i),
\dots
, vi) in there.

i)

we have

(9.8)

\begin{split}\Delta F(\tau)&=|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{-}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\\
&\leq|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{-}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{+}))|+|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{+}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))|+|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\\
&\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|\mathbf{u}^{-}-\mathbf{u}^{+}|+\mathcal{O}(1)|\mathbf{u}^{+}-\mathbf{\hat{u}}|+|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:nc}}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)|s_{j}|+|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\end{split}

To control the second term at the right-hand side of the above formula we point out that (recalling that
\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})>0
by definition of case i))

(9.9)

\begin{split}|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))&-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:rh}}}{{\leq}}[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}|\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\\
&\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s^{\prime}_{k}|[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\delta[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}\\
&\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}-[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}|+\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}-[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{-},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}|\\
&\quad+\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{-},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}-[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{-},\xi_{k})]^{+}|\\
&\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|\mathbf{\hat{u}}-\mathbf{u}^{+}|+\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|\mathbf{u}^{+}-\mathbf{u}^{-}|+\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|s^{\prime}_{k}-\xi_{k}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:nc}}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|s_{j}|\end{split}

To establish the third to last inequality we have used the fact that
[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{-},\xi_{k})]^{+}=0
, obtained by applying (
4.58
) with
\mathbf{u}^{-}
and
\xi_{k}
in place of
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
and
\xi_{k}
, respectively. By plugging the previous chains of inequalities into (
9.8
) we get (
9.7
)

ii)

we
use again (
9.8
) and to control the second term at the right hand side we proceed as follows. If
\xi_{k}=0
then owing to (
6.2
) we have

|s^{\prime}_{k}|\leq C_{1}|s_{j}|
and this yields

|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s^{\prime}_{k}|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s_{j}|.

If
\xi_{k}>0
then owing to (
6.4
) we have
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{-})\leq 0
. We then have

\begin{split}|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|&=|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\\
&=|\mathbf{g}\circ\mathbf{g}^{-1}\circ\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{g}\circ\mathbf{g}^{-1}\circ\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\\
&\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|\mathbf{g}^{-1}\circ\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{g}^{-1}\circ\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\leq\left|\int_{s^{\prime}_{k}}^{0}\lambda_{k}\mathbf{r}_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s)ds\right|\\
&\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\int_{s^{\prime}_{k}}^{0}|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s)|ds\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:gnl}}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)\int_{s^{\prime}_{k}}^{0}\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})ds\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s^{\prime}_{k}|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{-})\leq 0}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)|s^{\prime}_{k}|[\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})-\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{-})]\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s^{\prime}_{k}||\mathbf{\hat{u}}-\mathbf{u}^{-}|\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:nc}}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)|s^{\prime}_{k}||s_{j}|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|s_{j}|\end{split}

and by plugging the above inequality into (
9.8
) we arrive at (
9.7
).

iii)

the proof is the same as at the previous point, it suffices to replace
s^{\prime}_{k}
with
\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
and use (
6.45
).

iv)

the proof of (
9.7
) is the same as in case i).

v), vi)

since there is no wave of the
k
-th family entering the domain after the interaction we have
\Delta F(\tau)=|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{-}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})|
and one can then proceed
as in (
9.8
).

Case II:
at time
\tau
a wave front
\alpha
of the
k
-th family with (signed) strength
s
hits the boundary.
Assume for a moment that we have established the inequality

(9.10)

\Delta F(\tau)\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s|([\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)]^{-}+|\xi_{k}|).

Then owing to (
7.26
) we can choose the constant
K_{4}
in (
9.5
) in such a way that
\Delta\Lambda(\tau)\leq 0
.
To establish (
9.10
) we first assume that the boundary Riemann problem (
1.1
), (
4.3
) is solved by using the accurate boundary Riemann solver, we recall the analysis in §
5.3
and we separately consider the following cases. As in
Case I
, we term
\mathbf{u}^{-}
and

\mathbf{u}^{+}
the left and the right state of
\alpha
, respectively, and denote by
\mathbf{\hat{u}}
the same state as in (
5.9
). Note that, owing to (
5.5
) we have
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+})<0
. We now separately consider cases i),
\dots
,vi) in §
4.6.1
and §
5.3.1
.

i)

We point out that

(9.11)

\begin{split}\Delta F(\tau)&=|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{-}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\\
&\leq|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{-}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{+}))|+|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{+}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))|+|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\end{split}

To control first term at the right-hand side of (
9.11
) we recall (
9.4
). In the case
s<0
we recall that owing to (
5.5
) the speed of the wave-front is
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+})
and that
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+})<0
(otherwise the wave could not hit the boundary). Since the map
s\mapsto\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{i}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s))
is monotone non-decreasing owing to (
2.8
), we conclude that

\max\{|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s))|,|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+})|\}=|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s))|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:speedwft}}}{{=}}|\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)|=[\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)]^{-}\quad\text{if $s<0$}.

Owing to (
9.4
) and recalling (
5.4
) we conclude that, regardless the sign of
s
, we have

(9.12)

|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{-}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{+}))|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s|[\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)]^{-}.

To control the second term at the right-hand side of (
9.11
) we simply point out that

(9.13)

|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{+}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|\mathbf{u}^{+}-\mathbf{\hat{u}}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:me},\eqref{e:hatu}}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)|s|([\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)]^{-}+|\xi_{k}|).

We now recall (
5.4
) and control the third term at the right-hand side of (
9.11
):

(9.14)

\begin{split}|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-&\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:dflux},s^{\prime}_{k}>0}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)|s^{\prime}_{k}|[\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}\\
&\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\delta[\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}-[\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}|+\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|[\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}-[\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s+\xi_{k})]^{+}|\\
&\quad+\mathcal{O}(1)|s^{\prime}_{k}|[\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s+\xi_{k})]^{+}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:me}}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|\mathbf{\hat{u}}-\mathbf{u}^{+}|+\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|s|([\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)]^{-}+|\xi_{k}|)+\mathcal{O}(1)|s^{\prime}_{k}|[\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s+\xi_{k})]^{+}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:hatu},\eqref{e:me},\eqref{e:msigma}}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|s|([\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)]^{-}+|\xi_{k}|)+\mathcal{O}(1)|s+\xi_{k}|[\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s+\xi_{k})]^{+}\end{split}

When
\xi_{k}=0
, the above estimate yields (
9.10
), hence to control the last term at the right-hand side of the above expression we assume
\xi_{k}\neq 0
and we separately consider the following cases: if
\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s+\xi_{k})\leq 0
then the term vanishes and the estimate is complete. We are thus left to consider the case
\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s+\xi_{k})\geq 0
: recalling that
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+})<0
and since
\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)\leq 0
then
s\leq\underline{s}(\mathbf{u}^{+})
and
\xi_{k}\geq\underline{s}(\mathbf{u}^{+})-s\geq 0
. We also point out that
\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s),\xi_{k})\leq 0
owing to (
6.4
).
We thus have

(9.15)

[\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s+\xi_{k})]^{+}=\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s+\xi_{k}\leq\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s+\xi_{k})-\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s),\xi_{k}):=g(s,\xi_{k})

We now point out that the function
g
is of class
C^{2}
and satisfies

(9.16)

g(0,\xi)=0\;\text{for every $\xi$},\qquad g(s,0)=\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)-\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lax}}}{{\leq}}0\;\text{for $s\geq 0$}.

If
s\geq 0
we can then argue as follows: since
g(s,\xi_{k})\geq 0
and
g(s,0)\leq 0
then there must be
\xi(s)\in[0,\xi_{k}]
such that

g(s,\xi(s))=0
. By using (
9.16
) and arguing as in the proof of
[
10
, Lemma 2.5]
we then get

\begin{split}|g(s,\xi_{k})|&\leq\int_{\xi(s)}^{\xi_{k}}\left|\frac{\partial g}{\partial\xi}(s,z)\right|dz\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\int_{\xi(s)}^{\xi_{k}}|s|dz\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s||\xi_{k}-\xi(s)|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s||\xi_{k}|\;\text{for $s\geq 0$}.\end{split}

This allows us to control the last term at the right-hand side of (
9.14
) and concludes the proof of (
9.10
) in the case
s\geq 0
.
If
s<0
we recall that
s^{\prime}_{k}>0
because we are in case i). Owing to (
6.5
), this implies that, if
s+\xi_{k}\leq 0
, then

|s_{k}+\xi_{k}|\leq|s|([\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)]^{-}+|\xi_{k}|)

and by plugging this estimate into (
9.14
) we arrive at (
9.10
). We are thus left to control the last term at the right hand-side of (
9.14
) in the case
s<0
,
s+\xi_{k}>0
, which yields
\xi_{k}>-s>0.
We now consider the function

h(s,\xi):=[s+\xi]g(s,\xi),

where
g
is the same as in (
9.15
) and point out that
h
satisfies

h(0,\xi)=0,\quad h(s,-s)=0\quad\text{for every $\xi$, $s$}

and by arguing as in the proof of
[
10
, Lemma 2.5]
we then get

|h(s,\xi)|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s||\xi+s|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s||\xi|\quad\text{for $\xi>0>s$}

and this allows us to control the last term at the right-hand side of (
9.14
) and concludes the proof of (
9.10
).

ii)

we use (
9.11
) and to control the first and second term at the right-hand side we use (
9.12
) and (
9.13
), respectively. To control the third term at the right hand-side of (
9.11
) we recall that, since we are in case ii),
\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\leq s^{\prime}_{k}<0
and hence

0\leq\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k}))\leq\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})

and by using (
9.4
) we get

\begin{split}|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))&-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})))|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s^{\prime}_{k}|[\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})]^{+}\\
&=\mathcal{O}(1)|s^{\prime}_{k}|\big|[\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})]^{+}-[\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+})]^{+}\big|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|s^{\prime}_{k}||\mathbf{\hat{u}}-\mathbf{u}^{+}|\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:hatu},\eqref{e:me}}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|s|([\varsigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s)]^{-}+|\xi_{k}|)\end{split}

In the above expression we have used the equality
[\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+})]^{+}=0
: this follows from the fact that the wave-front
\alpha
has negative speed and the Lax entropy condition (if
s>0
) and the equality (
5.5
) (if
s<0
).

iii)

the proof is the same as at the previous point, it suffices to replace
s^{\prime}_{k}
with
\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})
and use (
6.45
).

iv)

we proceed as in point i)

v), vi)

in these cases there is no wave front of the
k
-th family entering the domain after the interaction and hence we have
\Delta F(\tau)=|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{-}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\hat{\mathbf{u}}))|
and we can argue as in (
9.11
).

To establish (
9.10
) in the case where we use the simplified boundary Riemann solver instead of the accurate Riemann solver we can use the exact same analysis since the trace of the approximate solution after the interaction is the same as in the case where we use the accurate Riemann solver.

Case III:
at time
\tau
the datum
\mathbf{u}_{bm}
has a discontinuity. We term
\mathbf{u}_{bm}^{+}
and
\mathbf{u}_{bm}^{-}
the right and left limits
\mathbf{u}_{bm}(\tau^{+})
and
\mathbf{u}_{bm}(\tau^{-})
, respectively,
\mathbf{u}^{+}
and
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
the traces of
\mathbf{u}_{m}(t,\cdot)
for
t=\tau^{-}
and
t=\tau^{+}
, respectively, and
\mathbf{\hat{u}}

the same state as in (
5.9
). Assume we have established the inequality

(9.17)

\Delta F(\tau)\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|\mathbf{u}_{bm}^{+}-\mathbf{u}_{bm}^{-}|;

then owing to (
7.37
) we can choose the constant
K_{4}
in (
9.5
) in such a way that
\Delta\Upsilon(\tau)\leq 0
. To establish (
9.17
) we point out that

(9.18)

\begin{split}\Delta F(\tau)&=|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{+}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}))|\leq|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{u}^{+}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))|+|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})))|\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:jd1}}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)|\mathbf{u}_{bm}^{+}-\mathbf{u}_{bm}^{-}|+|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}))|\end{split}

To control the second term at the right-hand side of the above expression we point out that, by separately considering cases i),
\dots
,vi) in §
4.6.1
and §
5.3.1
we conclude that
\mathbf{\bar{u}}=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\widetilde{s}_{k})
, where the value attained by
\widetilde{s}_{k}
in the various cases is as follows:

i)

\widetilde{s}_{k}=s^{\prime}_{k}>0
;

ii)

\widetilde{s}_{k}=s^{\prime}_{k}\leq 0
;

iii)

\widetilde{s}_{k}=\bar{s}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})\leq 0
, and hence
s^{\prime}_{k}<\widetilde{s}_{k}\leq 0
;

iv)

\widetilde{s}_{k}=s^{\prime}_{k}>0
;

v), vi)

\widetilde{s}_{k}=0
;

If
\widetilde{s}_{k}=0
then the second term at the right-hand side of (
9.18
) vanishes and this completes the proof of (
9.17
).
If
\widetilde{s}_{k}>0
, then
\widetilde{s}_{k}=s^{\prime}_{k}
and by applying Lemma
9.3
we get

(9.19)

\begin{split}|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-&\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\bar{\mathbf{u}}))|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\widetilde{s}_{k})]^{+}|\widetilde{s}_{k}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widetilde{s}_{k}=s^{\prime}_{k}}}{{=}}\mathcal{O}(1)[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}|s^{\prime}_{k}|\\
&\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}-[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}|+\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},s^{\prime}_{k})]^{+}-[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},\xi_{k})]^{+}|\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:jd1}}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|\mathbf{u}_{bm}^{+}-\mathbf{u}_{bm}^{-}|.\end{split}

To establish the second inequality in the previous expression, we have used the equality
[\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+},\xi_{k})]^{+}=0
, which follows from (
4.57
) applied with
\mathbf{u}^{+}
and
\xi_{k}
in place of
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
and
\xi^{\prime}_{k}
, respectively. By plugging (
9.19
) into (
9.18
) we establish (
9.17
).
If
\widetilde{s}_{k}<0
, then by applying Lemma
9.3
we get

(9.20)

|\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\hat{u}}))-\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}))|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)|\widetilde{s}_{k}|\max\{|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})|,|\lambda_{k}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\widetilde{s}_{k}))|\}.

Next, we recall that the inequality
\widetilde{s}_{k}<0
implies that we are in either case ii) or case iii) in §
5.2.1
, and this in turn yields

(9.21)

|\widetilde{s}_{k}|\leq|s^{\prime}_{k}|,\quad\max\{|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})|,|\lambda_{k}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}},\widetilde{s}_{k}))|\}=|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})|=\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})>0.

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+})\leq 0
we can then control the right-hand side of (
9.20
) by arguing as follows:

|s^{\prime}_{k}|[\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})]^{+}\leq|s^{\prime}_{k}||[\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\hat{u}})]^{+}-[\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+})]^{+}|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|\hat{\mathbf{u}}-\mathbf{u}^{+}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:jd1}}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)\delta|\mathbf{u}_{bm}^{+}-\mathbf{u}_{bm}^{-}|.

If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+})>0
then
\xi_{k}=0
by (
4.57
) applied with
\mathbf{u}^{+}
and
\xi_{k}
in place of
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
and
\xi^{\prime}_{k}
, respectively, and then owing to (
7.30
) we get
|s^{\prime}_{k}|\leq C_{6}|\mathbf{u}_{bm}^{+}-\mathbf{u}_{bm}^{-}|
and by (
9.21
) this allows us to control the right hand-side of (
9.20
). This concludes the proof of (
9.17
) and hence of Lemma
9.2
in the genuinely nonlinear case.
∎

Corollary 9.4
.

Under the same assumptions as in the statement of Theorem
1.1
we have (
9.1
).

Proof.

We point out that that
\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(\cdot,0)
is uniformly bounded because so is
\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}
by (
7.2
). Owing to Helly-Kolmogorov Compactness Theorem, the total variation bound (
9.3
) implies that
\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(\cdot,0)
converges up to subsequences in
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
to some limit function
\boldsymbol{\rho}
. By the analysis in
[
10
, p.144-5]
, we have

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\partial_{t}\varphi\ \mathbf{g}\circ\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x)+\partial_{x}\varphi\ \mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x)dxdt+\int_{0}^{+\infty}\varphi(t,0)\ \mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,0)dt\right)=0

for every
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(]0,+\infty[\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
and this yields
\boldsymbol{\rho}=\mathbf{f}\circ\mathbf{v}(\cdot,0)
using the fact that
\mathbf{v}
is of bounded total variation and hence has a trace at
x=0
, where
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}\circ\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{u}
is the same as in (
8.1
). Also, up to subsequences we have (
9.1
).
∎

9.3.
Construction and analysis of the set
\widetilde{E}
in the genuinely nonlinear case

The set
\widetilde{E}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
is defined as in (
9.2
). We recall that the set
D
is the set where the boundary datum
\mathbf{v}_{b}
is discontinuous and we now rigorously define the sets

F
,
H
and
G
.

9.3.1.
Construction of the set
F

For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
we construct a sequence of measures
\mu_{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
by proceeding as follows: first,
we term
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{n_{m}}
the times at which a wave front of the
\varepsilon_{m}
-wave front-tracking approximation
\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon_{m}}
hits the boundary;
next, for every
\tau_{\alpha}
,
\alpha=1,\dots,n_{m}
, we term
j_{\alpha}
and
s_{\alpha}
the family and the size of the hitting wave, and set

(9.22)

\chi_{\alpha}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A|s_{\alpha}|&j_{\alpha}<k\\
|s_{\alpha}|([\varsigma_{\alpha}(\mathbf{u}_{\alpha},s_{\alpha})]^{-}+|\xi_{k}|)&j_{\alpha}=k.\\
\end{array}\right.

Finally, we set

(9.23)

\mu_{m}:=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{m}}\chi_{\alpha}\delta_{t=\tau_{\alpha}},

where
\delta_{t=\tau_{\alpha}}
denotes the Dirac delta concentrated at
\tau_{\alpha}
.
Note that by using the specific piecewise constant structure of
\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}
and (
7.42
) we get

|\mu_{m}|(]0,T[)\leq\mathcal{O}(1)\mathrm{D\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}}(]0,T[\times\mathbb{R}_{+})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:settedue}}}{{\leq}}\mathcal{O}(1)T\delta,\quad\text{for every $T>0$}.

This implies that, up to subsequences,
\mu_{m}
converges weakly-
∗
in the sense of measures on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
to some limit measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
as
m\to+\infty
. We term
F
the set of atoms of
\mu
, that is

(9.24)

F:=\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:\mu(\{t\})>0\}.

Note that
F
is at most countable (and henceforth
\mathcal{L}^{1}
-negligible) by the local finiteness of
\mu
.

9.3.2.
Construction of the set
G

We employ the same construction of
maximal
\nu_{n}
-shock fronts
of the
k
-th family as in
[
10
, page 219, step 2]
. In particular, this yields the construction of a countable family
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of shock curves for the limit function
\mathbf{u}
, obtained as limits of
maximal
\nu_{n}
-shock fronts
of the approximate solution, which has strength larger of equal than
\nu_{n}/2
. Note that this shock curves are contained in the
closed
set
[0,+\infty[\times[0,+\infty[
and that there may be
0
-speed shocks exactly located at the origin.
We set

(9.25)

G:=\big\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:\,y_{n}(t)=0,\;y^{\prime}_{n}(t)=0\;\text{for some $n\in\mathbb{N}$}\big\}.

9.3.3.
Construction of the set
H

We consider the same measure
\mu^{IC}\in\mathcal{M}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,+\infty[)
of interaction and cancellation as in
[
10
, formula (10.68)]
, constructed by using
[
10
, formula (7.96)]
.
We term
H
the set of atoms of
\mu^{IC}
on the
x=0
axis, namely

(9.26)

H:=\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:\;\mu^{IC}(\{(t,0)\})>0\}.

Note that
H
is at most countable (and henceforth
\mathcal{L}^{1}
-negligible) by the local finiteness of the measure
\mu^{IC}
.

9.3.4.
Analysis of the set
\widetilde{E}

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
\widetilde{E}
be the same as in (
9.2
) and set

(9.27)

\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t):=\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}\mathbf{u}(t,x),

where
\mathbf{u}
is the same as in (
8.1
), and

(9.28)

\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t):=\lim_{x\to 0}\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x).

If
t\notin\widetilde{E}
then

(9.29)

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t)=\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t).

Proof.

We fix
t\notin\widetilde{E}
, recall footnote
19
at page
19
, argue by contradiction and assume that there is a constant
\iota
such that

(9.30)

|\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t)-\lim_{x\to 0}\mathbf{u}(t,x)|\geq\iota>0.

Next, we proceed according to the following steps.

Step 1:
we point out that (
8.1
) implies that, up to subsequences,
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x)
converges to
\mathbf{u}(t,x)
for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Owing to (
9.30
), this in turn implies that there are two sequences
\{x_{m}\}
and
\{y_{m}\}
such that
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}
is continuous at both
x_{m}
and
y_{m}
and

(9.31)

y_{m}>x_{m}\geq 0,\;|\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x_{m})-\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,y_{m})|\geq\frac{\iota}{2}\;\text{for every $m$},\quad\lim_{m\to+\infty}y_{m}=\lim_{m\to+\infty}x_{m}=0.

Step 2:
by recalling the structure of the function
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}
we conclude that the last inequality in (
9.31
) implies that the space interval
]x_{m},y_{m}[

is crossed at time
t
by an amount of wave fronts such that their combined strength satisfies

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\alpha$ wave in}\\
\,]x_{m},y_{m}[\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|\geq\frac{L\iota}{2}

for a suitable constant
L>0
.
Up to subsequences, we can then assume that at least one of the following cases is verified.

Case 1:
the wave fronts of the families
1,\dots,k-1
satisfy

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\alpha$ wave in}\\
\,]x_{m},y_{m}[\\
i_{\alpha}=1,\dots,k-1\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|\geq\frac{L\iota}{10}.

Then there are two possibilities:

i)

the combined strength of the wave fronts hitting the boundary in the interval
]t,t+y_{m}/c[
, where
c
is the same as in (
2.6
), is at least

L\iota/20
. By recalling (
9.22
) and (
9.23
) this implies

(9.32)

\mu_{m}\left(\left[t,t+\frac{y_{m}}{c}\right[\right)\geq\frac{LA\iota}{20}\quad\text{for every $m\in\mathbb{N}$}.

We claim that (
9.32
) yields
\mu(\{t\})>0
, that is
t\in F\subseteq\widetilde{E}
, and thus contradicts the assumption
t\notin\widetilde{E}
. To show that
\mu(\{t\})>0
we
use the outer regularity of
\mu
(see
[
2
, Proposition 1.43 (ii)]
), which in particular implies that to show that
\mu(\{t\})>0
it suffices to show that

(9.33)

\mu(\mathcal{O})\geq\frac{LA\iota}{20},\quad\text{for every open set $\mathcal{O}$ such that $t\in\mathcal{O}$}.

We now fix
\widetilde{m}
such that
[t,t+y_{\widetilde{m}}/c]\subseteq\mathcal{O}
, which implies
\mu([t,t+y_{\widetilde{m}}/c])\leq\mu(\mathcal{O})
.
Owing to the upper semicontinuity of the measure of compact sets with respect to weak
∗
convergence (see
[
2
, formula (1.8)]
) we also have that

(9.34)

\limsup_{m\to+\infty}\mu_{m}([t,t+y_{\widetilde{m}}/c])\leq\mu([t,t+y_{\widetilde{m}}/c])\leq\mu(\mathcal{O}).

By extracting, if needed, a subsequence from
\{y_{m}\}
, we can assume that
[t,t+y_{m}/c]\subseteq[t,t+y_{\widetilde{m}}/c]
for every
m\geq\widetilde{m}
, which implies

\frac{LA\iota}{20}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:abbagrande}}}{{\leq}}\limsup_{m\to+\infty}\mu_{m}([t,t+y_{m}/c])\leq\limsup_{m\to+\infty}\mu_{m}([t,t+y_{\widetilde{m}}/c])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:limsup}}}{{\leq}}\mu(\mathcal{O}),

which eventually yields (
9.33
).

ii)

an amount of wave fronts of total strength at least
L\iota/20
is cancelled by the interactions with other waves on the set
]t,t+y_{m}/c[\times]0,y_{m}[
. By recalling
[
10
, formula (7.96)]
this implies that

(9.35)

\mu^{IC}_{m}([t,t+y_{m}/c]\times[0,y_{m}])\geq L\iota/20.

and by arguing as in item i) above this implies
\mu^{IC}(\{(t,0)\})>0
and hence
t\in H\subseteq\widetilde{E}
, which contradicts the assumption
t\notin\widetilde{E}
.

Case 2:
the wave fronts of the families
k+1,\dots,N
satisfy

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\alpha$ wave in}\\
\,]x_{m},y_{m}[\\
i_{\alpha}=k+1,\dots,N\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|\geq\frac{L\iota}{10}.

Then there are two possibilities:

i)

an amount of waves of combined strength at least
L\iota/20
is generated on the interval
]t-y_{m}/c,t[
, where
c
is the same as in (
2.6
), either at discontinuity points of
\mathbf{u}_{bm}
or when a wave hits the boundary. By arguing as in
Case 1
, item i) we conclude that either
\mu^{IC}(\{(t,0)\})>0
or
t
is a discontinuity point of
\mathbf{u}_{b}
. Either way, we find a contradiction with the definition (
9.2
) of
\widetilde{E}
and the assumption
t\notin\widetilde{E}
.

ii)

an amount of wave fronts of total strength at least
L\iota/20
is generated by the interactions with other waves on the set
]t-y_{m}/c,t[\times]0,y_{m}[
. By recalling
[
10
, formula (7.96)]
this implies that
\mu^{IC}_{m}([t-y_{m}/c,t]\times[0,y_{m}])\geq L\iota/20
and by arguing as in item i) of
Step 1
this in turn implies
\mu^{IC}(\{(t,0)\})>0
, which contradicts the assumption
t\notin\widetilde{E}
.

Case 3:
there is
\upsilon>0
such that the combined amount of the waves of the
k
-th family with negative speed
\upsilon
-bounded away from
0
satisfies

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\alpha$ wave in}\\
\,]x_{m},y_{m}[\\
i_{\alpha}=k,\,\varsigma_{\alpha}<-\upsilon\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|\geq\frac{L\iota}{10}.

Then there are three possibilities:

i)

the combined strength of the wave fronts hitting the boundary in the interval
]t,t+2y_{m}/\upsilon[
is at least

L\iota/30
. This is ruled out by arguing as in
Case 1
, item i).

ii)

an amount of wave fronts of total strength at least
L\iota/30
is cancelled by the interactions with other waves on the set
]t,t+2y_{m}/\upsilon[\times]0,y_{m}[
. This is ruled out by arguing as in
Case 1
, item i).

iii)

in the time interval
]t,t+2y_{m}/\upsilon[
an amount of waves of total strength at least
L\iota/30
increases its speed from at most
-\upsilon
to at least
-\upsilon/2
owing to the interaction with other waves. By the Lipschitz continuity of the map
\mathbf{u}\mapsto\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u})
this implies that

\mu^{IC}_{m}([t,t+2y_{m}/\upsilon]~\times~[0,y_{m}])~\geq~\mathcal{O}(1)L\iota\upsilon~>~0
and by arguing as in item i) of
Step 1
this in turn implies
\mu^{IC}(\{(t,0)\})>0
, which contradicts the assumption
t\notin\widetilde{E}
.

Case 4:
there is
\upsilon>0
such that the combined amount of the waves of the
k
-th family with positive speed
\upsilon
-bounded away from
0
satisfies

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\alpha$ wave in}\\
\,]x_{m},y_{m}[\\
i_{\alpha}=k,\,\varsigma_{\alpha}>\upsilon\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|\geq\frac{L\iota}{10}.

This case is ruled out by combining the arguments in
Case 2
and
Case 3
.

Case 5:
there is a sequence
\eta_{m}
such that
\eta_{m}>0
for every
m
,
\lim_{m\to+\infty}\eta_{m}=0
and

(9.36)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\alpha$ wave in}\\
\,]x_{m},y_{m}[\\
i_{\alpha}=k,\,|\varsigma_{\alpha}|<\eta_{m}\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|\geq\frac{L\iota}{10}.

Assume for a moment that there are
\eta>0
,
\nu>0
such that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\alpha$ wave in}\\
\,]x_{m},y_{m}[\\
i_{\alpha}=k+1,\dots,N\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\alpha$ wave in}\\
\,]x_{m},y_{m}[\\
i_{\alpha}=1,\dots,k-1\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\alpha$ wave in}\\
\,]x_{m},y_{m}[\\
i_{\alpha}=k,|\varsigma_{\alpha}|\geq\eta\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|\geq\nu,

then we can argue as in
Cases
1,\dots,4
before. Hence, we can assume with no loss of generality that the
strength of all the waves different from the one in (
9.36
) vanishes in the
m\to+\infty
limit.
Up to subsequences, we know that one of the following two possibilities is verified.

Case 5A:
there is
\nu>0
such that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
there is at least one front comprised between
x_{m}
and
y_{m}
with strength greater or equal than
\nu>0
and speed in modulus smaller or equal than
\eta_{m}
.
Since owing to Lemma
8.2
the maximal strength
C_{9}r_{\varepsilon_{m}}
of the rarefaction fronts vanishes in the
m\to+\infty
limit, this front must be a shock front.
By the construction of maximal
\nu
-shock fronts given in
[
10
, p. 219]
and the definition (
9.25
) of
G
, this implies
t\in G\subseteq\widetilde{E}
and hence contradicts the assumption
t\notin\widetilde{E}
.

Case 5B:
the maximal strength of the wave-fronts confined between
x_{m}
and
y_{m}
vanishes in the
m\to+\infty
limit.
Since the strength of all the waves of the
j
-th family,
j\neq k
, vanishes in the
m\to+\infty
limit, the values
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x)
with
x\in]x_{m},y_{m}[
get
closer and closer to the curve
\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x_{m}),\cdot)
in the
m\to+\infty
limit. Since also the maximal speed of the wave fronts of
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,\cdot)
comprised between
x_{m}
and
y_{m}
vanishes in the
m\to+\infty
, we conclude that

\lim_{m\to+\infty}|\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x)-\mathbf{t}_{k}\big(\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x_{m}),\bar{s}(\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x_{m})\big)|=0\quad\text{for every $x\in[x_{m},y_{m}]$},

which contradicts (
9.31
). ∎

9.4.
Definition
1.1
in the case
t\notin\widetilde{E}

In this paragraph we verify the boundary condition in the statement of Theorem
9.1
on the set
\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\widetilde{E}
, where
\widetilde{E}
is the same as in (
9.2
). In other words, we show that for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
t\notin\widetilde{E}
, we have
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{v}_{b}(t)
in the sense of Definition
1.1
, provided
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)
is the trace of the limit function
\mathbf{v}
as in the statement of Theorem
9.1
. This is a consequence of Lemma
9.6
and Lemma
9.7
below.

For simplicity in this paragraph and in the following one we assume (
2.11
). The case of assumption C) in Hypothesis
5
is analogous. The case of assumption B) is slightly more delicate and is explicitly discussed in §
9.6.2
since the most prominent example where assumption B) is satisfied are the Navier-Stokes and viscous MHD equations in Eulerian coordinates when the fluid velocity vanishes, and in that case the
k
-th characteristic field is linearly degenerate.

Let
\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}
be the same as in (
9.28
) then owing to (
4.53
) we have

\boldsymbol{\beta}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t),\xi^{m}_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi^{m}_{k}(t)),\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\varepsilon_{m}}(t))=\mathbf{0}_{N}\quad\text{for a.e. $t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}$}

and suitable functions
\xi^{m}_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi^{m}_{k}(t)
satisfying
|\xi^{m}_{i}|\leq\delta
for every
i=\ell+1,\dots,k
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Note that owing to (
5.1
) we have that, up to subsequences,

(9.37)

\mathbf{u}_{b}^{\varepsilon_{m}}(t)\to\mathbf{u}_{b}(t),\quad\text{for a.e. $t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}$}.

By combining (
9.37
) and (
9.29
) with the Implicit Function Theorem we conclude that for a.e.
t\notin\widetilde{E}
we have that
\xi^{m}_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi^{m}_{k}(t)
converge as
m\to+\infty
to suitable functions
\xi_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi_{k}(t)
satisfying

(9.38)

\boldsymbol{\beta}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t),\xi_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi_{k}(t)),\mathbf{u}_{b}(t))=\mathbf{0}_{N}\quad\text{for a.e. $t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}$},

provided
\mathbf{\bar{u}}
is the same as in (
9.27
).

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t)
be the same as in (
9.27
) and
\xi_{k}(t)
the same as in (
9.38
). For a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
t\notin\widetilde{E}
, satisfying
\xi_{k}(t)\neq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))
, we have that
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t):=\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))
satisfies
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{v}_{b}(t)
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.In particular, property ii) is satisfied provided
\mathbf{\underline{v}}=\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)
.

Proof.

We recall Remark
5.2
and in particular that the approximation
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}
satisfies (
4.57
) with
\mathbf{u}^{+}=\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t)
and
\xi_{k}=\xi_{k}^{m}(t)
respectively. Up to subsequences we can assume that one of the following condition holds true:

a)

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t))>0
and
\xi^{m}_{k}(t)=0
for every
m
;

b)

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t))\leq 0
and
\xi^{m}_{k}(t)\leq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m})(t)
or every
m
.

Condition a) above implies
\xi_{k}(t)=0
, which by (
4.50
) yields
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{p}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t),\xi_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi_{k-1}(t),0)=\mathbf{0}_{N}
and by plugging this equality into (
4.49
) and recalling (
9.38
) we arrive at

(9.39)

\boldsymbol{\beta}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t),\xi_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi_{k-1}(t)),\mathbf{u}_{b}(t))=\mathbf{0}_{N},

where
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}
is defined in (
4.40
). By the construction of
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}
, there is a solution of (
3.1
) with initial datum
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t),\xi_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi_{k-1}(t))
which converges exponentially fast to
\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t)
as
y\to+\infty
. Owing to (
9.39
), this implies that there is a boundary layer
\mathbf{w}
satisfying (
4.7
) with
\mathbf{\underline{u}}=\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t)
and going back to the variable
\mathbf{v}
we conclude that
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t):=\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))
satisfies property ii) in Definition
1.1
with

\mathbf{\underline{v}}=\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)
.

Owing to (
9.29
) condition b) above yields
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))\leq 0
and
\xi_{k}(t)\leq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))
. Since
\xi_{k}(t)\neq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))
by assumption, we conclude that
\xi_{k}(t)<\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))
. If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))<0
then by recalling case vi) in §
4.6.1
we conclude that
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t):=\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))
satisfies property ii) in Definition
1.1
with
\mathbf{\underline{v}}=\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)
. If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))=0
then
\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))=\bar{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))=0
by (
4.13
) and (
4.15
), and by recalling case iii) in §
4.6.1
we conclude the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t)
be the same as in (
9.27
) and
\xi_{k}(t)
the same as in (
9.38
). For a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
t\notin\widetilde{E}
, satisfying
\xi_{k}(t)=\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))
we have that
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t):=\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))
satisfies
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{v}_{b}(t)
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.In particular, property ii) is satisfied provided
\mathbf{\underline{v}}(t)=\mathbf{v}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t),\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))))
.

Proof.

We recall cases a) and b) in the proof of Lemma
9.6
. In case a), we obtain
\xi_{k}(t)=0
, which implies
\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))=\xi_{k}(t)=0
and hence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t),\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))))=\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t)
. Owing to (
9.39
), and arguing as in the proof of Lemma
9.6
, this yields the desired conclusion.

We now focus on case b). If
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))=0
then
\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}})=0
, which yields on the one hand the equality
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t),\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t)))=\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t)
, on the other
the equalities
\xi_{k}(t)=\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))=0
and hence (by arguing as in the proof of Lemma
9.6
) the equality (
9.39
) and from this we arrive at the desired conclusion. Conversely, if
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))<0
we can combine (
9.38
) with the same argument as in case v) in §
4.6.1
and conclude the proof of the lemma.
∎

9.5.
Definition
1.1
in the case
t\in\widetilde{E}

In this paragraph we verify the boundary condition in the statement of Theorem
9.1
on the set
\widetilde{E}
. Namely, we show that for a.e.
t\in\widetilde{E}
, we have
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{v}_{b}(t)
in the sense of Definition
1.1
, provided
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)
is the same as in the statement of Theorem
9.1
. Since the sets
D,F
and
H
in the definition (
9.2
) of
\widetilde{E}
are negligible, this is a consequence of Lemma
9.10
below. We recall that in this paragraph, as in the previous one, to ease the exposition we assume (
2.11
).

Owing to the construction in §
9.3.2
we have that if
t\in G
then there is
\nu>0
and a sequence of maximal
\nu
-shock fronts
\{y_{m}\}
such that
y_{m}\to y
in
C^{0}
as
m\to+\infty
for some Lipschitz continuous curve
y
such that
y(t)=0
,
y^{\prime}(t)=0
. We now fix
t\in G
and term
\mathbf{u}^{-}_{m}(t)
and
\mathbf{u}^{+}_{m}(t)
the left and right state of the shock curve
y_{m}
at time
t
. We recall (
9.27
) and by arguing as in the proof of
[
10
, formula (10.80)]
and of
[
10
, formula (10.66)]
we get

(9.40)

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbf{u}^{+}_{m}(t)=\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t),\quad\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))\leq 0\;\text{for a.e. $t\in G$.}

Owing to the second condition in (
9.40
), for a.e.
t\in G
by setting

(9.41)

\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t)=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t),\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t)))

we get that
\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t)
and
\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t)
are the left and the right state, respectively, of a
0
-speed Lax admissible shock.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t)
be the same as in (
9.41
), then

(9.42)

\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t)=\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbf{u}^{-}_{m}(t)\;\text{for a.e. $t\in G$.}

Proof.

By the definition of
\mathbf{u}^{+}_{m}(t)
and
\mathbf{u}^{-}_{m}(t)
we have

(9.43)

\mathbf{u}^{-}_{m}(t)=\mathbf{t}_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+}_{m}(t),s_{m}(t))\;\text{for a.e. $t\in G$}

and for some
s_{m}(t)\in[-\delta,\delta].
Note that, owing to the definition of
G
and of
\nu
-shock front,
s_{m}(t)\geq\nu(t)/2
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and some
\nu(t)>0
.
We now fix any such
t\in G
and point out that, up to subsequences (which to ease the notation we do not relabel),
s_{m}(t)
converges to to some limit
s(t)\geq\nu(t)/2>0
. By the continuity of the function
\sigma_{k}
defined by (
4.11
) and by recalling (
9.40
) we arrive at

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+}_{m}(t),s_{m}(t))=\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t),s(t)).

On the other hand, by the analysis in
[
10
, p.227]
we get that
\lim_{m\to+\infty}\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+}_{m}(t),s_{m}(t))=0
, which combined with (
9.40
) yields

s(t)=\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))
. By passing to the limit in (
9.43
) and recalling (
9.41
) we eventually arrive at (
9.42
).
∎

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t)
and
\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t)
be the same as in (
9.28
) and in (
9.41
), respectively; then

(9.44)

\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t)=\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t)\quad\text{for a.e. $t\in G$}.

Proof.

We fix
t\in G\setminus F\cup H\cup D
satisfying (
9.42
) and establish (
9.44
). Since the sets
F
,
H
and
D
are all negligible, this provides the proof of the lemma. We argue by contradiction and assume that there is
\iota>0
such that

|\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t)-\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbf{u}^{-}_{m}(t)|\geq\iota.

In particular, this implies that there is a sequence of points
\{\widetilde{y}_{m}\}
(to ease the notation, we do not indicate the dependence on
t
) such that

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\widetilde{y}_{m}=0
,
0<\widetilde{y}_{m}<y_{m}
,
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}
is continuous at
(t,\widetilde{y}_{m})
,
|\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,\widetilde{y}_{m})-\mathbf{u}^{-}_{m}|
vanishes in the
m\to+\infty
limit, and therefore

|\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,\widetilde{y}_{m})-\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t)|\geq\iota/2\quad\text{for every $m$}.

We can then construct a sequence of points
\{x_{m}\}
such that
0<x_{m}<\widetilde{y}_{m}
,
\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,\cdot)
is continuous at
x_{m}
and

\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x_{m})
is arbitrarily close to
\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t)
, and hence

|\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,\widetilde{y}_{m})-\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,x_{m})|\geq\iota/4\quad\text{for every $m$.}

To show that this implies
t\in F\cup H\cup D
and hence find a contradiction with the assumptions of the present lemma we can then argue as in the proof of Lemma
9.5
, the only point that requires a different argument is the handling of
Case 5A
.
Assume therefore that we are in
Case 5
, that all the waves different from the one in (
9.36
) vanish in the
m\to+\infty
limit and that there is
\widetilde{\nu}>0
such that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
there is at least one shock-front of the
k
-th family comprised between
x_{m}
(on the left) and
\widetilde{y}_{m}
(on the right) and with strength at least
\widetilde{\nu}
and with speed vanishing in the
m\to+\infty
limit. Up to subsequences, we can then assume that one of the following conditions is satisfied.

•

The speed of the shock located at
y_{m}
is bounded away from
0
, uniformly in
m
. Since the strength of this shock is at least
\nu/2
by the construction of maximal
\nu
-shock front in
[
10
, p. 219]
we can handle this case by arguing as in
Case 3
and
Case 4
in the proof of Lemma
9.5
to conclude that in this case
t\in F\cup H\cup D
, which yields a contradiction.

•

The speed of the shock located at
y_{m}
vanishes in the
m\to+\infty
limit. Since
\mathbf{u}^{-}_{m}
is the left state of a shock with strength at least
\nu/2
and speed vanishing in the
m\to+\infty
limit, the genuine nonlinearity of the
k
-th characteristic field implies that
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{-}_{m})\geq\mathcal{O}(1)\nu>0
for
m
sufficiently large and this in turn yields
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,\widetilde{y}_{m}))\geq\mathcal{O}(1)\nu>0
. On the other side, by assumption between
x_{m}
and
\widetilde{y}_{m}

there is a shock with strength at least
\widetilde{\nu}>0
and speed vanishing in the
m\to+\infty
limit. By the Lax entropy condition, this implies that there is a sequence
\{z_{m}\}\subseteq]x_{m},\widetilde{y}_{m}[
such that
z_{m}
is just at the right of the shock and
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,z_{m})\leq-\mathcal{O}(1)\widetilde{\nu}<0
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We claim that this contradicts the assumption
that the total strength of all the waves different from the ones in (
9.36
) vanishes in the
m\to+\infty
limit. Indeed, since
z_{m}<\widetilde{y}_{m}
,
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,z_{m})\leq-\mathcal{O}(1)\widetilde{\nu}<0
,
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon_{m}}(t,\widetilde{y}_{m}))\geq\mathcal{O}(1)\nu>0
and the total strength of the wave-fronts of the
j
-th family,
j\neq k
, in
]z_{m},\widetilde{y}_{m}[
vanishes in the
in the
m\to+\infty
limit then there is
\mathcal{O}(1)\nu>0
and a group
\mathcal{I}_{m}
of rarefaction wave-fronts of the
k
-th family such that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{m}\\
i_{\alpha}=k,s_{\alpha}<0\end{subarray}}|s_{\alpha}|\geq\mathcal{O}(1)\nu,\quad\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{u}^{+}_{m\alpha})\geq\mathcal{O}(1)\nu>0\;\text{for every $\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{m}$ },

where
\mathbf{u}^{+}_{m\alpha}
represents the state at the right-hand side of the wave-front
\alpha
. This however contradicts the assumption that the total strength of all the waves different from the ones in (
9.36
) vanishes in the
m\to+\infty
limit and hence concludes the proof of the lemma.

∎

Lemma 9.10
.

For a.e.
t\in G
the trace
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t):=\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}\mathbf{v}(t,x)
satisfies
\mathbf{\bar{v}}(t)\sim_{\mathbf{D}}\mathbf{v}_{b}(t)
, in the sense of Definition
1.1
.
In particular, conditions i) and ii) are satisfied provided
\mathbf{\underline{v}}(t):=\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t))
and
\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t)
is the same as in (
9.41
) and satisfies (
9.42
) and (
9.44
).

Proof.

We proceed according to the following steps.

Step 1
: we establish property ii) in Definition
1.1
, provided
\mathbf{\underline{v}}:=\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t))
and
\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t)
is the same as in (
9.41
). By construction, we have that
20
20

20

We recall that in §
9.5
, as in §
9.4
, we assume (
2.11
) and hence focus on case A in Hypothesis
5
for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}

(9.45)

\boldsymbol{\beta}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t),\xi^{m}_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi^{m}_{k}(t)),\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon_{m}}_{b}(t))=\mathbf{0}_{N},

with
\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t)
and
\xi^{m}_{k}(t)
satisfying (
4.57
). Owing to (
9.37
) we have that
\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbf{u}_{bm}(t)=\mathbf{u}_{b}(t)
for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and by (
9.44
) we can pass to the limit in (
9.45
) and conclude that for a.e.
t\in G
we have that

\xi^{m}_{1}(t),\dots,\xi^{m}_{k}(t)
converge as
m\to+\infty
to some
\xi_{1}(t),\dots,\xi_{k}(t)
satisfying

(9.46)

\boldsymbol{\beta}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t),\xi_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi_{k}(t)),\mathbf{u}_{b}(t))=\mathbf{0}_{N}.

Owing to (
4.57
) for a.e.
t\in G
we can assume that up to subsequences one of the following conditions is satisfied for every
m
.

•

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t))>0
and
\xi^{m}_{k}(t)=0
, which by (
9.44
) yields
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t))\geq 0
and
\xi_{k}(t)=0
. Owing to (
4.50
) and (
4.49
), this implies

\boldsymbol{\phi}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t),\xi_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi_{k}(t))=\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t),\xi_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi_{k-1}(t))

and by plugging this equality into (
9.46
), recalling the definition (
4.40
) of
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}
and arguing as in the proof of Lemma
9.6
this yields the existence of a boundary layer
\mathbf{w}
satisfying (
4.7
).

•

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t))\leq 0
and
\xi^{m}_{k}(t)\leq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}_{m}(t))
, which by (
9.44
) yields
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t))\leq 0
and
\xi_{k}(t)\leq\underline{s}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t))
. Since
\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t)
is defined as in (
9.41
), with
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t))\leq 0
by (
9.40
), we have
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t))\geq 0
.
We conclude that
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t))=0
, which implies
\underline{s}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t))=0
and hence
\xi_{k}\leq 0
and, by (
9.41
),
\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t)=\mathbf{\bar{u}}(t)
.
We conclude that

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\phi}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t),\xi_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi_{k}(t))&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:bl10}}}{{=}}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{s}(\underbrace{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{k}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t)}_{=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{u}}\circ\mathbf{b}_{k}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t))\ \text{by~\eqref{e:uguale1}}},\xi_{k}(t)),\xi_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi_{k-1}(t))\\
&+\boldsymbol{\psi}_{p}(\mathbf{\underline{u}}(t),\xi_{\ell+1}(t),\dots,\xi_{k-1}(t),\xi_{k}(t)).\\
\end{split}

We now recall the construction of the Slaving Manifold given in §
4.4
. In particular, we recall (
4.48
) and that the solution of the Cauchy problem obtained by coupling (
3.2
) and the initial datum
\boldsymbol{\omega}(0)
in (
4.48
) satisfies (
4.46
), where
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}

is by (
4.45
) the solution of (
3.2
) with initial datum
